Conditioning of Banach Space Valued Gaussian Random Variables: An Approximation Approach Based on Martingales
Ingo Steinwart

Abstract
We investigate the conditional distributions of two Banach space valued, jointly Gaussian random variables. In particular, we show that these conditional distributions are again Gaussian and that their means and covariances can be determined by a general finite dimensional approximation scheme. Here, it turns out that the covariance operators occurring in this scheme converge with respect to the nuclear norm and that the conditional probabilities converge weakly. Furthermore, we discuss how our approximation scheme can be implemented in several classes of important Banach spaces such as (reproducing kernel) Hilbert spaces, spaces of continuous functions, and other spaces consisting of functions. As an example, we then apply our general results to the case of continuous Gaussian processes that are conditioned to partial but infinite observations of their paths. Here we show that conditioning on sufficiently rich, increasing sets of finitely many observations leads to consistent approximations, that is, both the mean and covariance functions converge uniformly and the conditional probabilities converge weakly. Moreover, we discuss how these results improve our understanding of the popular Gaussian processes for machine learning. From a technical perspective our results are based upon a Banach space valued martingale approach for regular conditional probabilities.



Mathematical Subject Classification (2010).
Primary 60G15; Secondary 60B10, 60B11, 62F15, 62G05, 62G20, 28C20, 68T05.

Key Words.
Conditional Gaussian distributions in Banach spaces, Gaussian processes for machine learning, Probabilistic numerics

1
Introduction

Gaussian processes for machine learning (GP4ML), see e.g.
[
47
,
7
,
32
]
, are a standard and widespread non-parametric
Bayesian technique that allows for both prediction and uncertainty quantification in a single approach.
In addition, their Bayesian update rule also plays an important role in other fields such as
information-based complexity, see e.g.
[
57
,
48
,
41
,
42
]
, probabilistic numerics and uncertainty quantification, see e.g.
[
55
,
13
,
28
]
,
Bayesian optimization, see e.g.
[
20
]
,
Bayesian methods for inverse problems, see e.g.
[
54
]
, and spatial statistics, see e.g.
[
21
,
22
]
.
Finally, beginning with
[
40
]
they are also
interesting for neural networks.

In the simplest setup, GP4MLs assume that a Gaussian process
X:=(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is given, where
T
is some index set.
Moreover, it is assumed that both the mean function
m:T\to\mathbb{R}
and the covariance function
k:T\times T\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle m(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}X_{t}\,,

(1)

\displaystyle k(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle:=\cov(X_{t_{1}},X_{t_{2}})

(2)

are available. For some fixed, finite set
S:=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}\subset T
it is further assumed that
we can observe the Gaussian vector
Y:=(X_{s})_{s\in S}
, that is, we can observe the Gaussian process
X
at the points
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
.
Given an observation
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
Y
, the “conditional Gaussian process” of
X
given the observation
Y=y
is
then also a Gaussian process, whose mean and covariance can be computed by

\displaystyle m_{X|Y=y}(t)

\displaystyle=m(t)+K_{t,S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,,

(3)

\displaystyle k_{X|Y}(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=k(t_{1},t_{2})-K_{t_{1},S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}K_{S,t_{2}}

(4)

for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in T
, where

K_{t_{1},S}
,
K_{S,S}
, and
K_{S,t_{2}}
denote covariance matrices with respect to the indicated indices and

K_{S,S}^{\dagger}
is the Moore-Penrose inverse of the
n\times n
-matrix
K_{S,S}
.
Moreover,
recall that the GP4ML literature often assumes noisy observations of the form

Y+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n})
has i.i.d. entries with
\varepsilon_{i}\sim{\cal N}(0,\sigma^{2})
and

\varepsilon
is independent of
X
. In this case,
K_{S,S}^{\dagger}
needs to be replaced by
(K_{S,S}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}
, where
I_{n}
denotes the
n
-dimensional
identity matrix, see again e.g.
[
47
, Chapter 2.2]
and
[
7
, Chapter 6.4]
, as well as the discussion following (
32
).

In the GP4ML literature, the Gaussian process
X
is usually
viewed as a mechanism to generate random functions, e.g. random continuous functions on a compact metric space
T
.
In this case, the “conditional Gaussian process” of
X
given the observations
Y=y
is also viewed as a mechanism generating random continuous functions,
despite the fact that the derivations of (
3
) and (
4
),
see e.g.
[
47
, Chapter 2.2]
and
[
7
, Chapter 6.4]
,
only consider the conditional distributions of
X_{t_{1}},\dots,X_{t_{m}}
for arbitrary, but
a-priori fixed

t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\in T
,
instead of the conditional distribution of entire paths. For this reason, we used quotation remarks when referring to the conditional Gaussian process.
One way to address this issue, is to view
X
as a
C(T)
-valued
Gaussian random variable, where
C(T)
denotes the Banach space of continuous
\mathbb{R}
-valued functions on
T
,
see Sections
2
and
5
for these and the following notions.
In this case, the above
Y
is an
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued Gaussian random variable and
X
and
Y
are jointly Gaussian.
Given their joint distribution
\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
on
C(T)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one then seeks a version
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of the regular
conditional probability of
X
given
Y
, since
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is the conditional distribution of
X
given the observation
Y=y
.
Note that by definition, each
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is a distribution on
C(T)
and therefore we can also
ensure that the “conditional Gaussian process” again produces continuous functions, provided that
(
3
) and (
4
) match the mean and covariance functions of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
.

The references mentioned at the beginning indicate that for the conditioning problem,

C(T)
is by no means the only Banach space one is interested in.
Therefore, let us now consider the general case. To this end, let

E
and
F
be Banach spaces, which due to technical simplicity are assumed to be separable.
Moreover, let
X
be an
E
-valued random variable and
Y
be an
F
-valued random variable such that
X
and
Y
are jointly Gaussian.
In the abstract setting one then seeks a version
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of the regular
conditional probability of
X
given
Y
, since
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is the distribution on
E
that equals
the conditional distribution of
X
given the observation
Y=y
.
Compared to the
C(T)
-case described earlier, this generalization
opens several additional possibilities. First, of course, we can consider Banach spaces of functions
E
other than
C(T)
, e.g. spaces
of differentiable functions. Second, we no longer need to assume that we can observe the random functions on some finite number of observational points.
Instead, we could assume, e.g., that we observe derivatives of the random functions at some observational points
[
44
]
.
More generally,
Y
can, for example, be composed by any other finite set of bounded linear functionals as considered in e.g.
[
58
]

and in the context of (finite element) PDE solvers in e.g.
[
12
,
11
,
44
,
45
]
. Moreover, even
infinite sets of
functionals can be meaningful: To be concrete, in the
C(T)
-example above, the abstract setting makes it possible to
consider arbitrary subsets
S\subset T
for the observational points. This generalization is is important for e.g. PDE-related applications,
where
S
may be used to describe (partial) boundary conditions
see e.g. the recent
[
28
,
44
]
and the references mentioned therein.
Abstracting from these examples leads to the “observational model”

\displaystyle Y=\mathsf{S}\circ X\,,

(5)

where
\mathsf{S}:E\to F
is a
known bounded linear operator.
Here we note that in the
C(T)
-example,
\mathsf{S}
is the restriction operator
C(T)\to C(S)
given by
f\mapsto f_{|S}
. However,
(
5
) goes far beyond this case, and in fact (
5
) is a somewhat classical instance of the general conditional problem as we will
see below when reviewing the existing literature.

Although working with infinite dimensional
Y
has clear conceptual advantages, there is also a drawback:
Indeed, it is often infeasible to fully observe or process infinite dimensional
Y
, or to
compute
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
for such
Y
.
In these cases, a natural ansatz is to use a sequence
(Y_{n})
of
observable, finite dimensional Gaussian random variables, for which the condition step is feasible. For example, in the
C(T)
-case with
infinite
S\subset T
, one could choose an increasing sequence
(S_{n})
of finite subsets
S_{n}\subset S
, whose union is dense in
S
, and
set

\displaystyle Y_{n}:=(X_{s})_{s\in S_{n}}\,.

(6)

The hope of this approach is,
of course, that the resulting
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}
’s provide a convergent approximation of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
, e.g. in terms
of their means and covariances, or as distributions. Now,
in the case
S=T
of full observations, there are indeed results that guarantee convergence of the means and variances as we will discuss below,
and since in this case the conditional distributions
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
are point masses at the observations, these results can be interpreted as contractions of the
Bayesian method given by (
1
) and (
2
).
Because of the applications mentioned earlier, we are, however, mostly interested in case
S\neq T
of partial observations, in which

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
does not simply concentrate on single observations, and where it is usually impossible to
compute
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
in closed form.
This case covers, for example,
the PDE applications mentioned above, where (
6
) provides a simple approximation approach, see e.g.
[
12
,
46
,
44
]
for some concrete examples.

Let us now discuss what is already known for
the fully infinite dimensional conditioning and its possible approximations by finite dimensional observations:
To begin with, in the case of finite dimensional observations, that is

F=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
[
8
, Corollary 3.10.3]
shows that there is a version

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
, such that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is Gaussian for all
y
. However, no
formulas for the mean and covariance of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
are provided.
Moreover, the observational model (
5
) for centered
X
and with arbitrary
E
and
F
has been investigated in

[
56
]
, where the authors show that the regular conditional probabilities are Gaussian measures, see their Theorem 3.11.
Furthermore, in the case
F=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, their Corollary 3.13 presents explicit formulas for the conditional mean and covariance
with the help of a so-called representing sequence of the covariance operator
\cov(Y)
.
In addition, a closer look at their proofs reveals, that analogous formulas hold true for general, separable
F
.
The latter formulas have been made explicit and generalized to non-centered
X
in
[
58
]
. Moreover, both papers
establish the linearity of the conditional mean mapping, and
[
58
]
also describes formulas for sequential updates, and
in the case of
E=C(T)
and
F=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the update rules (
3
) and (
4
)
are generalized to arbitrary, bounded linear observational operators
\mathsf{S}:C(T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Unfortunately, however, the mean and covariance formulas for infinite dimensional
F
become less handy, as they require
to have a representing sequence for
\cov(Y)
. Although such
sequences as well as abstract constructions for them exist, see e.g.
[
56
, Lemma 3.5]
or
[
53
, Lemma 8.2.3]
,
they are rarely at hand in situations in which
F
is not a Hilbert space.
As a consequence, it seems fair to say that the derived formulas have some limited practical value.

The case of
C(T)
-valued
X
is also considered in
[
36
]
. There,
T=[0,t^{*}]
is
an interval and
Y
is given by a partial observation of
X
up to some earlier time
s\in[0,t^{*}]
,
that is
Y:=X_{|[0,s]}
. Under an additional assumption,
the author then proves that the conditional distribution
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)

is a Gaussian measure on
C(T)
and that the map
y\mapsto\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is continuous with respect to the weak convergence of probability measures.
Furthermore, it is shown that his additional assumption is also necessary for the existence of such continuous conditional probabilities.
In fact it is worth to mention that both results are actually first shown for the general observational model (
5
) and then applied to the

C(T)
-case, but the motivation in
[
36
]
clearly focuses on the
C(T)
-case.

A different dependence of the conditional distributions is considered in
[
2
]
. There, the general case of joint Gaussian random variables

X
and
Y
is considered and, in addition, it is assumed that their joint distribution
\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y),\alpha}

depends measurably on a parameter
\alpha
. The main result of

[
2
]
then shows that there exist (regular) conditional probabilities
\mathrm{P}_{(X|Y),\alpha}

such that
(y,\alpha)\mapsto\mathrm{P}_{(X|Y),\alpha}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is measurable if the set of probability measures is equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
generated by the topology of weak convergence of probability measures.

If both
E
and
F
are Hilbert spaces, the conditioning problem has also been investigated by different techniques.
For example, in
[
43
]
formulas for the mean and covariance of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
are
established in the full infinite dimensional setting in terms of both
a)
so-called shorted operators,
and
b)
suitable sequences of oblique, i.e. non-orthogonal, projections.
Moreover,
[
33
]
shows, besides many other results, that some of the formulas derived in

[
43
]
can be significantly simplified. Independently,
[
38
,
25
]
established simple formulas for the the mean and covariance,
which essentially resemble (
3
) and (
4
) in an abstract sense, under the additional assumption that
\cov(Y)
is injective.

The approximation (
6
) in the
C(T)
-case with
S=T
has also been considered in the literature.
For example, convergence rates for the conditional mean functions, that is, for
m_{X|Y_{n}=y_{n}}\to m_{X|Y=y}
,
have been derived in
[
60
]
for specific Gaussian processes
and the results of
[
19
]
can also be used to obtain such rates for random observational points and additional, vanishing observational noise.
We refer to
[
32
, Section 5.1]
for a detailed discussion, where we note that
[
19
]
provides the better results compared to
the source
[
52
]
mentioned in
[
32
]
, since
a)
a clipping step for the mean is avoided, and
b)
also stronger norms
for the convergence are
considered.
In addition, convergence rates for Sobolev type covariance functions can be derived using the fill-distance and results from kernel interpolation

[
62
]
. For a detailed discussion, we refer to
[
32
, Section 5.2]
and the references mentioned therein.
Finally,
[
35
]
recently established a uniform convergence result for the conditional mean and covariance functions under rather general
assumptions on the underlying space
T
and the prior kernel
k
. In addition, this paper provides a small list of earlier, less general convergence results
in the
C(T)
-case with
S=T
.

In this work, we address the general infinite dimensional conditioning problem and a suitable approximation scheme simultaneously.
Namely, we show that given a sufficiently rich sequence
(y_{n}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
in the dual of
F
, see Definition
3.2
and the examples in
Section
4
, we have the weak convergence

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|y_{n})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)

for almost all observations
y
of
Y
, where
Y_{n}
is the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued Gaussian random variable

Y_{n}:=(\langle y_{1}^{\prime},Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F},\dots,\langle y_{n}^{\prime},Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}
and

y_{n}:=(\langle y_{1}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F},\dots,\langle y_{n}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}
is an observation of
Y_{n}
.
In addition, we show that the mean vectors and covariance operators also converge, see Theorem
3.3
for these three statements.
Moreover, we prove that the means and covariances of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|y_{n})
can be computed analogously to the GP4ML case
(
3
) and (
4
), see
Theorem
3.3
. Furthermore,
we show that the set of
y\in F
for which the means converge is a measurable, affine linear subspace of
F
with full measure. This in turn
can be used to construct a “benign” version of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
, see Theorem
3.5
.
Finally, we apply our theory to the
C(T)
-valued case, where we establish uniformly convergent
limit versions of the update rules
(
3
) and (
4
) for general
S\subset T
. These results
generalize the findings of

[
35
]
in the sense that neither
S=T
is required nor additional assumptions on the observational model are needed.

As the title of this paper indicates, the major work horse in our proofs is a Banach space-valued martingale approach,
which is coupled with the relationship between regular conditional probabilities and conditional expectations
in the Banach space case, see Theorem
6.3
. In this respect we note that martingale arguments have already been
used by e.g.
[
38
,
43
]
in the Hilbert space setting, but as far as we know, not in the general Banach space setting.
In addition, the resulting martingale approximations in
[
38
,
43
]
are restricted to rather specific filtrations, whereas our
results only require a natural richness assumption on the filtration.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows: In Section
2
we recall all notions and notations from probability theory and
functional analysis that are necessary to formulate our main results. These main results are then presented in Section
3
.
Moreover, in Section
4
we investigate the “sufficiently rich” sequences
(y_{n}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
mentioned above
in more detail. In particular, we establish a general existence result and discuss simple constructions for some concrete types of spaces including
(reproducing kernel) Hilbert spaces and spaces of continuous or smooth functions.
The

C(T)
-valued case is subsequently investigated in more detail in Section
5
.
Sections
6

to
8
contain the proofs of the results of Sections
3
to
5
.
Finally, we have attached five detailed supplements on

A)
measurability in Banach spaces and Banach space valued integration,

B)
covariance operators,

C)
weak convergence, characteristic functions, and Gaussian random variables,

D)
regular conditional probabilities and conditional expectations in the Banach space case,
and

E)
continuous Gaussian processes.
Most material in these supplements has been simply collected and unified from the existing literature, but a couple of
mostly simple results are also added since we could not find them in the literature.

2
Preliminaries

Throughout this paper,
E
and
F
denote Banach spaces. Moreover, we write
B_{E}
for the closed unit ball
of
E
, and
E^{\prime}
for the dual of
E
.
Furthermore,
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:E^{\prime}\times E\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the standard dual pairing defined by

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:=x^{\prime}(x)\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime},x\in E.

Moreover, recall that the
w^{*}
-topology on
E^{\prime}
is the smallest topology for which all
\langle\,\cdot\,,x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}

are continuous.

On
\mathbb{R}^{d}
we always consider the usual Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}^{d}
and in the case
d=1
we simply write

\mathfrak{B}
. Furthermore recall that on general Banach spaces
E
, there are
at least two natural

\sigma
-algebras: The first one is the usual Borel
\sigma
-algebra

\mathfrak{B}(E)
that is generated by the norm topology on
E
and the second one is

\sigma(E^{\prime})
, that is, the smallest
\sigma
-algebra on
E
, for which all bounded, linear
x^{\prime}:E\to\mathbb{R}
are measurable.
In general, we have
\sigma(E^{\prime})\subset\mathfrak{B}(E)
, but for separable
E
it is well-known that
\sigma(E^{\prime})=\mathfrak{B}(E)
, see
Theorem
A.2
.

If
(\Omega,{\cal A})
is a measurable space and
E
is a Banach space, then
an
X:\Omega\to E
is weakly measurable if it is
({\cal A},\sigma(E^{\prime}))
-measurable, or equivalently, if
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

is measurable for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
X
is strongly measurable if it is
({\cal A},\mathfrak{B}(E))
-measurable and
X(\Omega)
is separable.
If
E
is separable, then
weak and strong measurability are equivalent, see Theorem
A.2
, and for this reason
we sometimes simply speak of measurable
X
.
Finally, if
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
is a probability
space, then a strongly measurable
X:\Omega\to E
is called a random variable,
and in this case its image measure
\mathrm{P}_{X}
on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
is called the distribution of
X
.

For a fixed probability space
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
, a Banach space
E
, and
p\in[1,\infty)
, we write

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}:=\biggl\{X:\Omega\to E\,\Bigl|\,X\mbox{ is strongly measurable and }\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)<\infty\biggr\}

for the space of
p
-times Bochner integrable functions.
Each
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
is called Bochner integrable and for these
X
one can define the expectation by the
E
-valued Bochner integral

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}X:=\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,

see also
Supplement
A
, which collects some information on Bochner integrals.
Moreover, for
X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
, where
F
is another Banach space,
the cross covariance of
X
and
Y
is the bounded linear
operator
\cov(X,Y):F^{\prime}\to E
defined by the Bochner integrals

\displaystyle\cov(X,Y)(y^{\prime})=\int_{\Omega}\langle y^{\prime},Y-\mathbb{E}Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,(X-\mathbb{E}X)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,\qquad\qquad y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}.

Note that
\cov(X,Y)
is even nuclear and thus also compact. We refer to
e.g.
[
59
, Theorem 2.5 in Chapter III.2]
, or Lemma
B.7
for an alternative
argument, and to (
B.17
) and (
B.18
) for the definition of nuclear operators and their norms.
In particular, the covariance of
X
, which is defined by

\displaystyle\cov(X):=\cov(X,X)\,,

is a nuclear operator
\cov(X):E^{\prime}\to E
. For more information on (cross) covariances we refer to
Supplement
B
.
Finally, in the case
E=\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
F=\mathbb{R}^{n}
the cross covariance operator
\cov(X,Y):(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

can, of course, be described by the
m\times n
-covariance matrix

\displaystyle K_{X,Y}:=\bigl(\cov(X_{i},Y_{j})\bigr)_{i,j}\,,

(7)

where
X_{i}
and
Y_{j}
denote the
i
th, respectively
j
th, component of
X
and
Y
.

Recall that a probability measure
\mathrm{P}
on
\mathbb{R}
is a Gaussian measure, if
there are
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma\in[0,\infty)
such that
the characteristic function of
\mathrm{P}

is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\mu}\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{\sigma^{2}t^{2}}{2}\biggr)\,,\qquad\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

In the case
\sigma>0
, we thus have
\mathrm{P}={\cal N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
, i.e.
\mathrm{P}
is a normal distribution with expectation
\mu

and variance
\sigma^{2}
.
If
\sigma=0
, the probability measure
\mathrm{P}
has all its mass on
\mu
and we write
{\cal N}(\mu,0):=\mathrm{P}
.
Finally, if
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
is a probability space and
X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable, then
X
is said to be an
\mathbb{R}
-valued
Gaussian random variable
if its distribution
\mathrm{P}_{X}

is a Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
With these preparations we can now define both
E
-valued Gaussian random variables and Gaussian measures
on
E
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space and
E
be a separable Banach space. Then
X:\Omega\to E
is
a Gaussian random variable, if

\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is a Gaussian random variable
for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.

Moreover, a probability measure
\mathrm{Q}
on
\sigma(E^{\prime})=\mathfrak{B}(E)
is a Gaussian measure, if
the image measure
\mathrm{Q}_{x^{\prime}}
on
\mathbb{R}
is a Gaussian measure for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.

Finally, if we have another separable Banach spaces
F
and a
Y:\Omega\to F
, then we say that

X
and
Y
are jointly Gaussian
random variables, if
(X,Y):\Omega\to E\times F
is a Gaussian random variable, that is,

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+\langle y^{\prime},Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

(8)

is a Gaussian random variable
for all

x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
.

Since
E
is separable, Gaussian random variables are automatically strongly measurable. Moreover,
it directly follows from the definitions that

\mathrm{Q}
is a Gaussian measure on
\sigma(E^{\prime})
, if and only if the identity map

\id_{E}:E\to E
defined on the probability space
(E,\sigma(E^{\prime}),\mathrm{Q})
is a Gaussian random variable.
Furthermore, recall that
Fernique’s theorem, see e.g.
[
37
, Corollary 3.2]
, shows that Gaussian random variables

X:\Omega\to E
on some probability space
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)<\infty

(9)

for all
p\in[1,\infty)
. Consequently, for every Gaussian random variable
X
its
expectation
\mathbb{E}X
and covariance
\cov(X)
are defined. Moreover, if
\mathrm{Q}
is a Gaussian measure on
\sigma(E^{\prime})
, then
we define its expectation and covariance by those of the Gaussian random variable
\id_{E}
mentioned above, that is

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathrm{Q}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}}\id_{E}=\int_{E}x\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}(x)\,,

\displaystyle\cov(\mathrm{Q})(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\cov_{\mathrm{Q}}(\id_{E})(x^{\prime})=\int_{E}\langle x^{\prime},x-\mu_{\mathrm{Q}}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot(x-\mu_{\mathrm{Q}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}(x)\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}.

Like in the finite dimensional case, the expectation and covariance
uniquely determine a Gaussian measure. Namely, if
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}
are
Gaussian measures on
\sigma(E^{\prime})
with
\mu_{\mathrm{P}}=\mu_{\mathrm{Q}}
and
\cov(\mathrm{P})=\cov(\mathrm{Q})
, then we have
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{Q}
, see
Proposition

C.7
and the discussion at the beginning of Supplement
C
.
For Gaussian measures
\mathrm{Q}
we thus write

\displaystyle{\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{\mathrm{Q}},\cov(\mathrm{Q})\bigr):=\mathrm{Q}\,.

(10)

Finally, for a Gaussian random variable

X:\Omega\to E
on a probability space
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})

we call

\displaystyle{\cal G}(X):=\overline{\bigl\{\langle x^{\prime},X-\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}\bigr\}}^{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}

the Gaussian Hilbert space associated to
X
. We refer to Lemma
C.6
for more information.

Given jointly Gaussian
random variables
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to F
, the major goal of this work is to investigate the
conditional distribution of
X
given an observation
Y=y
.
To make this mathematically rigorous,
we need to recall the following definition.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(T,{\cal B})
and
(U,{\cal A})
be measurable spaces and
\mathrm{P}
be a probability measure on
(T\times U,{\cal B}\otimes{\cal A})
.
Then we say that a map

\mathrm{P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):{\cal B}\times U\to[0,1]
is a regular conditional probability
of
\mathrm{P}
given
U
,
if the following three
conditions are satisfied:

i)

For all
u\in U
the map
\mathrm{P}(\,\cdot\,|u):{\cal B}\to[0,1]
is a probability measure.

ii)

For all
B\in{\cal B}
the map
\mathrm{P}(B|\,\cdot\,):U\to[0,1]
is
{\cal A}
-measurable.

iii)

For all
B\in{\cal B}
and
A\in{\cal A}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}(B\times A)=\int_{U}\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(u)\mathrm{P}(B|u)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)\,.

(11)

Here,
\mathrm{P}_{U}
denotes the marginal distribution of
\mathrm{P}
on
U
, that is
\mathrm{P}_{U}:=\mathrm{P}_{\pi_{U}}
, where
\mathrm{P}_{\pi_{U}}

is the image measure of
\mathrm{P}
under the
coordinate projection
\pi_{U}:T\times U\to U
.

If
E
and
F
are separable Banach spaces and
\mathrm{Q}
is a probability measure on

\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes\mathfrak{B}(F)
, then there always exists a regular conditional probability

\mathrm{Q}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes F\to[0,1]
of
\mathrm{Q}
given
F
. In addition, this regular
conditional probability is always
\mathrm{Q}_{F}
-almost surely unique. These statements directly follow from a more general and well-known
result on the existence and uniqueness of regular conditional probabilities, which is recalled in Theorem
D.1
.

Let us now apply these notion and results to jointly Gaussian
random variables
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to F
defined on some probability space

(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
.
To this end, let

\mathrm{Q}:=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
be their joint distribution on
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes\mathfrak{B}(F)
.
A quick calculation yields
\mathrm{Q}_{F}=\mathrm{P}_{Y}
, and by our discussion above there exists
a
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-almost surely unique regular conditional probability

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times F\to[0,1]

of
\mathrm{Q}
given
F
.
We write
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):=\mathrm{Q}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
and speak of a version of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y
.
Now recall that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B|y)
describes the probability of
\{X\in B\}
under the condition that we have observed
y=Y(\omega)
.
Consequently, our goal is to
describe

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
modulo
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero sets.
This task will be accomplished by

i)

showing that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is a Gaussian measure, and

ii)

determining the expectation and covariance of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
.

As discussed around (
10
) these two steps completely describe
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
.

In the finite dimensional case, the solution of this task is folklore,
see e.g.
[
47
, Appendix A.2]
,
[
7
, Chapter 2.3]
,
[
27
, Lemma 4.3]
,
[
54
, Theorem 6.20]
, and also
[
44
, Section 4]
.
In the following formulation of this solution, we write
A^{\dagger}
for the Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse of an

m\times n
-matrix
A
, and later we use the same notation for linear maps
A:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space and
X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be
jointly Gaussian random variables. Then for every version

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y

there exists an
N\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y}(y),K_{X|Y}\bigr)\,,

where

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y}(y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}X+K_{X,Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,,

\displaystyle K_{X|Y}

\displaystyle:=K_{X,X}-K_{X,Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}K_{Y,X}\,.

Finally, recall that conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
and
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)

of Banach space valued random variables
X
are defined
analogously to the
\mathbb{R}
-valued case. In addition, many properties of
\mathbb{R}
-valued conditional expectations
still hold in the Banach space case. For details we refer to Definition
D.3
and the subsequent results in Supplement
D
.

3
Main Results

In this section we present the main results of this work that investigate the regular conditional probabilities arising from
conditioning one Banach space valued Gaussian random variable with respect to another one.

We begin by presenting a theorem
that generalizes and extends the finite dimensional Theorem
2.3
to the case in which the random variable

X
is Banach space valued.
Of course, for such
X
we can no longer work with matrices, but apart from this difference, the first part of the following
theorem is an almost literal copy of Theorem
2.3
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
be a separable Banach space, and
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be
be
jointly Gaussian random variables.
Moreover, let

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y}(y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(12)

\displaystyle C_{X|Y}

\displaystyle:=\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X)\,.

(13)

Then for every version

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y

there exists an
N\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y}(y),C_{X|Y}\bigr)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
.
In addition, the random variable
Z:\Omega\to E
defined by

\displaystyle Z:=\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y=\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)

is both a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
and a
Gaussian random variable with
\mathbb{E}Z=\mathbb{E}X
and

\displaystyle\cov(Z)

\displaystyle=\cov(X)-C_{X|Y}\,,

\displaystyle{\cal G}(Z)

\displaystyle\subset{\cal G}(Y)\,.

As mentioned in the introduction,
[
8
, Corollary 3.10.3]
has shown that there is a version

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
, such that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is Gaussian for
all

y
.
Moreover, for more specific cases,
formulas for the mean and covariance have also been derived by other authors as laid out in the introduction.
However, we could not find such formulas in the full generality presented in Theorem
3.1
.
Since these formulas play a crucial role for our approximation scheme in the case of infinite dimensional
Y
, we thus decided
to present and prove Theorem
3.1
as a result.

Our next and main goal of this section is to generalize Theorem
3.1
to infinite dimensional
Y
. Here we recall that we cannot expect a literal
generalization. To illustrate this, let us assume for simplicity that
H:=F
is an infinite dimensional Hilbert space. Using the
canonical Riesz-Fréchet-identification of
H^{\prime}
with
H
, we can then view
\cov(Y)
as a compact linear operator
\cov(Y):H\to H
.
Now recall that a bounded linear
A:H\to H
has a Moore-Penrose inverse if and only if its image
\ran A
is closed, see e.g.
[
26
, Theorem 2.4]
.
Moreover, it is well-known that compact operators have a closed range, if and only if their range is actually finite dimensional, see e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 3.4.6]
.
Consequently,
the Moore-Penrose inverse
(\cov Y)^{\dagger}
exists if and only if
\ran\cov(Y)
is finite dimensional, and
the latter is only possible, if the range of
Y
is almost surely contained in a finite-dimensional subspace of
F
,
see e.g.
[
36
, Theorem 2.1]
or
[
55
, Theorem 2.43]
. In other words, even in the Hilbert space case there is no hope for a literal generalization of (
12
) and (
13
)
to truly infinite dimensional
Y
.

In the following, we will resolve this issue by considering a sequence
(Y_{n})
of suitably chosen finite dimensional “projections”
of
Y
. For each
Y_{n}
we can then apply Theorem
3.1
, and it remains to show that the resulting
sequence of regular conditional probabilities
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}
converges to the regular conditional probability
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
.
To begin with, the following definition introduces the type of “projections” we need for this approach.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
F
be a separable Banach space and
(A_{n})
be a sequence of bounded linear operators
A_{n}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then we say that

(A_{n})
is a filtering sequence for
F
, if we have
\sigma(A_{n})\subset\sigma(A_{n+1})
for all
n\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\sigma(F^{\prime})=\sigma\bigl(A_{n}:n\geq 1\bigr)\,.

The measure-theoretic description of filtering sequences is useful for establishing our general
result on conditioning jointly Gaussian random variables, yet it is somewhat cumbersome to verify.
For this reason we present some simple approaches for constructing filtering sequences in Section
4
.
In addition, we will see in Theorem
4.2

that for any separable Banach space
F
there exists a filtering sequence. Furthermore, we show in Section
4
that
for important classes of spaces filtering sequences can be easily constructed and applied. As an illustrative example, we will
work out the details for the spaces
C(T)
in Section
5
.

With the help of filtering sequences we can now present our main result on conditioning one Gaussian random variable with respect
to another one.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
and
F
be separable Banach spaces,
and
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to F
be jointly Gaussian random variables.
Moreover, let
(A_{n})
be a filtering sequence for
F
and

\displaystyle Y_{n}:=A_{n}\circ Y\,,\qquad\qquad n\geq 1.

Then for all versions
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
and

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times F\to[0,1]
of the regular conditional probabilities of
X
given
Y_{n}
, respectively
Y
,
there exists an
N\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N

the following statements hold true:

i)

For all
n\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(A_{n}(y)),C_{X|Y_{n}}\bigr)\,,

where

\mu_{X|Y_{n}}
and
C_{X|Y_{n}}
are defined as in
(
12
), respectively
(
13
).

ii)

The measure
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is a Gaussian measure on
E
.

iii)

The expectation
\mu_{X|Y}(y)
of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is given by

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y}(y)=\mathbb{E}X+\lim_{n\to\infty}\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(A_{n}(y)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})\,,

where the convergence is with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{E}
.

iv)

The covariance operator
C_{X|Y}:E^{\prime}\to E
of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is given by

\displaystyle C_{X|Y}=\cov(X)-\lim_{n\to\infty}\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)\,,

where the convergence is with respect to the nuclear norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}
and thus also with respect to the operator norm.

v)

The weak convergence
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))\to\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
holds true.

vi)

If we define the random variable
Z:\Omega\to E
by
Z(\omega):=0
for all
\omega\in Y^{-1}(N)
and

\displaystyle Z(\omega):=\mathbb{E}X+\lim_{n\to\infty}\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\bigl(Y_{n}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n}\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega\setminus Y^{-1}(N),

then
Z
is both a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
and
a Gaussian random variable with
\mathbb{E}Z=\mathbb{E}X
and

\displaystyle\cov(Z)

\displaystyle=\cov(X)-C_{X|Y}\,.

Finally, the limit in the definition of
Z
also holds in the
{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
-sense.

vii)

We have
{\cal G}(Z)\subset{\cal G}(Y)
and
the orthogonal projection
\Pi_{Y}:{\cal G}(X,Y)\to{\cal G}(X,Y)
onto the subspace
{\cal G}(Y)
is given by

\displaystyle\Pi_{Y}f=\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\,,\qquad\qquad f\in{\cal G}(X,Y).

viii)

For all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, for which
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable, and all
\omega\not\in Y^{-1}(N)
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}(Y(\omega))\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime},X(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle C_{X|Y}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0\,.

In a nutshell,
i)
simply applies Theorem
3.1

to the random variables
X
and
Y_{n}
. The statements
ii)
to
v)
provide the Gaussianity of the
conditional probabilities as well as the approximations announced in the introduction.
In addition,
vi)
shows

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y=\mathbb{E}(X|Y),

which below we will investigate in more detail.
Furthermore,
vii)
describes the behaviour of the orthogonal projection
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|Y):{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}\to{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
when it is
restricted to

{\cal G}(X,Y)
.
Here we note that in the observational model (
5
), we have
{\cal G}(X,Y)={\cal G}(X)
and
{\cal G}(Y)\subset{\cal G}(X)
,
as one can quickly derive from Lemma
C.6
,
and therefore
the conditional expectation considered in
vi)

becomes the orthogonal projection
\Pi_{Y}:{\cal G}(X)\to{\cal G}(X)
onto the subspace
{\cal G}(Y)
.
Finally,
viii)
generalizes the well-known concentration of Gaussian
posterior measures on the observations from the finite to the infinite dimensional case.

Our next goal is to shed some more light on the structure of the conditional distributions.
To this end,
let us assume that we are in the situation of Theorem
3.3
.
Then by (
12
)
each
\mu_{X|Y_{n}}\circ A_{n}:F\to E
is continuous and thus strongly measurable, where we use the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}(F)=\sigma(F^{\prime})
on
F
.
Let us define the map
\mu_{X|Y}:F\to E
by

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y}(y):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{F\setminus N}(y)\cdot\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(A_{n}(y))\,,\qquad\qquad y\in Y\,,

(14)

which is possible by and consistent with part
iii)
. Then
\mu_{X|Y}
is the pointwise limit of a sequence of strongly measurable functions, and therefore also (strongly)
measurable.
Moreover, the definition of
Z
in
part
vi)
gives

\displaystyle Z=\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y\,,

and since
Z
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
, we see that
\mu_{X|Y}
is a version of the “factorized” conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(X|Y=\,\cdot\,):F\to E
.

Now note that
\mu_{X|Y}
carries all information required to reconstruct the Gaussian measures
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
for
y\in F\setminus N
: Namely we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y}(y),\cov(X)-\cov(\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y)\bigr)

for all
y\in F\setminus N
,
where we note that
\mathbb{E}\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)=\mu_{X|Y}(y)
directly follows from
iii)
, while for the covariance we used

vi)
and

\displaystyle\cov\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)=C_{X|Y}=\cov(X)-\cov(Z)=\cov(X)-\cov(\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y)\,.

This observation raises the question, if
we also obtain a version of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
by setting

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y):={\cal N}\bigl(\mu(y),\cov(X)-\cov(\mu\circ Y)\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in Y

(15)

for an
arbitrary
version
\mu
of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y=\,\cdot\,):F\to E
. Since each such
\mu
coincides
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-almost surely with the above
\mu_{X|Y}

it is not overly surprising that the construction (
15
) is indeed possible. Yet it turns out in the proof of the following result that some work is
required to ensure the measurability of
y\mapsto\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B|y)
for all
B\in\mathfrak{B}(E)
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
and
F
be separable Banach spaces,
and
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to F
be jointly Gaussian random variables.
Moreover, let
\mu:F\to E
be a measurable such that

\displaystyle Z:=\mu\circ Y

is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
. Then
(
15
) defines a version of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y
.

We have seen around (
14
) that a filtering sequence in combination with
Theorem
3.3
can be used to construct a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y=\,\cdot\,):F\to E
. The next result shows that
we can refine this construction to have a better control over the nuisance
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero set
N
.
Combining this with the construction (
15
) we can then also find a version
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)

that we can control for
all

y\in F
. In particular, this will generalize

[
8
, Corollary 3.10.3]
mentioned in the introduction from finite dimensional to infinite dimensional
Y
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
and
F
be separable Banach spaces,
and
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to F
be jointly Gaussian random variables.
Moreover, let
\mathfrak{A}:=(A_{n})
be a filtering sequence for
F
and

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}:=\bigl\{y\in F:\exists\lim_{n\to\infty}\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(A_{n}(y)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})\bigr\}\,,

where
Y_{n}:=A_{n}\circ Y
for all
n\geq 1
. Then the following statements hold true:

i)

We have
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}})=1
.

ii)

The set
F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}:=-\mathbb{E}Y+F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
is a measurable subspace of
F
with
\ran\cov(Y,X)\subset F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
.

iii)

The map
\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}:F\to E
defined by

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(y):=\mathbb{E}X+\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}}(y)\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(A_{n}(y)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})\,,\qquad y\in Y\,,

is measurable and
\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\circ Y
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
.

iv)

The map
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(\,\cdot\,|y):={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(y),\cov(X)-\cov(\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\circ Y)\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in Y

is a version of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y
.

Note that if

X
and
Y
are centered, then
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}=F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
holds, and therefore
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
is a measurable subspace of full measure
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
.
Moreover, for all
y\in F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(y)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}A_{n}(y)\,.

Consequently, the restriction of
\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
to
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
is linear and measurable.
Note that in the Hilbert space case such linear maps also occurred in

[
38
]
.

Let us finally discuss how the
theorems above do provide some frequentist consistency and contraction results
for
the Bayesian method given by the prior distribution
\mathrm{P}_{X}
and the conditional distributions.
To this end, let us assume that we have an
E
-valued Gaussian random variable
X
on some probability space
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})

and
a filtering sequence
\mathfrak{A}:=(A_{n})
for
E
.
Moreover, assume that we cannot observe
X
but only

X_{n}:=A_{n}\circ X
. This gives us versions
\mathrm{P}_{X|X_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of the regular conditional
probabilities of
X
given
X_{n}
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|X_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(x))={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|X_{n}}(x),C_{X|X_{n}}\bigr)

for all
x\in E
,
where

\displaystyle\mu_{X|X_{n}}(A_{n}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,X_{n})(\cov X_{n})^{\dagger}(A_{n}(x)-\mathbb{E}X_{n})\,,

\displaystyle C_{X|X_{n}}

\displaystyle=\cov(X)-\cov(X,X_{n})(\cov X_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(X_{n},X)\,.

To describe their behavior for
n\to\infty
, we set
Y:=X
. Then our results above
give an
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}(N)=0
such that for all
\omega\in\Omega\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{X|X_{n}}(X_{n}(\omega))\to\mu_{X|X}((X(\omega))=X(\omega)

(16)

with convergence in
E
, where the last identity follows from
viii)
of Theorem
3.3

in combination with
Hahn-Banach’s theorem since

\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
Similarly,
part
viii)
of Theorem
3.3
also shows

C_{X|X}x^{\prime}=0
for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, that is
C_{X|X}=0
, and hence we find

\displaystyle C_{X|X_{n}}\to C_{X|X}=0

(17)

with respect to the nuclear norm. Part
v)
of Theorem
3.3

thus yields the weak convergence

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|X_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|X_{n}(\omega))\to{\cal N}\bigl(X(\omega),0\bigr)

(18)

for all
\omega\in\Omega\setminus N
. In other words, with increasing
n
, the distributions

\mathrm{P}_{X|X_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|X_{n}(\omega))
concentrate more and more around the observation
X(\omega)
.
Note that these convergence results can also be interpreted from a frequentist point of view for
the Bayes method given by the posterior distributions
\mathrm{P}_{X|X_{n}}
and the prior distribution
\mathrm{P}_{X}
, if it
is assumed that the “true” parameter is some
X(\omega_{0})
: Namely, the convergence in
(
18
)
provides a consistency result in the sense of

[
23
, Chapter 6.2]
, while (
16
) and (
17
) describe
a contraction result for the parameters in the sense of

[
24
, Chapter 7.3.1]
.

4
Filtering Sequences

Theorems
3.3
and
3.5
crucially depend on the existence of
filtering sequences and our ability to construct and apply them. In this section, we therefore investigate filtering sequences in some detail.
In particular, we show that for separable
Banach spaces
F
there always exists a filtering sequence. In addition, we present
some natural and readily available filtering sequences for Hilbert spaces and reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces,
as well as for the spaces
C(T)
.
Finally, we also investigate both
C^{1}([0,1])
and general Banach spaces consisting of functions.

Our first result shows that a sufficiently rich sequence
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
can be used to construct
a filtering sequence. It thus translates
the measure-theoretic notion
of filtering sequences into the language of functional analysis.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
F
be a separable Banach space and
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
with

\displaystyle\overline{\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{w^{*}}=F^{\prime}\,,

(19)

where on the left side the
w^{*}
-closure is considered. For
n\geq 1
we
define
A_{n}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle A_{n}(y):=\bigl(\langle y_{1}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F},\dots,\langle y_{n}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in F.

(20)

Then
(A_{n})
is a filtering sequence for
F
.

Note that (
19
) is, for example, satisfied, if we have a sequence
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
, for which (
19
) even holds for the
norm-closure, that is,

\displaystyle\overline{\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{\|\,\cdot\,\|_{F^{\prime}}}=F^{\prime}\,.

(21)

Unfortunately, a sequence
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
with (
21
) exists if and only if
F^{\prime}
is separable. As a consequence,
spaces such as
F:=C([0,1]^{d})
do not enjoy (
21
), and for this reason we will mostly focus on (
19
).

Now, our first main result of this section shows the existence of filtering sequences for generic, separable
F
, where
we recall from Theorem
A.1
that for such
F
we always have a countable and
w^{*}
-dense
\mathfrak{D}_{F^{\prime}}\subset B_{F^{\prime}}
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
F
be a separable Banach space and

\mathfrak{D}_{F^{\prime}}\subset B_{F^{\prime}}
be countable and
w^{*}
-dense with enumeration
\mathfrak{D}_{F^{\prime}}=\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}
.
For
n\geq 1
we
define
A_{n}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by (
20
).
Then
(A_{n})
is a filtering sequence for
F
.

Combining Theorem
4.2
with Theorem
3.3
we see, for example, that the regular
conditional probabilities
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}
of jointly Gaussian random variables
X
and
Y
are always almost surely Gaussian measures.
Moreover, we can always approximate their means and covariances by the described finite dimensional projections.

Proposition
4.1
shows that sequences
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}

satisfying (
19
)
can be used to construct filtering sequences.
Unfortunately, working with the
w^{*}
-closure in (
19
) is often rather complicated. For this reason the
following result
provides a characterization of sequences satisfying (
19
) that can be easier verified in some situations.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
F
be a separable Banach space and
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
be a sequence. Then the following statements are equivalent:

i)

We have
\overline{\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{w^{*}}=F^{\prime}
.

ii)

The sequence
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset F^{\prime}
is separating, that is, for all
y\in F\setminus\{0\}
there is an
i\geq 1
with
\langle y^{\prime}_{i},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\neq 0
.

Let us now turn to more specific spaces.
The following corollary considers
orthonormal bases (ONBs) in separable Hilbert spaces.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
H
be a separable Hilbert space with
\dim H=\infty
and
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an ONB of
H
.
For
n\geq 1
we
define
A_{n}:H\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle A_{n}(y):=\bigl(\langle e_{1},y\rangle_{H},\dots,\langle e_{n},y\rangle_{H}\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in H.

Then
(A_{n})
is a filtering sequence for
H
.

Of course, it is possible to generalize Corollary
4.4
to Banach spaces
F
whose dual
F^{\prime}
have a Schauder base.
We skip the rather obvious details.

As described in the introduction one of our goals is to
better understand GP4MLs, which are typically viewed to generate random functions.
To capture this in a general form, we say
that a Banach space
F
is
a Banach spaces consisting of functions (BSF) on some set
T
, if
F
consists of functions
T\to\mathbb{R}
, and the point evaluations
\delta_{t}:F\to\mathbb{R}

given by
\delta_{t}f=f(t)
are continuous for all
t\in T
.

Before we show how these point evaluations can be used to construct filtering sequences, we note that
BSFs span a wide class of spaces. For example, the space
\ell_{\infty}(T)
of all bounded functions
T\to\mathbb{R}

equipped with the supremums norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is a BSF. In addition,
the space
C_{b}(T,\metric)
of
bounded, continuous functions
T\to\mathbb{R}
on some (pseudo) metric space
(T,\metric)
is a BSF, if we again
equip
C_{b}(T,\metric)
with the supremums norm. Moreover,
reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs) are covered, as they are by definition
both Hilbert spaces and BSFs, see
e.g.
[
51
, Chapter 4.2]
for details.
Furthermore, spaces of bounded,
\alpha
-Hölder continuous functions as well as spaces of bounded, continuously differentiable functions are BSFs.
In addition, Sobolev spaces can be viewed as BSFs if their smoothness is sufficiently large in the sense that Sobolev’s embedding
theorem is satisfied. BSFs have recently attracted some interest in the machine learning community, and we refer for more information on BSFs to e.g.
[
4
,
50
]

and the references mentioned therein. Finally, note that BSFs also play an important role for the PDE applications mentioned in the introduction, see e.g.
[
44
]
.

Now, if
F
is a
BSF on
T
and
T_{n}:=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\subset T
, then

I_{T_{n}}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by

\displaystyle I_{T_{n}}(f):=\bigl(\langle\delta_{t_{1}},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F},\dots,\langle\delta_{t_{n}},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad f\in F

(22)

is a bounded linear operator.
The following result shows that in certain situations these operators can be used to construct filtering sequences.

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a separable pseudo-metric space and
F
be a separable BSF on
T
for which all
f\in F
are continuous.
Furthermore, let
T_{\infty}\subset T
be a countable and dense subset with enumeration
T_{\infty}=\{t_{i}:i\geq 1\}
.
Then
(\delta_{t_{i}})\subset F^{\prime}
is separating and

(I_{T_{n}})
is a filtering sequence for
F
, where

I_{T_{n}}
is defined by (
22
)
for
T_{n}:=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
.

Recall that compact metric spaces
(T,\metric)
are separable and that the corresponding space
C(T)

is also separable, see e.g.
[
15
, Theorem V.6.6]
or
[
18
, Corollary 11.2.5]
.
Therefore, we obtain the following corollary of Theorem
4.5
without any further proof.

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space and
T_{\infty}\subset T
be a countable, dense subset with enumeration
T_{\infty}=\{t_{i}:i\geq 1\}
.
Then

(I_{T_{n}})
is a filtering sequence for
C(T)
, where we have set
T_{n}:=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
.

Our next goal is to apply Theorem
4.5
to RKHSs.
To this end, recall that every RKHS
H
on
T
has a unique kernel
k:T\times T\to\mathbb{R}
, which by the reproducing property
can be used to represent the point evaluations. More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{t},h\rangle_{H^{\prime},H}=\langle k(t,\,\cdot\,),h\rangle_{H}\,,\qquad\qquad t\in T,\,h\in H,

see e.g.
[
51
, Chapter 4.2]
. With these preparations, the application of Theorem
4.5
to RKHSs reads as follows.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a separable, pseudo-metric space and
H
be an RKHS on
T
with continuous kernel
k:T\times T\to\mathbb{R}
.
Then
H
is separable and for all countable and dense subsets
T_{\infty}=\{t_{i}:i\geq 1\}
of
T
the operators

I_{n}:H\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by

\displaystyle I_{n}(h):=\bigl(\langle k(t_{1},\,\cdot\,),h\rangle_{H},\dots,\langle k(t_{n},\,\cdot\,),h\rangle_{H}\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad h\in H

(23)

give a filtering sequence
(I_{n})
for
H
.

In the following, we show that for all separable BSFs
F
on
T
it is possible to construct
a pseudo metric on
T
such that the assumptions of Theorem
4.5
are satisfied.

We first consider the case of RKHSs
H
on some set
T
since in this case, the construction is straightforward.
To begin with, let
us recall, that

\metric_{k}:T\times T\to[0,\infty)
defined by

\displaystyle\metric_{k}(t_{1},t_{2}):=\|k(t_{1},\,\cdot\,)-k(t_{2},\,\cdot\,)\|_{H}=\sqrt{k(t_{1},t_{1})-2k(t_{1},t_{2})+k(t_{2},t_{2})}

for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in T

gives a pseudo-metric on
T
called the kernel metric, see e.g.
[
51
, Chapter 4.3]
. With this preparation,
the RKHS case reads as follows.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
H
be a separable RKHS on
T
with kernel
k
. Then
(T,\metric_{k})
is a separable pseudo-metric space and
all
h\in H
are continuous with respect to
\metric_{k}
.

Combining Proposition
4.8
with Theorem
4.5
we see that (
23
)
defines a filtering sequence if we choose a countable and
\metric_{k}
-dense subset
T_{\infty}
of
T
.

Our next result will generalize Proposition
4.8
to separable BSFs
F
. Here, a natural idea is to
pull back a metric from
F^{\prime}
to
T

with the help of the “feature map”
\Phi:T\to F^{\prime}

given by
t\mapsto\delta_{t}

as we have done for the kernel metric. Unfortunately, however, we can no longer take the norm of
F^{\prime}
for this approach as
the needed separability of
T
would
require
F^{\prime}
to be separable. Moreover, simply taking a metric describing the
w^{*}
-topology on a (scaled) ball
of
B_{F^{\prime}}
is not possible either, as the above
\Phi
is bounded if and only if
F\subset\ell_{\infty}(T)
, and the latter is
an assumption we would like to avoid.
Nonetheless, the general idea is possible if we consider a modified map and a different metric, as the proof
of the following theorem reveals.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
F
be a separable BSF on
T
. Then there exists a pseudo-metric
\metric
on
T

such that
(T,\metric)
is separable and all
f\in F
are continuous with respect to
\metric
. In addition, there
exists a countable subset
T_{\infty}=\{t_{i}:i\geq 1\}
of
T
such that
(\delta_{t_{i}})\subset F^{\prime}
is separating.

To illustrate the consequences of Theorem
4.9

we fix a separable BSF
F
on
T
and a set

T_{\infty}=\{t_{i}:i\geq 1\}
as in Theorem
4.9
. Then Proposition

4.3
gives

\displaystyle\overline{\spann\{\delta_{t_{i}}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{w^{*}}=F^{\prime}\,,

and Theorem
4.5
ensures that

(I_{T_{n}})
is a filtering sequence for
F
, where we have set
T_{n}:=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
.
In other words, for separable BSFs it is always possible to apply our main Theorems
3.3
and
3.5

with filtering sequences based upon point evaluations.

Finally we present an alternative to point evaluations in the BSF context.
To keep the presentation short, we focus on the space

C^{1}([0,1])
of continuous functions
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
that are continuously differentiable on
(0,1)

and for which there exists a continuous
g:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
g_{|(0,1)}=f^{\prime}
. As usual, we equip this space
with the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{1}([0,1])}:=\|f\|_{\infty}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\,,\qquad\qquad f\in C^{1}([0,1]).

It is well-known that
C^{1}([0,1])
is a separable BSF. Now, the following simple lemma
provides a separating sequence for
C^{1}([0,1])
and thus also a filtering sequence via Proposition
4.3
and
Proposition
4.1
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\{t_{i}:i\geq 1\}\subset(0,1)
be dense in
[0,1]
and
y_{i}^{\prime}:C^{1}([0,1])\to\mathbb{R}
be defined by

y_{i}^{\prime}(f):=f^{\prime}(t_{i})
for all
f\in C^{1}([0,1])
.
If we further set
y_{0}^{\prime}:=\delta_{0}
, then
(y_{i}^{\prime})_{i\geq 0}
is a separating sequence for
C^{1}([0,1])
.

Note that
Lemma
4.10
actually
provides an alternative to point evaluations since for
i\geq 1
the functionals
y_{i}^{\prime}
do not access
f
but its derivative.
Moreover, generalizations of Lemma
4.10
to higher dimensions and derivatives are straightforward, and therefore
our results of Section
3
can indeed be applied to the PDE
applications mentioned in the introduction.

5
Conditioning Continuous Gaussian Processes

In this section we illustrate how the general results of Section
3
can be applied to the GP4ML setting. Namely, we will
consider the conditioning of Gaussian random variables for
the spaces

E:=C(T)
and
F:=C(S)
, where
(T,\metric)
is a compact metric space and
S\subset T
is closed.
In addition, we will discuss how these results can be formulated from a
Gaussian process perspective.

To apply our general results we first note that
C(T)
is a separable, see e.g.
[
15
, Theorem V.6.6]
or
[
18
, Corollary 11.2.5]
.
Moreover, to describe the dual of
C(T)
, we write

{\cal M}(T)
for the space of all finite, signed Borel measures equipped with the
total variation norm. Riesz’ representation theorem then shows that the map
\hat{\cdot}\,:{\cal M}(T)\to C(T)^{\prime}
given by

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mapsto\hat{\nu}:=\biggl(f\mapsto\int_{T}f\,\mathrm{d}\nu\biggr)

(24)

is an isometric isomorphism,
see e.g.
[
14
, Theorem 7.3.6]
and
[
6
, Corollary 29.13]
.
In addition, note that (
24
) maps the Dirac measures
\delta_{\{t\}}
to the point
evaluation functionals
\delta_{t}
.

To translate our main results to Gaussian processes, we also need
a well-known, canonical one-to-one relationship between Gaussian processes with continuous paths and
C(T)
-valued Gaussian
random variables. To begin with we fix a probability space

(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
and a stochastic process
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
over
\Omega
.
Then
(X_{t})_{t\in T}

is called a Gaussian process if, for all

n\geq 1
and all
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T
, the
random variable
(X_{t_{1}},\dots,X_{t_{n}}):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is Gaussian.
Moreover, for
a fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, the map
X_{\bullet}(\omega):T\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)

is called a path.
We say that the process has continuous paths, if
X_{\bullet}(\omega)\in C(T)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Clearly, if only
\mathrm{P}
-almost all paths are continuous, then we can easily construct a version of
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
having
continuous paths. In the following, we assume that we have already chosen such a version.

To describe the above mentioned relationship, let us now fix a
map
X:\Omega\to C(T)
. Then we obtain
a family
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
of functions
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
by setting

\displaystyle X_{t}(\omega):=\langle\delta_{t},X(\omega)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=(X(\omega))(t)\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega.

(25)

Obviously, this construction ensures that all paths
t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)
are continuous.
Conversely, if we have a family
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
of functions
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that for each
\omega\in\Omega
the path
X_{\bullet}(\omega)
is
continuous, then

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{\bullet}(\omega)

(26)

defines a map
X:\Omega\to C(T)
.
Clearly, the operations (
25
) and (
26
) are inverse to each other.
The following folklore result shows that they map
C(T)
-valued Gaussian random variables to Gaussian processes with continuous paths and vice versa.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,

X:\Omega\to C(T)
be a map, and

(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be given by (
25
). Then the following statements are equivalent:

i)

X
is a
C(T)
-valued Gaussian random variable.

ii)

(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is a Gaussian process with continuous paths.

In this case, both the mean and the covariance function are continuous.

It is well-known that a Gaussian process
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
with continuous paths can be described by the mean and covariance
functions given by (
1
) and (
2
).
Moreover, if
X
is the Gaussian random variable associated to the process via (
26
), then
the mean
\mathbb{E}X
and the covariance operator
\cov(X)
can be used to compute the mean and covariance function, namely we have

\displaystyle m(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}X_{t}=\langle\delta_{t},\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\,,

(27)

\displaystyle k(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=\cov(X_{t_{1}},X_{t_{2}})=\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\cov(X)\delta_{t_{2}}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

(28)

for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in T
, where we have used (
A.11
), respectively (
B.1
).
Conversely, if we have the mean function
m
, then (
27
) shows that we know the function
\mathbb{E}X\in C(T)
pointwise, i.e. we know
\mathbb{E}X
.
Moreover, the covariance function determines the covariance operator by

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mu},\cov(X)\hat{\nu}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\int_{T}\int_{T}k(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(s)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)\,,\qquad\qquad\mu,\nu\in{\cal M}(T),

see Lemma
E.4
for details.
This justifies the notation

\displaystyle{\cal N}(m,k):={\cal N}\bigl(\mathbb{E}X,\cov X\bigr)

for the distribution of a given Gaussian random variable
X:\Omega\to C(T)
, where
m
and
k
are the mean and covariance function of the associated
Gaussian process.

In summary, instead of working with Gaussian random variables
X:\Omega\to C(T)
we can alternatively consider
Gaussian processes
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
with continuous paths together with their mean and covariance function.
In the following we will adopt this point of view, which is common in the GP4ML
literature, see e.g.
[
7
,
47
,
32
]
.
In particular, we speak of conditioned processes, when
we condition the associated Gaussian random variables.

Let us now assume that we have a compact metric space
(T,\metric)
,
a Gaussian processes
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
with continuous paths, and an observation

(Y_{s})_{s\in S}

of this
process on a non-empty
S\subset T
. In other words, we have
Y_{s}(\omega)=X_{s}(\omega)
for all
s\in S
and
\omega\in\Omega
, where

(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
is the underlying probability space.
By the continuity of the paths we may assume without loss of generality
that
S
is closed, and thus also a compact metric space. Now,
to describe this setup by Gaussian random variables, we write

E:=C(T)
and
F:=C(S)
and denote the Gaussian random variables associated to the two processes above via
(
26
) by
X:\Omega\to C(T)
and
Y:\Omega\to C(S)
.
This gives

\displaystyle Y(\omega)=(X(\omega))_{|S}\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega,

(29)

that is
Y=\,\cdot\,_{|S}\circ X
, where the restriction operator
\,\cdot\,_{|S}:C(T)\to C(S)
is bounded and linear.

We first consider the case of a finite set
S=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}
.
Here we recall from (
22
) that the
bounded linear operator
I_{S}:C(T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by

\displaystyle I_{S}(f):=f(S):=\bigl(f(s_{1}),\dots,f(s_{n})\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad f\in C(T)\,.

(30)

In this case, (
29
) reduces to
Y=I_{S}\circ X
as
C(S)
can be canonically identified by
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Finally, note that if we have another finite set
R=\{r_{1},\dots,r_{m}\}
, then the corresponding covariance operator

\cov(I_{R}\circ X,I_{S}\circ X):\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
can be represented by the
m\times n
-matrix

\displaystyle K_{R,S}:=\bigl(\cov(X_{r_{i}},X_{s_{j}})\bigr)_{i,j}=\bigl(k(r_{i},s_{j})\bigr)_{i,j}

as discussed around (
7
). Furthermore, in the case
R=\{r\}
, we use the shorthand
K_{r,S}:=K_{R,S}
, and an analogous
simplified notation is used in the case
S=\{s\}
.

With these preparations, the application of Theorem
3.1
to the specific
observational model (
29
) with finite
S
reads as follows.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space, and
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a
Gaussian processes on
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
with continuous paths.
Moreover, let
X:\Omega\to C(T)
be the Gaussian random variable associated to the process via (
26
)
and
S:=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}\subset T
. Finally, let

\displaystyle Y:=I_{S}\circ X\,,

(31)

where
I_{S}:C(T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by (
30
).
Then for every version

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(C(T))\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y

there exist an
N\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(m_{X|Y=y},k_{X|Y}\bigr)\,,

where the mean and covariance functions are given by

\displaystyle m_{X|Y=y}(t)

\displaystyle=m(t)+K_{t,S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}(y-m(S))\,,

\displaystyle k_{X|Y}(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=k(t_{1},t_{2})-K_{t_{1},S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}K_{S,t_{2}}\,.

Before we proceed with conditioning with respect to observations on infinite
S
, we note that we can easily generalize the observational model
(
31
) to e.g.

\displaystyle Y:=I_{S}\circ X+\varepsilon\,,

(32)

where
\varepsilon\sim{\cal N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
is independent of
X
,
\sigma\geq 0
, and
I_{n}
denotes the
n
-dimensional identity matrix.
Indeed, in view of Theorem
3.1
, we only need to recompute the representing matrices of the operators
\cov(X,Y)
,
\cov(Y)
, and

\cov(Y,X)
. However, we have, for example,

\displaystyle\cov(Y)

\displaystyle=\cov\bigl(I_{S}\circ X+\varepsilon,I_{S}\circ X+\varepsilon\bigr)

\displaystyle=\cov(I_{S}\circ X,I_{S}\circ X)+\cov(I_{S}\circ X,\varepsilon)+\cov(\varepsilon,I_{S}\circ X)+\cov(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\cov(Y)+\cov(\varepsilon)\,,

where in the last step we used the independence of
X
and
\varepsilon
. Consequently,
\cov(Y)
is represented by the matrix
K_{S,S}+\sigma^{2}I_{n}
.
Some additional, but essentially
analogous calculations then show that the
corresponding mean and covariance functions can be computed by

\displaystyle m_{X|Y=y}(t)

\displaystyle=m(t)+K_{t,S}(K_{S,S}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{\dagger}(y-m(S))\,,

\displaystyle k_{X|Y}(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=k(t_{1},t_{2})-K_{t_{1},S}(K_{S,S}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{\dagger}K_{S,t_{2}}

for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in T
. Not surprisingly, these formulas are the well-known textbook formulas from the GP4MLs literature, see e.g.
[
47
, Chapter 2.2]
. However, there is is subtle but important difference in their meaning: The mentioned textbook formulas do not ensure
that a Gaussian process with the above mean and covariance function has a version with continuous paths and even if this
can be derived by other means, they do not ensure that the resulting measure of such a version equals the regular conditional probability on
C(T)

we seek. In contrast, our approach establishes exactly these guarantees, so that the common functional interpretation of GP4MLs is indeed justified.
This is similar in spirit to
[
58
, Corollary 4]
, where more general functionals on
C(T)
are considered
in the noise free case
\varepsilon=0
.

Let us now turn to observations on infinite, closed
S\subset T
, where again
(T,\metric)
is a compact metric space.
Since in this case
(S,\metric)
is also a compact metric space, there exists
a countable, dense
S_{\infty}\subset S
with enumeration
S_{\infty}=\{s_{i}:i\geq 1\}
, and
we have seen in Corollary
4.6
that
the evaluation operators
I_{S_{n}}:C(S)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
S_{n}:=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}

give a filtering sequence for
C(S)
.
Roughly speaking, this sequence provides us with
a mean to approximate the infinite dimensional
observational random variable
Y:=X_{|S}
with the help of the sequence
(Y_{n})
of finite dimensional observations
Y_{n}:=X_{|S_{n}}
taken
at the points
S_{n}
. Applying Theorem
3.3
to this situation leads to:

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,

(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space,
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a
Gaussian process on
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
with continuous paths, and

X:\Omega\to C(T)
be the Gaussian random variable associated to the process via (
26
). For closed

S\subset T
let

\displaystyle Y:=X_{|S}\,.

(33)

Finally, let
S_{\infty}\subset S
be countable and dense with enumeration
S_{\infty}=\{s_{i}:i\geq 1\}
. We write

S_{n}:=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}
for all
n\geq 1
.
Then for every version

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(C(T))\times C(S)\to[0,1]
of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y

there exist an
N\in\mathfrak{B}(C(S))
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
such that for all
g\in C(S)\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|g)={\cal N}\bigl(m_{X|Y=y},k_{X|Y}\bigr)\,,

where the mean and covariance functions are given by

\displaystyle m_{X|Y=g}(t)

\displaystyle=m(t)+\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{t,S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}(g(S_{n})-m(S_{n}))\,,

(34)

\displaystyle k_{X|Y}(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=k(t_{1},t_{2})-\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{t_{1},S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}K_{S_{n},t_{2}}\,

(35)

and the convergence is uniform in
t\in T
, respectively in
t_{1},t_{2}\in T
.
Moreover, for all
g\in C(S)\setminus N
,
s\in S
, and
t\in T
we have

\displaystyle m_{X|Y=g}(s)

\displaystyle=g(s)\,,

(36)

\displaystyle k_{X|Y}(s,t)

\displaystyle=0\,.

(37)

Of course, the remaining assertions of Theorem
3.3
as well as Theorems
3.4
and
3.5

also hold in the setup considered in
Theorem
5.3
.
For example, we have the weak convergence

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|(X_{|S_{n}})}(\,\cdot\,|g(S_{n}))\to\mathrm{P}_{X|(X_{|S})}(\,\cdot\,|g)\,.

(38)

In addition, combining the approximations
(
34
) and
(
35
) with (
36
) and
(
37
) shows

\displaystyle m(s)+K_{s,S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}(g(S_{n})-m(S_{n}))

\displaystyle\to g(s)\,,

\displaystyle k(s,t)-K_{s,S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}K_{S_{n},t}

\displaystyle\to 0

for
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-almost all observed paths
g\in C(S)
and
all
s\in S
and
t\in T
, where again the convergence is uniform in
s
, respectively
s
and
t
.
Note that these findings substantially generalize the state-of-the-art contractions results established in
[
35
]

in the sense that neither
S=T
nor additional assumptions on the observational model are needed.
Finally, to the best of our knowledge there is no known result ensuring (
38
)
even under restricted assumptions.

6
Proofs of the Results of Section
3

6.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

Before we can present the proof of Theorem
3.1
we need an auxiliary result.
To motivate it, let
E
be a Banach space,
T:E^{\prime}\to E
be bounded and linear, and
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. If
(x^{\prime})^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
denotes the dual of
x^{\prime}
, then
we can consider the
bounded linear map
x^{\prime}\circ T\circ(x^{\prime})^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
. Since both
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}
are one-dimensional, this map
can be described by a
1\times 1
-matrix, which is determined in the next lemma.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
E
be a Banach space,
T:E^{\prime}\to E
be a bounded, linear operator, and
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bigl(x^{\prime}\circ T\circ(x^{\prime})^{\prime}\bigr)(\langle t,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}})=\langle x^{\prime},Tx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot t\,,\qquad\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},

where
(x^{\prime})^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
denotes the dual of
x^{\prime}:E\to\mathbb{R}
and

\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
denotes the inner product of the Hilbert space
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Recall that
the elements in
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
can be represented by
\mathbb{R}
via
the canonical isomorphism
t\mapsto\langle t,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Now observe that for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in E
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle(x^{\prime})^{\prime}(\langle t,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}),x\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\bigl\langle\langle t,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}},x^{\prime}(x)\bigr\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{\prime},\mathbb{R}}=\langle t,x^{\prime}(x)\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}=t\cdot x^{\prime}(x)=\langle tx^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

In other words, we have

(x^{\prime})^{\prime}(\langle t,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}})=tx^{\prime}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. This in turn yields

\displaystyle\bigl(x^{\prime}\circ T\circ(x^{\prime})^{\prime}\bigr)(\langle 1,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}})=(x^{\prime}\circ T)\bigl((x^{\prime})^{\prime}(\langle 1,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\mathbb{R}})\bigr)=(x^{\prime}\circ T)(x^{\prime})=\langle x^{\prime},Tx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

and by multiplying both sides with
t\in\mathbb{R}
we obtain the assertion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

We begin by some preparations. To this end, we fix a version
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y
.
Moreover,
we choose a countable,
w^{*}
-dense
\mathfrak{D}\subset B_{E^{\prime}}

with the help of Theorem
A.1
.
Finally, we fix an enumeration
(x^{\prime}_{i})
of
\mathfrak{D}
, that is,
\mathfrak{D}=\{x_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}
.

For
i\geq 1
we now define
X_{i}:=\langle x^{\prime}_{i},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
.
Then
X_{i}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
and
Y:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

are jointly Gaussian random variables with joint distribution
\mathrm{P}_{(X_{i},Y)}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We
fix a version
\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of the
regular
conditional probability of
X_{i}
given
Y
.
By Theorem
2.3
there then exists
an
N_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{i}^{\prime})=0
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N_{i}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y),K_{X_{i}|Y}\bigr)\,,

where the expectation and covariance are given by

\displaystyle\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}X_{i}+K_{X_{i},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,,

\displaystyle K_{X_{i}|Y}

\displaystyle:=K_{X_{i},X_{i}}-K_{X_{i},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}K_{Y,X_{i}}\,.

Using the definition of
X_{i}
, the expression for the expectation reads as

\displaystyle\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}X_{i}+K_{X_{i},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime}_{i},\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+\cov(x^{\prime}_{i}\circ X,Y)\cov(Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime}_{i},\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+\bigl\langle x^{\prime}_{i},\cov(X,Y)\cov(Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle x^{\prime}_{i},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)\cov(Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

(39)

where we used the permutability of integration and operator application (
A.11
)
and the cross covariance formula for linearly transformed random variables (
B.3
).
To investigate
K_{X_{i}|Y}
in a similar fashion, we consider the bounded linear operator
R_{X,Y}:E^{\prime}\to E
defined by

\displaystyle R_{X,Y}:=\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X)\,.

By
X_{i}=x^{\prime}_{i}\circ X
and (
B.3
) we then find

\displaystyle\cov(X_{i},Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X_{i})=x^{\prime}_{i}\circ R_{X,Y}\circ(x^{\prime}_{i})^{\prime}\,.

Moreover, Lemma
6.1
shows that the operator
x^{\prime}_{i}\circ R_{X,Y}\circ(x^{\prime}_{i})^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
is described by
the
1\times 1
-matrix
\langle x_{i}^{\prime},R_{X,Y}x_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
, while the definition of the covariance matrices
K_{X_{i},Y}
,
K_{Y,Y}
, and
K_{Y,X_{i}}

shows that

\cov(X_{i},Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X_{i}):\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
is described by the
1\times 1
-matrix
K_{X_{i},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}K_{Y,X_{i}}
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle K_{X_{i},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}K_{Y,X_{i}}=\langle x_{i}^{\prime},R_{X,Y}x_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

(40)

In addition, (
B.6
) shows

\displaystyle K_{X_{i},X_{i}}=\var(X_{i})=\var(\langle x_{i}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E})=\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\cov(X)x_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

Combining both considerations, we thus find
