\displaystyle K_{X_{i}|Y}=K_{X_{i},X_{i}}-K_{X_{i},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}K_{Y,X_{i}}=\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\cov(X)x_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\langle x_{i}^{\prime},R_{X,Y}x_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

(41)

Our next goal is to describe the relationship between

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
and the just considered
\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
with the help of
Theorem
D.2
, which describes regular conditional probabilities under transformations.
To this end, we set
T_{0}:=E
and
T_{1}:=\mathbb{R}
, as well as
U_{0}:=U_{1}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
{\cal A}_{0}:={\cal A}_{1}:=\mathfrak{B}^{n}
.
Moreover, we consider the maps
\Phi:=x_{i}^{\prime}
and
\Psi:=\id_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
as well as the measures

\mathrm{P}^{(0)}:=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
and
\mathrm{P}^{(1)}:=\mathrm{P}_{(X_{i},Y)}=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{(\Phi,\Psi)}
.
By Theorem
D.2
there then exists
an
N_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{i}^{\prime\prime})=\mathrm{P}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{(0)}(N_{i}^{\prime\prime})=0
such that for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N_{i}^{\prime\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}\bigl((x_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}(B)|y\bigr)=\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(B|y)\,,\qquad\qquad B\in\mathfrak{B}.

Consequently, we find

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y),K_{X_{i}|Y}\bigr)

(42)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus(N_{i}^{\prime}\cup N_{i}^{\prime\prime})
.
We define

\displaystyle N:=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(N_{i}^{\prime}\cup N_{i}^{\prime\prime})\,.

Obviously, this gives
N\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
, and by construction
(
42
) holds for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
and
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathfrak{D}
.

Let us now fix a
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
. Then we have just seen in (
42
) that
for all
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
the
image measure

(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))_{x^{\prime}_{i}}
of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
under
x_{i}^{\prime}
is
Gaussian, and by
Theorem
C.4
we can thus conclude that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is Gaussian.

Next we verify the formulas for the expectation and covariance.
Let us first consider the expectation:
For all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
and
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathfrak{D}
, the identity (
42
) shows

\displaystyle\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}\bigr)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\id_{\mathbb{R}}\,\mathrm{d}\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\int_{E}(\id_{\mathbb{R}}\circ x_{i}^{\prime})(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\langle x_{i}^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}(y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

(43)

where in the third step we used the transformation formula for image measures, and in the last step
we used that
\id_{E}
is Bochner-integrable with respect to
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
since

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is a Gaussian measure, and thus (
9
) holds true.
Using (
39
) we thus find

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}(y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\bigl\langle x^{\prime}_{i},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)\cov(Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

for all
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
. Since
\mathfrak{D}
is
w^{*}
-dense in
B_{E^{\prime}}
, a simple limit argument then
shows that the latter identity not only holds for all
x_{i}^{\prime}
but actually for all
x^{\prime}\in B_{E^{\prime}}
.
By Hahn-Banach’s theorem we thus find

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y}(y)=\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)\cov(Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N.

To verify the formula for the covariance, we again fix an
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
. For
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
,
the Identity (
42
) and the fact that
\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}
is a distribution on
\mathbb{R}
then yield

\displaystyle K_{X_{i}|Y}=\var\Bigl(\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigl(\id_{\mathbb{R}}(t)-\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y)\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\bigl(\id_{\mathbb{R}}\circ x_{i}^{\prime}-\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y)\bigr)^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\bigl(\langle x_{i}^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\mu_{X_{i}|Y}(y)\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\bigl\langle x_{i}^{\prime},x-\mu_{X|Y}(y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}^{2}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\cov\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)x_{i}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

where we have also used a projection formula for covariances (
B.6
), the basic transformation formula for
\mathbb{R}
-valued integrals,
(
43
), and (
B.1
).
Combining this with (
41
) we thus find

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\cov\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)x_{i}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{i}^{\prime},\cov(X)x_{i}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\bigl\langle x_{i}^{\prime},R_{X,Y}x_{i}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

for all
x_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
.
Now the left hand side of this identity is
w^{*}
-continuous on
B_{E^{\prime}}
by
Theorem
B.3
.
Moreover,
(\cov Y)^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}
is symmetric and non-negative, see the discussion around (
B.8
).
Consequently,
the right-hand side
is also
w^{*}
-continuous on
B_{E^{\prime}}
by
Theorem
B.3
and Lemma
B.4
. Since

\mathfrak{D}
is
w^{*}
-dense in
B_{E^{\prime}}
, we thus find

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\cov\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)x^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\bigl\langle x^{\prime},R_{X,Y}x^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

for all
x^{\prime}\in B_{E^{\prime}}
. By scaling this implies

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\bigl(\cov(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))+R_{X,Y}\bigr)x^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

(44)

for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
(\cov Y)^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}
is symmetric and non-negative, Lemma
B.4
shows
that
R_{X,Y}
is an abstract covariance operator, and this implies that

\displaystyle\cov(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))+R_{X,Y}:E^{\prime}\to E

is an abstract covariance operator.
In addition,
\cov(X):E^{\prime}\to E
is also an abstract covariance operator, and (
44
) shows that
both abstract covariance operators coincide on the diagonal. Lemma
B.2

thus yields
\cov(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))+R_{X,Y}=\cov(X)
, that is, we have found
the formula for the covariance.

Let us now prove the claims made for
Z
.
To this end, we write
\pi_{E}:E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to E
and
\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}:E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
for the coordinate
projections onto
E
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Moreover,
we define
N_{0}:=Y^{-1}(N)
, which
yields
N_{0}\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}(N_{0})=0
.
For
\omega\in\Omega\setminus N_{0}
and
y:=Y(\omega)
we then have
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
and therefore

\mu_{X|Y}(y)
is the mean of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
.
Moreover, since
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is Gaussian, we have
\id_{E}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y),E)}
.
For
g:=\id_{E}

this gives
g\circ\pi_{E}(\,\cdot\,,y)=g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y),E)}
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
,
and therefore, the random variable
Z_{g}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to E
defined by

\displaystyle Z_{g}(y):=\int_{E}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N}(y)\cdot\bigl((g\circ\pi_{E})(x,y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\mathrm{d}x|y)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

provides
a version
Z_{g}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
of
\mathbb{E}(g\circ\pi_{E}|\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}^{-1}(\mathfrak{B}^{n}))=\mathbb{E}(g\circ\pi_{E}|E\times\mathfrak{B}^{n})

by Theorem
D.6
applied to the probability measure

\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
on
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n}=\sigma(E^{\prime})\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\int_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{1}_{E\times A}\cdot(Z_{g}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}=\int_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{1}_{E\times A}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}

(45)

for all
A\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
.
Moreover, for
\omega\in\Omega\setminus N_{0}
we find

\displaystyle Z(\omega)=\mu_{X|Y}(Y(\omega))

\displaystyle=\int_{E}x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\mathrm{d}x|Y(\omega))

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N}(Y(\omega))\cdot\bigl((g\circ\pi_{E})(x,Y(\omega))\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\mathrm{d}x|Y(\omega))

\displaystyle=Z_{g}(Y(\omega))\,,

which shows
\mathrm{P}(\{Z\neq Z_{g}\circ Y\})=0
.
Since
Z
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable by construction, it thus
suffices to show that

Z_{g}\circ Y
is a version of

\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
. Here the
\sigma(Y)
-measurability of
Z_{g}\circ Y
is ensured by construction.
Moreover, for
A_{0}\in\sigma(Y)
there exists an
A\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with
A_{0}=Y^{-1}(A)
, and with this we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\!\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}\cdot(Z_{g}\circ Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{\Omega}\!(\boldsymbol{1}_{A}\circ Y)\cdot(Z_{g}\circ Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\!\boldsymbol{1}_{A}Z_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}

\displaystyle=\int_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\!\!(\boldsymbol{1}_{A}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\cdot(Z_{g}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}

\displaystyle=\int_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{1}_{E\times A}\cdot(Z_{g}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}

as well as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}\cdot X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{1}_{A}\circ Y)\cdot X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\bigl(\boldsymbol{1}_{A}\circ\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(X,Y)\bigr)\cdot\bigl(g\circ\pi_{E}(X,Y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\boldsymbol{1}_{E\times A}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}\,.

Combining the latter two calculations with (
45
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}\cdot(Z_{g}\circ Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}\cdot X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

for all
A_{0}\in\sigma(Y)
. This shows that
Z_{g}\circ Y
is a version of

\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
and hence so is
Z
.
Moreover, the map
T:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to E
defined by

\displaystyle Ty:=\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(46)

is affine linear and continuous
with
Z=T\circ Y
. Since
Y
is Gaussian random variable, we then see that
Z
is also a Gaussian random variable.
In addition, we have
\mathbb{E}Z=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|Y))=\mathbb{E}X
by Theorem
D.4
.

To verify the formula for
\cov(Z)
, we first consider the case
E=\mathbb{R}
, in which we only need to determine
\var Z
.
Since we already know
\mathbb{E}Z=\mathbb{E}X
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\var Z=\mathbb{E}Z^{2}-(\mathbb{E}Z)^{2}=\mathbb{E}Z^{2}-(\mathbb{E}X)^{2}\,.

(47)

Moreover, since for
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
we have
\mu_{X|Y}(y)=Ty
we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}Z^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(T\circ Y)^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(Ty)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mu_{X|Y}(y))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)

(48)

by the definition (
46
) of
T
and the already established formula for
\mu_{X|Y}(y)
.
Since
E=\mathbb{R}
we further have
K_{X|Y}=\var(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N
and combining this with
(
48
) and (
47
) leads to

\displaystyle K_{X|Y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K_{X|Y}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)-\bigl(\mu_{X|Y}(y)\bigr)^{2}\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)-\mathbb{E}Z^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\pi_{E})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}-\var Z-(\mathbb{E}X)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}-\var Z-(\mathbb{E}X)^{2}

\displaystyle=\var X-\var Z\,,

where in the third to last step we used the disintegration formula (
D.9
). In other words, we have found the desired covariance formula in the case
E=\mathbb{R}
.

To verify the formula for
\cov(Z)
in the case of general
E
, we pick an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}

and define
X_{0}:=\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
as well as

\displaystyle Z_{0}:=\mathbb{E}X_{0}+\cov(X_{0},Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,.

Using (
B.3
) this definition can also be expressed as

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}X_{0}+\bigl(x^{\prime}\circ\cov(X,Y)\circ(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\bigr)(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}X_{0}+\cov(X_{0},Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=Z_{0}\,.

Since
X_{0}
and
Y
are jointly Gaussian random variables, the already treated
case
E=\mathbb{R}
thus shows

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},\cov(Z)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\var(\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E})=\var Z_{0}=\var(X_{0})-K_{X_{0}|Y}

\displaystyle=K_{X_{0},Y}K_{Y,Y}^{\dagger}K_{Y,X_{0}}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime},R_{X,Y}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

where in first step we used (
B.6
) and in
the last step we repeated the calculations of (
40
).
Since
\cov(Z)
is an abstract
covariance operator and we have seen around (
44
) that
R_{X,Y}
is also an abstract
covariance operator, Lemma
B.2

then yields
\cov(Z)=R_{X,Y}
.

Finally, for the proof of
{\cal G}(Z)\subset{\cal G}(Y)
, we consider the bounded linear operator
A:=\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to E
.
The definition of
Z
then gives

\displaystyle Z-\mathbb{E}Z=Z-\mathbb{E}X=\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)=A(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,.

By Lemma
C.6
we thus conclude
{\cal G}(Z)={\cal G}(Z-\mathbb{E}Z)\subset{\cal G}(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)={\cal G}(Y)
.
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Before we can prove Theorem
3.3
, we again need some
additional machinery.

We begin with a result showing that filtering sequences induce filtrations in a general setup.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
F
be a separable Banach space
and
(A_{n})
be a filtering sequence for
F
.
Moreover, let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
Y:\Omega\to F
be
\sigma(F^{\prime})
- measurable, and
Y_{n}:=A_{n}\circ Y
. Then

(\sigma(Y_{n}))
is a filtration in
{\cal A}
and
we have

\displaystyle\sigma(Y)=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\sigma(Y_{n})\biggr)\,.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
.

We first note that we have

\displaystyle\sigma(Y_{n})=\sigma(A_{n}\circ Y)=(A_{n}\circ Y)^{-1}(\mathfrak{B}^{n})=Y^{-1}(A_{n}^{-1}(\mathfrak{B}^{n}))=Y^{-1}(\sigma(A_{n}))\,,

where the second and the last step uses an elementary formula for the
\sigma
-algebra generated by a single map,
see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 1.78]
. Since
(\sigma(A_{n}))
is a filtration, so is
(\sigma(Y_{n}))
.

To establish the formula for
\sigma(Y)
, we write

{\cal E}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\sigma(A_{n})
. By assumption we know that

\sigma({\cal E})=\sigma(F^{\prime})
, and hence we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma(Y)=Y^{-1}(\sigma({\cal E}))=\sigma(Y^{-1}({\cal E}))=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}Y^{-1}(\sigma(A_{n}))\biggr)=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\sigma(Y_{n})\biggr)\,,

where in the second step we used another well-known fact on generated
\sigma
-algebras, see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 1.81]
and its proof.
∎

The next result translates martingale convergence to
convergence of integrals taken with respect to regular conditional probabilities. Readers unfamiliar with the notion of uniform tightness are referred to
Supplement
C
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
and
F
be separable Banach spaces,
and
X:\Omega\to E
and
Y:\Omega\to F
be random variables.
Moreover,
let
(A_{n})
be a filtering sequence for
F
and

\displaystyle Y_{n}:=A_{n}\circ Y\,,\qquad\qquad n\geq 1.

Then for all versions
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,1]
of the regular conditional probabilities of
X
given
Y_{n}

there
exists an
N\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl\{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)):n\geq 1\bigr\}

is uniformly tight for all
y\in F\setminus N
.

In addition, for all Banach spaces
G
and
all
\mathrm{P}_{X}
-Bochner integrable
g:E\to G
there exists an
N_{g}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{g})=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N_{g}
and
n\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y),G)}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)),G)}

(49)

as well as

\displaystyle\int_{E}g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))\to\int_{E}g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)\,,

(50)

where the convergence is with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{G}
.

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

We begin with some preparations needed for both assertions.
To this end, we write

{\cal A}_{n}:=\sigma(A_{n})\subset\sigma(F^{\prime})

and
{\cal A}_{\infty}:=\sigma(F^{\prime})
. We further define
A_{\infty}:=\id_{F}
and
Y_{\infty}:=Y
, which gives
Y_{\infty}=A_{\infty}\circ Y
.
Since
(A_{n})
is a filtering sequence we then have

\displaystyle\sigma(A_{\infty})={\cal A}_{\infty}=\sigma\bigl(A_{n}:n\geq 1\bigr)\,.

In addition, we denote
the restriction of
\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
onto
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
by
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}:=\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}\bigr)_{|\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For
n=\infty
, this gives
\mathrm{Q}^{(\infty)}=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the identity

Y_{n}=A_{n}\circ Y
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y_{n})}=\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}\bigr)_{(\id_{E},A_{n})}=\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{(\id_{E},A_{n})}\,.

With the help of Theorem
D.1

we now fix a regular conditional probability
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
and write

\mathrm{Q}^{(\infty)}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):=\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
.
Moreover, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
we analogously fix a regular conditional probability

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}(E)\times F\to[0,1]

of
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}
.
Theorem
D.2
applied to
\Phi:=\id_{E}:E\to E
and
\Psi:=A_{n}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the probability measures
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}:=\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}
and

\mathrm{P}^{(1)}:=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y_{n})}

then gives an
N^{(1)}_{n}\in{\cal A}_{n}

with
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{F}(N^{(1)}_{n}))=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N^{(1)}_{n}
and all
B\in\mathfrak{B}(E)

we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(B|y)=\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}\bigl(B|A_{n}(y)\bigr)\,.

(51)

Here we note that in the case
n=\infty
our construction gives
\mathrm{Q}^{(\infty)}=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
as noted above
and therefore (
51
) holds in the case
n=\infty
for all
y\in F
.
Let us now define

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{trafo}}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}N^{(1)}_{n}\,.

Since for all
n\geq 1
we have
N^{(1)}_{n}\in{\cal A}_{n}\subset{\cal A}_{\infty}=\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{n}^{(1)})=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}(E\times N_{n}^{(1)})=\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(E\times N_{n}^{(1)})=\mathrm{Q}_{F}^{(n)}(N_{n}^{(1)})=0\,,

(52)

we then find
N_{\mathrm{trafo}}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{\mathrm{trafo}})=0
. In addition,
(
51
) holds for all
y\in F\setminus N_{\mathrm{trafo}}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
by our construction.
Finally, our construction also ensures
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{E}=\mathrm{P}_{X}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Our first goal is to establish (
49
) and (
50
). To this end,
we fix a Banach space
G
and
a
\mathrm{P}_{X}
-Bochner integrable
g:E\to G
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}

our construction then ensures

\displaystyle\int_{E\times F}\|g\circ\pi_{E}\|_{G}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}=\int_{E}\|g\|_{G}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{E}=\int_{E}\|g\|_{G}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}<\infty\,.

(53)

In other words, we have

g\circ\pi_{E}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{Q}^{(n)},G)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
In the next step we will
apply the martingale convergence Theorem
D.5
. To this end,
we define the
\sigma
-algebras

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{n}:=\pi_{F}^{-1}({\cal A}_{n})=\{E\times A:A\in{\cal A}_{n}\}\,,\qquad\qquad n\geq 1

and we further write

\displaystyle{\cal C}_{\infty}:=\pi_{F}^{-1}(\sigma(F^{\prime}))=\{E\times A:A\in\sigma(F^{\prime})\}=\{E\times A:A\in{\cal A}_{\infty}\}\,.

Since

({\cal A}_{n})
is a filtration
with
{\cal A}_{\infty}=\sigma({\cal A}_{n}:n\geq 1)
, we quickly check
that

({\cal C}_{n})
is a filtration
with
{\cal C}_{\infty}=\sigma({\cal C}_{n}:n\geq 1)
.
For every fixed choice of versions
g_{n}
of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}}(g\circ\pi_{E}|{\cal C}_{n})

and
g_{\infty}
of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}}(g\circ\pi_{E}|{\cal C}_{\infty})

Theorem
D.5

applied to the probability space
(E\times F,\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes\sigma(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)})

then gives an
N^{\prime}\in{\cal C}_{\infty}
with
\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}(N^{\prime})=0
and

\displaystyle\|g_{n}(x,y)-g_{\infty}(x,y)\|_{G}\to 0\,,\qquad\qquad(x,y)\in(E\times F)\setminus N^{\prime}\,.

(54)

Moreover, note that
N^{\prime}
is of the form
N^{\prime}=E\times N_{\mathrm{mart}}
with
N_{\mathrm{mart}}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
since
N^{\prime}\in{\cal C}_{\infty}
. In addition, we have

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{\mathrm{mart}})=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}(N^{\prime})=0
.

Let us now translate this convergence to regular conditional probabilities. To this end, we apply
Theorem
D.6
to
g\circ\pi_{E}
and the measures
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}
defined on
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
:
Because of (
53
) we then obtain
an
N^{(2)}_{n}\in{\cal A}_{n}
with
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{F}(N^{(2)}_{n})=0
and

\displaystyle g=g\circ\pi_{E}(\,\cdot\,,y)\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,|y),G)}\,,\qquad\qquad y\in F\setminus N^{(2)}_{n},\,n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}

(55)

such that

Z_{n}:F\to G
defined by

\displaystyle Z_{n}(y):=\int_{E}\boldsymbol{1}_{F\setminus N^{(2)}_{n}}(y)\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})(x,y)\,\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(\mathrm{d}x|y)=\int_{E}\boldsymbol{1}_{F\setminus N^{(2)}_{n}}(y)\cdot g(x)\,\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

for all
y\in F

is strongly measurable, the disintegration formula (
D.9
) holds true, and

Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F}:E\times F\to G
is a version of

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}}(g\circ\pi_{E}|{\cal C}_{n})
, that is, we have

\displaystyle\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}=\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}

(56)

for all
C\in{\cal C}_{n}
.
Our next intermediate goal is to verify that

Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F}
is also a version of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}}(g\circ\pi_{E}|{\cal C}_{n})
.
Here, the strong
{\cal C}_{n}
-measurability is obviously satisfied since
Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F}
is a version of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}}(g\circ\pi_{E}|{\cal C}_{n})
.
Moreover, for
C\in{\cal C}_{n}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}=\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}\,,

(57)

where we used that
\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F})
is strongly
{\cal C}_{n}
-measurable and thus also strong

\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
-measurable
and that
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}
is the restriction of
\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
onto
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
.
In addition,
g\circ\pi_{E}
is strongly
\{B\times F:B\in\mathfrak{B}(E)\}
-measurable
and thus also strongly
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
-measurable.
Consequently,

\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})
is also strongly
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
-measurable, and since

\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}
is the restriction of
\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
onto
\mathfrak{B}(E)\otimes{\cal A}_{n}
we find

\displaystyle\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}=\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}\,.

(58)

Combining (
57
) with (
56
) and
(
58
), we now find

\displaystyle\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}=\int_{E\times F}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(g\circ\pi_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}

for all
C\in{\cal C}_{n}
. In other words,
Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F}
is indeed a version of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}}(g\circ\pi_{E}|{\cal C}_{n})
.
For all
x\in E
and

y\in F\setminus N_{\mathrm{mart}}
the martingale convergence (
54
) thus shows

\displaystyle Z_{n}(y)=Z_{n}\circ\pi_{F}(x,y)\to Z_{\infty}\circ\pi_{F}(x,y)=Z_{\infty}(y)\,.

Let us define
N_{g}:=N_{\mathrm{trafo}}\cup N_{\mathrm{mart}}\cup N^{(2)}_{\infty}\cup\bigcup_{n\geq 1}N^{(2)}_{n}
.
Clearly, this definition gives
N_{g}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{g})=0
, see the calculation (
52
).
For
y\in F\setminus N_{g}
our considerations (
55
) and (
51
) further give

\displaystyle g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(\,\cdot\,|y),G)}={{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)),G)}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
as well as

\displaystyle\int_{E}\!g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}\bigl(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y)\bigr)=\int_{E}\!g(x)\,\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(\mathrm{d}x|y)=Z_{n}(y)\to Z_{\infty}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\!g(x)\,\mathrm{Q}^{(\infty)}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}\!g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

for
n\to\infty
, where the convergence is with respect to the norm
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{G}
.
In other words we have shown both (
49
) and (
50
).

Let us finally establish the uniform tightness. To this end, we first note that
\{\mathrm{P}_{X}\}
is uniformly tight
as discussed around (
C.8
),
and hence Lemma
C.8

gives a
\mathfrak{B}(E)
-measurable
V:E\to[0,\infty]
such that
\{V\leq c\}
is compact for all
c\in[0,\infty)
and
V\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X})}
.
Consequently,
N_{\mathrm{tight}}:=\{V=\infty\}\in\mathfrak{B}(E)
satisfies
\mathrm{P}_{X}(N_{\mathrm{tight}})=0
.
We write
G:=\mathbb{R}
and
g:=\boldsymbol{1}_{E\setminus N_{\mathrm{tight}}}V
, where we use the convention
0\cdot\infty:=0
.
Then we have
g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X},\mathbb{R})}
, and therefore we already know that there
exists an
N_{g}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{g})=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N_{g}
we have

g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y),\mathbb{R})}
and both (
49
) and (
50
) hold true.
Consequently, there exists an
n_{0}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\int_{E}g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))\leq 1+\int_{E}g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

for all
n\geq n_{0}
and all
y\in F\setminus N_{g}
. By applying (
49
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{E}g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))<\infty

(59)

for all
y\in F\setminus N_{g}
. Now note that

\{g\leq c\}
may not be compact, and therefore we cannot apply
Lemma
C.8
directly. For this reason, we
translate (
59
) back to the function
V
. To this end, we note that we have

\displaystyle 0=\mathrm{P}_{X}(N_{\mathrm{tight}})=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}(N_{\mathrm{tight}}\times F)=\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(N_{\mathrm{tight}}\times F)=\int_{F}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(N_{\mathrm{tight}}|y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{F}(y)\,.

Consequently, there exists an
N_{n}^{(3)}\in{\cal A}_{n}
with
\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}_{F}(N_{n}^{(3)})=0
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(N_{\mathrm{tight}}|y)=0\,,\qquad\qquad y\in F\setminus N_{n}^{(3)}.

Repeating the calculation (
52
) then shows
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{n}^{(3)})=0
. We define

\displaystyle N:=N_{g}\cup\bigcup_{n\geq 1}(N_{n}^{(1)}\cup N_{n}^{(3)})\,.

This gives
N\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
and for
y\in F\setminus N
we have both (
59
)
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(N_{\mathrm{tight}}|A_{n}(y))=\mathrm{Q}^{(n)}(N_{\mathrm{tight}}|y)=0\,.

Moreover, we have
N_{\mathrm{tight}}=\{V=\infty\}=\{V\neq g\}
by the definition of
g
.
The previous identity thus implies

\displaystyle\int_{E}V(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))=\int_{E}g(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))

for all
y\in F\setminus N
.
Inserting this into (
59
) then gives the uniform tightness by Lemma
C.8
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

In the following, we sequentially prove all parts of Theorem
3.3
. To this end,
we construct for each part
k)
an

N^{k)}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N^{k)})=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N^{k)}
the claims of the parts
i)
to
k)
hold true.
The desired
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero set
N
is then given by
N:=N^{viii)}
.

i).
Since
(\id_{E},A_{n}):E\times F\to E\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a bounded and linear operator we quickly see that

(X,Y_{n})
is a Gaussian random variable. For each
n\geq 1
,
Theorem
3.1
then gives an
N_{n}^{(1)}\in\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y_{n}}(N_{n}^{(1)})=0
such for all

y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N_{n}^{(1)}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|y_{n})={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(y_{n}),C_{X|Y_{n}}\bigr)\,,

(60)

where
\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(y_{n})\in E
and
C_{X|Y_{n}}:E^{\prime}\to E
are described in Theorem
3.1
.
We define

\displaystyle N^{i)}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}A_{n}^{-1}(N_{n}^{(1)})\,.

Note that we have
A_{n}^{-1}(N_{n}^{(1)})\in\sigma(A_{n})\subset\sigma(F^{\prime})
, and
Y_{n}=A_{n}\circ Y
shows

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(A_{n}^{-1}(N_{n}^{(1)}))=\mathrm{P}_{Y_{n}}(N_{n}^{(1)})=0
. Consequently, we have
N^{i)}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N^{i)})=0
.
Moreover, for
y\in F\setminus N^{i)}
we have
y_{n}:=A_{n}(y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus N_{n}^{(1)}
and therefore, the claims follow from
(
60
).

ii).

We first consider the case
E=\mathbb{R}
. Here, we fix an enumeration
(t_{m})_{m\geq 3}
of
\mathbb{Q}
and consider the functions

g_{m}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

\displaystyle g_{m}(x):=\exp(\mathrm{i}t_{m}x)\,,\qquad\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}

as well as the two functions
g_{1},g_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
g_{i}(x):=x^{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Let
G:=\mathbb{C}
with
\mathbb{C}
being interpreted as the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since for
m\geq 3

the function
g_{m}
is bounded and continuous, it is
\mathrm{P}_{X}
-Bochner integrable.
For each
m\geq 3
Theorem
6.3
thus
gives an
N_{m}^{(2)}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{m}^{(2)})=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N_{m}^{(2)}
we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))}(t_{m})=\int_{E}g_{m}(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))

\displaystyle\to\int_{E}g_{m}(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)}(t_{m})\,.

In addition, for
i=1,2
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g_{i}|\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}(x)<\infty

since
\mathrm{P}_{X}
is a Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Consequently,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are also
\mathrm{P}_{X}
-Bochner integrable, and therefore Theorem
6.3

shows that there exist
N_{1}^{(2)},N_{2}^{(2)}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{i}^{(2)})=0
such that for
i=1,2
and all
y\in F\setminus N_{i}^{(2)}
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{n,y}^{(i)}:=\int_{E}x^{i}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))\to\int_{E}x^{i}\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)=:\mu_{\infty,y}^{(i)}\,.

In other words, the first two raw moments
\mu_{n,y}^{(i)}
of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))

converge to the first two raw moments
\mu_{\infty,y}^{(i)}
of
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
.
Now, the first raw moments are, of course, the expectations. Since the variances can be computed by the first and second raw moments, we further obtain

\displaystyle\sigma_{n,y}^{2}:=\var\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))

\displaystyle\to\var\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)=:\sigma_{\infty,y}^{2}\,.

Let us define
N^{\prime}:=N^{i)}\cup\bigcup_{m\geq 1}N_{m}^{(2)}
. This implies
N^{\prime}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N^{\prime})=0
and

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))}(t)\to\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)}(t)

for all
t\in\mathbb{Q}
and
y\in F\setminus N^{\prime}
, and since
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))={\cal N}(\mu_{n,y}^{(1)},\sigma_{n,y}^{2})
for such
y
we find

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\mu_{n,y}^{(1)}}\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{t^{2}\sigma_{n,y}^{2}}{2}\biggr)\to\varphi_{{\cal N}(\mu_{\infty,y},\sigma_{\infty,y}^{2})}(t)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
y\in F\setminus N^{\prime}
. Consequently, for each fixed
y\in F\setminus N^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)}(t)=\varphi_{{\cal N}(\mu_{\infty,y},\sigma_{\infty,y}^{2})}(t)

(61)

for all
t\in\mathbb{Q}
, and since both characteristic functions are continuous, we conclude that (
61
)
actually holds for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. This shows that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is a Gaussian measure.

Let us now consider the case of a general separable Banach space
E
. Here we fix a countable

\mathfrak{D}_{E^{\prime}}\subset B_{E^{\prime}}
that is
w^{*}
-dense in
B_{E^{\prime}}
and an enumeration
\mathfrak{D}_{E^{\prime}}=\{x_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}
.
We write
X_{i}:=x_{i}^{\prime}\circ X
.
Our considerations in the case
E=\mathbb{R}

then show that for each
i\geq 1
there exists an
N_{i}^{\prime}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{i}^{\prime})=0
such that

\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is a Gaussian measure
for all
y\in F\setminus N_{i}^{\prime}
.

As in the proof of Theorem
3.1
we now translate this result to

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
with the help of Theorem
D.2
. To this end,
we set
T_{0}:=E
and
T_{1}:=\mathbb{R}
, as well as
U_{0}:=U_{1}:=F
and
{\cal A}_{0}:={\cal A}_{1}:=\sigma(F^{\prime})
.
Moreover, we consider the maps
\Phi:=x_{i}^{\prime}
and
\Psi:=\id_{F}
as well as the measures

\mathrm{P}^{(0)}:=\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}
and
\mathrm{P}^{(1)}:=\mathrm{P}_{(X_{i},Y)}=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{(\Phi,\Psi)}
.
By Theorem
D.2
there then
exists an
N_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with

\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{i}^{\prime\prime})=0
such that for all
y\in F\setminus N_{i}^{\prime\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}\bigl((x_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}(B)|y\bigr)=\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(B|y)\,,\qquad\qquad B\in\mathfrak{B}.

Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\bigr)_{x^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathrm{P}_{X_{i}|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)

for all
y\in F\setminus N_{i}^{\prime\prime}
.
Setting

\displaystyle N^{ii)}:=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(N_{i}^{\prime}\cup N_{i}^{\prime\prime})

we find

N^{ii)}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N^{ii)})=0
and our construction ensures that

(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))_{x^{\prime}_{i}}
is a Gaussian measure for all
i\geq 1
and all
y\in F\setminus N^{ii)}
.
Theorem
C.4
thus shows that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is Gaussian for
y\in F\setminus N^{ii)}
.

iii).
Let us consider the map
g:=\id_{E}
. Since
E
is separable,
g
is strongly measurable. Moreover,
since
X
is a Gaussian random variable, Fernique’s theorem, see (
9
), ensures

\displaystyle\int_{E}\|g\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}=\int_{E}\|x\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}(x)<\infty\,,

that is,
g:E\to E
is
\mathrm{P}_{X}
-Bochner integrable.
Therefore, Theorem
6.3
shows that there exists an
N_{g}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{g})=0

such that for all
y\in F\setminus(N^{ii)}\cup N_{g})
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(A_{n}(y))=\int_{E}x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))\to\int_{E}x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)=\mu_{X|Y}(y)\,.

Setting
N^{iii)}:=N^{ii)}\cup N_{g}
thus shows
iii)
.

iv).

Let us consider the maps
h:E\to[0,\infty)
and
g:E\to\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E)
defined by

\displaystyle h(x):=\|x\|_{E}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle g(x):=x\otimes x\,,

where
the tensor products as well as the space of nuclear operators
\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E)

are discussed in front of Lemma
B.7
and Lemma
B.5
, respectively.
In addition, we consider
the probability space

(E,\mathfrak{B}(E),\mathrm{P}_{X})
and the function

\tilde{X}:=\id_{E}
. Because
E
is separable,
\tilde{X}
is
strongly measurable. In addition, since
\mathrm{P}_{X}
is a Gaussian measure, Fernique’s theorem, see (
9
), shows

\displaystyle\int_{E}\|\id_{E}\|^{2}_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}=\int_{E}\|x\|_{E}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}(x)<\infty\,,

and hence we find
\tilde{X}\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P}_{X},E)}
.
Consequently, Lemma
B.7
shows that the
\mathfrak{N}(E,E)
-valued function

g=\tilde{X}\otimes\tilde{X}
is also strongly measurable
with
g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E))}
.
Therefore, Theorem
6.3
applied with
G:=\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E)

shows that there exists an
N_{g}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{g})=0

such that for all
y\in F\setminus N_{g}
we have

\displaystyle g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y),G)}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad g\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)),G)}

for all
n\geq 1
as well as the
\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E)
-convergence

\displaystyle\int_{E}x\otimes x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))

\displaystyle\to\int_{E}x\otimes x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)\,.

(62)

Moreover, Theorem
6.3
applied to
h=\|\tilde{X}\|_{E}^{2}
with
G:=\mathbb{R}

gives an
N_{h}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{h})=0

such that for all
y\in F\setminus N_{h}
we have

\displaystyle\tilde{X}\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\tilde{X}\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)))}

for all
n\geq 1
.
We define
N^{iv)}:=N^{iii)}\cup N_{g}\cup N_{h}
.
For
y\in F\setminus N^{iv)}
, Lemma
B.7
applied to
\tilde{X}\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)))}
thus shows

\displaystyle C_{X|Y_{n}}

\displaystyle=\cov\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))

\displaystyle=\int_{E}(\tilde{X}\otimes\tilde{X})(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))

\displaystyle\quad-\biggl(\int_{E}\tilde{X}(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))\biggr)\otimes\biggl(\int_{E}\tilde{X}(x)\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))\biggr)

\displaystyle=\int_{E}x\otimes x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\mathrm{d}x|A_{n}(y))-\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(A_{n}(y))\otimes\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(A_{n}(y))

\displaystyle\to\int_{E}x\otimes x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)-\mu_{X|Y}(y)\otimes\mu_{X|Y}(y)

with convergence in
\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E)
, where in the last step we used (
62
) and part
iii)

in combination with Lemma
B.5
.
Moreover, Lemma
B.7
applied to
\tilde{X}\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y))}
also shows

\displaystyle\cov\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)=\int_{E}x\otimes x\,\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\mathrm{d}x|y)-\mu_{X|Y}(y)\otimes\mu_{X|Y}(y)

for all
y\in F\setminus N^{iv)}
. Combining these considerations, we obtain the claimed
C_{X|Y_{n}}\to\cov\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
with convergence in
\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},E)
.
Furthermore, all
C_{X|Y_{n}}
are independent of
y
, and hence so is their limit
\cov\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
, that is, the notation
C_{X|Y}
is indeed justified.

v).
Let us fix a
y\in F\setminus N^{iv)}
.
We then know from
i)
and Proposition
C.7
that

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))}(x^{\prime})=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y_{n}}(A_{n}(y))\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{\langle x^{\prime},C_{X|Y_{n}}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}{2}\biggr)

(63)

holds for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
Moreover, note that
iv)
implies

\displaystyle\bigl|\langle x^{\prime},C_{X|Y_{n}}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\langle x^{\prime},C_{X|Y}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\ \bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl|\langle x^{\prime},(C_{X|Y_{n}}-C_{X|Y})x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|C_{X|Y_{n}}-C_{X|Y}\|\cdot\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}

\displaystyle\to 0\,.

Combining this with

iii)
and (
63
) we thus obtain for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))}(x^{\prime})\to\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}(y)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{\langle x^{\prime},C_{X|Y}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}{2}\biggr)\,.

(64)

Furthermore, by Theorem
6.3
there exists an
N^{\star}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N^{\star})=0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl\{\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y)):n\geq 1\bigr\}

(65)

is uniformly tight for all
y\in F\setminus N^{\star}
. We define
N^{v)}:=N^{iv)}\cup N^{\star}
.
For
y\in F\setminus N^{v)}
we then have both (
64
)
and (
65
) and therefore
Theorem
C.9
shows that there then exists a probability measure
\nu_{y}
on
X
such that

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y_{n}}(\,\cdot\,|A_{n}(y))\to\nu_{y}
weakly and

\displaystyle\varphi_{\nu_{y}}(x^{\prime})=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}(y)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{\langle x^{\prime},C_{X|Y}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}{2}\biggr)\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}\,.

Proposition
C.7
then shows that
\nu_{y}
is a Gaussian measure with mean
\mu_{X|Y}(y)
and covariance operator
C_{X|Y}
.
By
ii)
,
iii)
, and
iv)
we further know that
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is also a
Gaussian measure with mean
\mu_{X|Y}(y)
and covariance operator
C_{X|Y}
. As discussed in front of
(
10
)
we can then conclude that
\nu_{y}=\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
.

vi).
We write
N:=N^{vi)}:=N^{v)}
. Then

N_{\Omega}:=Y^{-1}(N)\in{\cal A}
satisfies
\mathrm{P}(N_{\Omega})=0
since
N
is a
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero set.
For
n\geq 1
we now define the random variable
Z_{n}:\Omega\to E
by

\displaystyle Z_{n}:=\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(Y_{n}-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})\,.

Since for
\omega\in\Omega
and
y:=Y(\omega)
we have
\omega\in N_{\Omega}
if and only if
y\in N
, part
iii)

together with
A_{n}(y)=A_{n}(Y(\omega))=Y_{n}(\omega)

then shows that
the definition of
Z
can be written as

\displaystyle Z(\omega)=\begin{cases}\lim_{n\to\infty}Z_{n}(\omega)\,,&\mbox{ if }\omega\in\Omega\setminus N_{\Omega}\\
0&\mbox{ else.}\end{cases}

Consequently,
Z
as defined in
vi)
is indeed a random variable and
we have the
\mathrm{P}
-almost sure convergence
Z_{n}\to Z
. Moreover, each
Z_{n}
is
\sigma(Y_{n})=\sigma(A_{n}\circ Y)
-measurable and since

N_{\Omega}\in\sigma(Y)
, we conclude that
Z
is actually
\sigma(Y)
-measurable.
In addition,
by Theorem
3.1

each
Z_{n}

is a
version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y_{n})
and a
Gaussian random variable with
\mathbb{E}Z_{n}=\mathbb{E}X
and

\displaystyle\cov Z_{n}

\displaystyle=\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)\,,

\displaystyle{\cal G}(Z_{n})

\displaystyle\subset{\cal G}(Y_{n})\,.

(66)

Now, given an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, we have
\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x^{\prime},Z(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
for all
\omega\in\Omega\setminus N_{\Omega}
, and
therefore Theorem
C.3
shows that
\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued Gaussian random variable with

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{E}\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

(67)

\displaystyle\var\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle\to\var\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

(68)

Now, since all
\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
are Gaussian,
Z
is a Gaussian random variable.
Moreover, we have
\mathbb{E}\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
, and hence we obtain

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\mathbb{E}\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

Since this holds for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
we obtain

\mathbb{E}X=\mathbb{E}Z
.
Similarly, (
B.6
) yields

\displaystyle\var\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},\cov(Z_{n})x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle\to\langle x^{\prime},(\cov(X)-C_{X|Y})x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

where in the last step we used that
iv)
. Together with (
B.6
) and (
68
)
this gives

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},\cov(Z)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\var\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},(\cov(X)-C_{X|Y})x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

and since this holds for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, Lemma
B.2
shows
\cov(Z)=\cov(X)-C_{X|Y}
.

Our next step is to show that
Z
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
. To this end,
we write
{\cal F}_{n}:=\sigma(Y_{n})
and
{\cal F}_{\infty}:=\sigma(Y)
. Then we already know that
Z_{n}
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal F}_{n})
.
Moreover,
Lemma
6.2
shows that

({\cal F}_{n})
is a filtration with

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{\infty}=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}{\cal F}_{n}\biggr)\,.

By the martingale convergence Theorem

D.5
we thus find

\displaystyle Z_{n}\to\mathbb{E}(X|Y)

in
{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
\mathrm{P}
-almost surely. Since we already have the
\mathrm{P}
-almost sure convergence
Z_{n}\to Z
, we conclude that

\mathrm{P}(\{Z=\mathbb{E}(X|Y)\})=1
, and this in turn implies that
Z
is indeed a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
and

Z_{n}\to Z
in
{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
.

vii).
We set
N^{vii)}:=N^{vi)}
and fix
an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
Since
Y_{n}-\mathbb{E}Y_{n}=A_{n}\circ Y-\mathbb{E}(A_{n}\circ Y)=A_{n}\circ(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)
and
A_{n}
is bounded and linear,
Lemma
C.6
shows
{\cal G}(Y_{n})\subset{\cal G}(Y)
.
Combining this with (
66
) yields

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}-\mathbb{E}Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{\cal G}(Z_{n})\subset{\cal G}(Y_{n})\subset{\cal G}(Y)\,.

Moreover,
Z_{n}\to Z
in
{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
implies

\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
in
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
. By
\mathbb{E}Z_{n}=\mathbb{E}X=\mathbb{E}Z
we conclude

\langle x^{\prime},Z_{n}-\mathbb{E}Z_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x^{\prime},Z-\mathbb{E}Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
in
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
.
Since
{\cal G}(Y)

is a closed subspace of
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
by definition, we thus find
\langle x^{\prime},Z-\mathbb{E}Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{\cal G}(Y)
.
Taking the
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
-closure then yields
{\cal G}(Z)\subset{\cal G}(Y)
.

To verify the formula for the orthogonal projection, we
recall that a bounded linear operator on a Hilbert space
is an orthogonal projection if and only if it is idempotent and self-adjoint, see e.g.
[
15
, Proposition II.3.3]
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|Y):{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}\to{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
is known to be an orthogonal projection, where we note that this result is rarely stated
in the formulation above, so we quickly prove it here using well-known properties of conditional expectations, see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 8.14]
.
First,
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|Y)
is idempotent since for
f\in{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(f|Y)|Y)=\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\,.

(69)

Moreover, for
f,g\in{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
we have

\displaystyle\langle f,\mathbb{E}(g|Y)\rangle_{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(f\cdot{\mathbb{E}(g|Y)}\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(f\cdot{\mathbb{E}(g|Y)}|Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl({\mathbb{E}(f|Y)}\cdot{\mathbb{E}(g|Y)}\bigr)\,,

and since we analogously obtain
\langle g,\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\rangle_{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl({\mathbb{E}(f|Y)}\cdot{\mathbb{E}(g|Y)}\bigr)
, we find

\displaystyle\langle f,\mathbb{E}(g|Y)\rangle_{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}=\langle\mathbb{E}(f|Y),g\rangle_{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}\,,

(70)

that is,
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|Y)
is also self-adjoint. Our next goal is to show

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\in{\cal G}(Y)\,,\qquad\qquad f\in{\cal G}(X,Y).

(71)

To this end, we fix
an
f\in{\cal G}(X,Y)
. By the definition of
{\cal G}(X,Y)
, there
then exists a sequence
(f_{n})
of the form
f_{n}=\langle x_{n}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+\langle y_{n}^{\prime},Y\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

with
f_{n}\to f
in
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
, where
x_{n}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y_{n}^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
are suitable functionals.
Now, Theorem
D.4
together with
{\cal G}(Z)\subset{\cal G}(Y)
implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl(\langle x_{n}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}|Y\bigr)=\bigl\langle x_{n}^{\prime},\mathbb{E}(X|Y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{n}^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{\cal G}(Y)\,.

Moreover, since
\langle y_{n}^{\prime},Y\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl(\langle y_{n}^{\prime},Y\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}|Y\bigr)=\langle y_{n}^{\prime},Y\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{\cal G}(Y)\,.

Combining both considerations shows
\mathbb{E}(f_{n}|Y)\in{\cal G}(Y)
for all
n\geq 1
. Since

\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|Y):{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}\to{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
is continuous, we further have the
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
-convergence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(f_{n}|Y)\to\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\,,

and since
{\cal G}(Y)
is a closed subspace of
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
we conclude
\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\in{\cal G}(Y)
.
This finishes the proof of (
71
). Since
{\cal G}(Y)\subset{\cal G}(X,Y)
we
can now consider the operator

\displaystyle\Pi:{\cal G}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\to{\cal G}(X,Y)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{E}(f|Y)\,.

The operator
\Pi
is bounded and linear as it is the restriction of
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|Y)
to
{\cal G}(X,Y)

Moreover, (
69
) shows that
\Pi
is idempotent
and since (
70
) holds for all
f,g\in{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
it also holds for all
f,g\in{\cal G}(X,Y)
,
and hence
\Pi
is self-adjoint. In other words,
\Pi
is an orthogonal projection.

It remains to show
\ran\Pi={\cal G}(Y)
. Here, we note that the inclusion “
\subset
”
has already been established in (
71
). For the proof of the converse inclusion, we pick
an
f\in{\cal G}(Y)
. By Lemma
C.6
we know that the
\mathrm{P}
-equivalence class of
f
contains
a
\sigma(Y)
-measurable representative, so we may assume without loss of generality that
f
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable. This leads to

\displaystyle f=\mathbb{E}(f|Y)=\Pi f\in\ran\Pi\,.

viii).

We fix an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, for which

\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable. Using Theorem
D.4
we then obtain

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}(X|Y)\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigl|Y\bigr)=\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

where the first identity holds up to the
\mathrm{P}
-zero set
Y^{-1}(N^{vii)})\in{\cal A}

and
in the second identity we simply considered the version
Z
of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
. Moreover,
the third and forth identities are meant in the sense that
both
\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}(X|Y)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
and
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
are versions of
\mathbb{E}(\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}|Y)
.
Consequently, the set

\displaystyle N_{x^{\prime}}:=\bigl\{\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\neq\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigr\}

is a
\mathrm{P}
-zero set with
N_{x^{\prime}}\in\sigma(Y)
. Let us now consider the set

\displaystyle{\cal F}:=\bigl\{x^{\prime}\in B_{E^{\prime}}:\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\mbox{ is $\sigma(Y)$-measurable }\bigr\}

as well as a metric
\metric_{w^{*}}
describing the relative
w^{*}
-topology of
B_{E^{\prime}}
. Since
subsets of separable metric spaces are separable, and
(B_{E^{\prime}},\metric_{w^{*}})
is separable by Theorem
A.1
,
we conclude that there exists a countable
{\cal F}_{0}\subset{\cal F}
that is
\metric_{w^{*}}
-dense in
{\cal F}
.
We define

\displaystyle N_{{\cal F}_{0}}:=\bigcup_{x^{\prime}\in{\cal F}_{0}}N_{x^{\prime}}\,.

By construction, we have
N_{{\cal F}_{0}}\in\sigma(Y)
with
\mathrm{P}(N_{{\cal F}_{0}})=0
. Moreover,
N_{{\cal F}_{0}}\in\sigma(Y)
gives an

N_{{\cal F}_{0}}^{\prime}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
Y^{-1}(N_{{\cal F}_{0}}^{\prime})=N_{{\cal F}_{0}}
and
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{{\cal F}_{0}}^{\prime})=0
. We define

N^{viii)}:=N^{vii)}\cup N_{{\cal F}_{0}}^{\prime}
. By construction, we then have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\mu_{X|Y}(Y(\omega))\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},X(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in{\cal F}_{0},\omega\not\in Y^{-1}(N^{viii)})\,.

Since
{\cal F}_{0}
is
\metric_{w^{*}}
-dense in
{\cal F}
, we then find the first assertion for all
x^{\prime}\in{\cal F}
, and a simple rescaling argument
then gives the desired identity in the general case.

Finally, by (
B.6
), part
vi)
, Theorem
D.4
, and the assumed
\sigma
-measurability we obtain

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},\cov(Z)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\var(\langle x^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E})=\var\bigl(\langle x^{\prime},\mathbb{E}(X|Y)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\var\bigl(\mathbb{E}(\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}|Y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\var\bigl(\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

Using the identity
C_{X|Y}=\cov(X)-\cov(Z)
established in
vi)
, we then find

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},C_{X|Y}x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\bigl\langle x^{\prime},\cov(Z)x^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=0\,.

By (
B.9
) this implies
C_{X|Y}x^{\prime}=0
as discussed there.
∎

6.3
Proofs of Theorems
3.4
and
3.5

In this subsection we present the proofs of the remaining two theorems of Section
3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
.

In the following we write
C_{X|Y}:=\cov(X)-\cov(\mu\circ Y)
and
verify the three conditions of Definition
2.2
.

We begin by observing that

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)
is, of course, a probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
for every
y\in F
by our construction (
15
).

To verify the measurability condition
ii)
, we define

\displaystyle{\cal E}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}}\sigma(x^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle{\cal B}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{B\in\sigma(E^{\prime}):y\mapsto\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B|y)\mbox{ is measurable }\bigr\}\,.

We first show that
{\cal E}\subset{\cal B}
. To this end, we choose a
B\in{\cal E}
. Then there exists an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and an
A\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
B=(x^{\prime})^{-1}(A)
.
For fixed
y\in F
we then obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu(y),C_{X|Y}\bigr)\bigl((x^{\prime})^{-1}(A)\bigr)=\Bigl({\cal N}\bigl(\mu(y),C_{X|Y}\bigr)\Bigr)_{x^{\prime}}(A)={\cal N}(m(y),\sigma^{2})(A)\,,

where we have set
m(y):=\langle x^{\prime},\mu(y)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
and
\sigma^{2}
denotes the
1\times 1
-matrix representing the linear operator
x^{\prime}\circ C_{X|Y}\circ(x^{\prime})^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Note the map
y\mapsto m(y)
is
(\sigma(F^{\prime}),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable since
\mu
is
(\sigma(F^{\prime}),\sigma(E^{\prime}))
-measurable and
x^{\prime}
is
(\sigma(E^{\prime}),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable.

It remains to verify the measurability of the map
y\mapsto{\cal N}(m(y),\sigma^{2})(A)
. In the case
\sigma^{2}=0
this measurability immediately follows from

\displaystyle{\cal N}\bigl(m(y),\sigma^{2}\bigr)(A)=\delta_{\{m(y)\}}(A)=\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(m(y))

and the measurability of
m
and
A
. Moreover, in the case
\sigma^{2}>0
we have

\displaystyle{\cal N}(m(y),\sigma^{2})(A)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(t)\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(t-m(y))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\,.

Now, the integrand
f(t,y):=\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(t)\exp\bigl(-\frac{(t-m(y))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\bigr)
is a non-negative
(\mathfrak{B}\otimes\sigma(F^{\prime}),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable function,
and therefore Fubini-Tonelli’s theorem, see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 14.16]
, yields the measurability of
y\mapsto{\cal N}(m(y),\sigma^{2})(A)
.
In both cases we have thus found
B\in{\cal B}
, and hence we have established
{\cal E}\subset{\cal B}
.

Let us now show that
{\cal B}
is a
\sigma
-algebra. Here,
\emptyset\in{\cal B}
is obvious since
y\mapsto\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\emptyset|y)=0
for all
y\in F
.
Moreover, if we have a
B\in{\cal B}
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(E\setminus B|y)=1-\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B|y)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in F

quickly shows that
E\setminus B\in{\cal B}
. Similarly, if we have mutually disjoint
B_{1},B_{2},\dots\in{\cal B}
and we set
B:=B_{1}\cup B_{2}\cup\dots
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B|y)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B_{i}|y)\,,\qquad\qquad y\in F

and since each
y\mapsto\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(B_{i}|y)
is measurable, we conclude that
B\in{\cal B}
.

With these preparations we obtain
\sigma({\cal E})\subset\sigma({\cal B})={\cal B}\subset\sigma(E^{\prime})
, and since we also have
\sigma({\cal E})=\sigma(E^{\prime})
, we conclude

{\cal B}=\sigma(E^{\prime})=\mathfrak{B}(E)
. This shows condition
ii)
of Definition
2.2
.

Let us finally verify condition
iii)
of Definition
2.2
. To this end, we fix an arbitrary version

\tilde{\mathrm{P}}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y
and an
N\in\sigma(F^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0
according to
Theorem
3.3
. We define
\mu_{X|Y}:F\to E
by (
14
) and write

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}:=\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y\,.

Then part
vi)
of Theorem
3.3
shows that
\tilde{Z}
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
, and hence we have

\displaystyle 0=\mathrm{P}(\{Z\neq\tilde{Z}\})=\mathrm{P}(\{\mu\circ Y\neq\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y\})=\mathrm{P}\bigl(Y^{-1}(\{\mu\neq\mu_{X|Y}\})\bigr)=\mathrm{P}_{Y}(\{\mu\neq\mu_{X|Y}\})\,,

where in the first step we used the almost sure uniqueness of conditional expectations in the Banach space valued case, which is discussed
after Definition
D.3
.
In other words, the set
N_{1}:=\{\mu\neq\mu_{X|Y}\}
satisfies

N_{1}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})=\mathfrak{B}(F)
and
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N_{1})=0
. For the
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero set
N_{2}:=N\cup N_{1}
and
y\in F\setminus N_{2}
we then have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu(y),C_{X|Y}\bigr)={\cal N}\bigl(\mu_{X|Y}(y),C_{X|Y}\bigr)=\tilde{\mathrm{P}}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|y)\,.

For
B\in\mathfrak{B}(E)
and
A\in\mathfrak{B}(F)
we thus find

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{(X,Y)}(B\times A)=\int_{F}\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(y)\tilde{\mathrm{P}}(B|y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)=\int_{F}\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(y)\mathrm{P}(B|y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{Y}(y)\,,

i.e. we have verified condition
iii)
of Definition
2.2
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Let
B_{n}:F\to E
be the bounded linear operator given by

\displaystyle B_{n}:=\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}A_{n}\,.

Since
\mathbb{E}Y_{n}=A_{n}(\mathbb{E}Y)
, we then find

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}=\bigl\{y\in F:\exists\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{n}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\}\,.

We now pick a version
\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)

of the regular conditional probability of
X
given
Y
and a
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero set
N\in\sigma(F^{\prime})

according to Theorem
3.3
. In addition, we define
\mu_{X|Y}:F\to E

with the help of part
iii)
of Theorem
3.3
and (
14
).
Note that part
iii)
of Theorem
3.3
shows
F\setminus N\subset F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
.

i).
Since
E
is complete, we have

\displaystyle F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle=\bigl\{y\in F:(B_{n}(y-\mathbb{E}Y))\mbox{ is Cauchy sequence in $E$}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\bigcup_{n_{0}\geq 1}\bigcap_{n,m\geq n_{0}}\bigl\{y\in F:\|B_{n}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)-B_{m}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\|_{E}\leq 1/k\bigr\}\,.

By the continuity of the map
y\mapsto\|B_{n}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)-B_{m}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\|_{E}
, we further see that

\displaystyle\bigl\{y\in F:\|B_{n}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)-B_{m}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\|_{E}\leq 1/k\bigr\}\in\mathfrak{B}(F)

for all
k,n,m\in\mathbb{N}
. Combining both observations yields
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}\in\mathfrak{B}(F)=\sigma(F^{\prime})
.

For the proof of
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}})=1
we simply combine the already observed inclusion

F\setminus N\subset F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
with
\mathrm{P}_{Y}(F\setminus N)=1
.

ii).
We first note that
B_{n}(0)=0
shows
\mathbb{E}Y\in F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
, and hence
0\in F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
.
Moreover, for
y_{1},y_{2}\in F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

we have
\tilde{y}_{i}:=y_{i}+\mathbb{E}Y\in F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
and

\displaystyle B_{n}\bigl((\alpha_{1}y_{1}+\alpha_{2}y_{2}+\mathbb{E}Y)-\mathbb{E}Y\bigr)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}B_{n}y_{1}+\alpha_{2}B_{n}y_{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}B_{n}(\tilde{y}_{1}-\mathbb{E}Y)+\alpha_{2}B_{n}(\tilde{y}_{2}-\mathbb{E}Y)\,.

From this we easily conclude that
\alpha_{1}y_{1}+\alpha_{2}y_{2}+\mathbb{E}Y\in F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
, that is
\alpha_{1}y_{1}+\alpha_{2}y_{2}\in F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
.
Finally,
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}\in\sigma(F^{\prime})=\mathfrak{B}(F)
implies
F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\in\mathfrak{B}(F)=\sigma(F^{\prime})
since translations
F\to F

are continuous and thus
(\mathfrak{B}(F),\mathfrak{B}(F))
-measurable.

Let us now show
\ran\cov(Y,X)\subset F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
.
To this end, we fix an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and set
y:=\cov(Y,X)(x^{\prime})
.
This gives
A_{n}y=(A_{n}\circ\cov(Y,X))(x^{\prime})=\cov(Y_{n},X)(x^{\prime})
and
by part
iv)
of Theorem
3.3

we know that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl(\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)\bigr)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl(\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\bigr)(A_{n}y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}B_{n}y

exists. In other words, we have
y+\mathbb{E}Y\in F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
, and hence
y\in-\mathbb{E}Y+F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}=F_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
.

iii).
The definition of
\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}:F\to E
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}(y)=\mathbb{E}X+\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}}(y)\cdot B_{n}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,.

In particular,
\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}
is the pointwise limit of a sequence of strongly measurable functions
F\to E
, and hence also strongly measurable.
Moreover, our construction ensures
F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}\cap F\setminus N\subset\{\mu_{X|Y}=\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\}
, and since we already know

F\setminus N\subset F_{\mathrm{conv},\mathfrak{A}}
we conclude that
\{\mu_{X|Y}\neq\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\}\subset N
.
This shows

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\bigl(\{\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y\neq\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\circ Y\}\bigr)=\mathrm{P}_{Y}\bigl(\{\mu_{X|Y}\neq\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\}\bigr)\leq\mathrm{P}_{Y}(N)=0\,.

Since
\mu_{X|Y}\circ Y
is a version of

\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
by part
iv)
of Theorem
3.3
, we conclude that
\mu_{X|Y,\mathfrak{A}}\circ Y
is also a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|Y)
.

iv).
This is a direct consequence of
iii)
and Theorem
3.4
.
∎

7
Proofs Related to Filtering Sequences in Section
4

This section contains all proofs for the results of Section
4
as well as some
auxiliary results.

We begin by investigating
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by some bounded linear
A:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
To this end,
let
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
be the standard ONB of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We then write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{A}:=\bigl\{a_{i}:F\to\mathbb{R}\,\bigl|\,a_{i}:=\langle e_{i},A\rangle,i=1,\dots,n\bigr\}

for the set of its coordinate functions
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
. Clearly, we have
\mathfrak{C}_{A}\subset F^{\prime}
.
To relate
\sigma(A)
with
\mathfrak{C}_{A}
we further need to recall that
the annihilator of a non-empty
B\subset F
is defined by

\displaystyle B^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}

\displaystyle:=\bigl\{y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}:\langle y^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=0\mbox{ for all }y\in B\bigr\}\,.

Analogously, the annihilator
of a non-empty set
B\subset F^{\prime}
is defined by

\displaystyle B_{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}:=\bigl\{y\in F:\langle y^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=0\mbox{ for all }y^{\prime}\in B\bigr\}\,.

With these preparations, our next intermediate result reads as follows.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
F
be a Banach space and
A:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded linear operator. Then the following statements hold true:

i)

We have
\sigma(A)=\sigma(\mathfrak{C}_{A})=\sigma(\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A})
.

ii)

We have
\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A}=\ran A^{\prime}=(\ker A)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

i).
We first note that in view of Lemma
A.3
we only need to show
\sigma(A)=\sigma(\mathfrak{C}_{A})
.
To establish this identity, let

a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}:F\to\mathbb{R}
be the coordinate functions of
A
, that is
\mathfrak{C}_{A}=\{a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}\}
.
Then,
[
34
, Theorem 1.90]

shows that for a given
\sigma
-algebra
{\cal A}^{\prime}
on
F
,
the map
A:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
({\cal A}^{\prime},\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable if and only if all
a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}
are

({\cal A}^{\prime},\mathfrak{B})
-measurable.

If we now consider
{\cal A}^{\prime}:=\sigma(A)
, then
A
is

({\cal A}^{\prime},\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable, and hence
we conclude that
\sigma(a_{i}^{\prime})\subset{\cal A}^{\prime}=\sigma(A)
for all
i=1,\dots,n
. This gives the inclusion

\sigma(a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime})\subset\sigma(A)
.
Conversely, for
{\cal A}^{\prime}:=\sigma(a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime})
, we know that
all
a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime}
are

({\cal A}^{\prime},\mathfrak{B})
-measurable, and hence
A
is
{\cal A}^{\prime}
-measurable, that is

\sigma(A)\subset\sigma(a_{1}^{\prime},\dots,a_{n}^{\prime})
.

ii).
We first note that
A
is given by
A=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle a_{i}^{\prime},\,\cdot\,\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}e_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{\prime}\otimes e_{i}
,
see (
B.14
), where
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
is the standard ONB of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
By
(
B.16
) its adjoint
A^{\prime}
is thus given by

\displaystyle A^{\prime}t=\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\otimes a_{i}^{\prime}\biggr)(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle e_{i},t\rangle\cdot a_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}a_{i}^{\prime}\,,\qquad\qquad t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

where we use the canonical identification of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with both
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime\prime}
. This shows
\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A}=\ran A^{\prime}
.

For the proof of the second identity, we note that
the linearity and continuity of
A
gives

\displaystyle(\ker A)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=\bigl((\ran A^{\prime})_{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}\bigr)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=\overline{\ran A^{\prime}}^{w^{*}}\,,

(72)

where the first identity can be found in e.g.
[
39
, Lemma 3.1.16]
or
[
15
, Proposition VI.1.8]
,
and the second identity can be found in
e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 2.6.6]
. Moreover, since the subspace
\ran A^{\prime}
of
F^{\prime}
is finite dimensional, it is
w^{*}
-closed, see e.g.
[
39
, Corollary 2.2.32]
or
[
49
, Theorem 1.21]
. Combining the latter with
(
72
) yields
(\ker A)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=\ran A^{\prime}
.
∎

The next proposition provides a general sufficient condition for filtering sequences. It will be at the core of the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
F
be a separable Banach space and
(A_{n})
be a sequence of bounded linear operators
A_{n}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then,
(A_{n})
is a filtering sequence for
F
if we have both
\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\subset\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n+1}}
for all
n\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}}^{w^{*}}=F^{\prime}\,.

(73)

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

By Lemma
7.1
we find

\displaystyle\sigma(A_{n})=\sigma(\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}})\subset\sigma(\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n+1}})=\sigma(A_{n+1})\,.

To verify the condition
\sigma(F^{\prime})=\sigma\bigl(A_{n}:n\geq 1\bigr)
, we write
F_{n}^{\prime}:=\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}

and

\displaystyle F_{\infty}^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}F_{n}^{\prime}\,.

Since
F_{n}^{\prime}\subset F_{n+1}^{\prime}
for all
n\geq 1
, we quickly see that
F_{\infty}^{\prime}
is a subspace of
F^{\prime}
. Using our assumption and
Lemma

A.3
we further have

\displaystyle\sigma(F^{\prime})=\sigma(\overline{F_{\infty}^{\prime}}^{w^{*}})=\sigma(F_{\infty}^{\prime})=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{y^{\prime}\in F_{\infty}^{\prime}}\!\sigma(y^{\prime})\!\biggr)=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\bigcup_{y^{\prime}\in F_{n}^{\prime}}\!\sigma(y^{\prime})\!\biggr)

\displaystyle\subset\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\sigma(F_{n}^{\prime})\!\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sigma\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\sigma(A_{n})\!\biggr),

where in the last step we used the already observed
\sigma(F_{n}^{\prime})=\sigma(\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}})=\sigma(A_{n})
.
Hence we have found
\sigma(F^{\prime})\subset\sigma\bigl(A_{n}:n\geq 1\bigr)
. The converse inclusion is trivial.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Our construction gives
\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}=\{y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{n}^{\prime}\}
.
This identity implies
\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\subset\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n+1}}
for all
n\geq 1
as well as

\displaystyle\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\,.

Applying Proposition
7.2
then yields the assertion.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

We will apply Proposition
4.1
.
To verify (
19
) we pick a
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality we may assume
y^{\prime}\neq 0
.
We define
y_{\infty}^{\prime}:=\|y^{\prime}\|^{-1}y^{\prime}
.
Since
y_{\infty}^{\prime}\in B_{F^{\prime}}
and the relative
w^{*}
-topology of
B_{E^{\prime}}
is metrizable, see Theorem
A.1
, there then exists a sequence

(y_{i_{k}}^{\prime})\subset\mathfrak{D}_{F^{\prime}}
with
y_{i_{k}}^{\prime}\to y_{\infty}^{\prime}
in the
w^{*}
-topology. This shows

\displaystyle y_{\infty}^{\prime}\in\overline{\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{w^{*}}\,,

and rescaling gives the inclusion “
\supset
” in (
19
). The converse inclusion
is trivially satisfied.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

For
n\geq 1
we
define
A_{n}:F\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by (
20
). This definition ensures

\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\subset\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n+1}}
for all
n\geq 1
and hence we have

\displaystyle\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\,.

Moreover, by Lemma
7.1
we know
\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}=(\ker{A_{n}})^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}
, and hence we find

\displaystyle\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\biggr)_{\!{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}(\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}})_{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\bigl((\ker A_{n})^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}\bigr)_{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\overline{\ker A_{n}}^{\|\cdot\|_{F}}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\ker A_{n},

where the first identity almost directly follows from the definition of annihilators, and the third identity can be found in e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 1.10.15]
.
We thus obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{w^{*}}=\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}}^{w^{*}}=\biggl(\biggl(\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\spann\mathfrak{C}_{A_{n}}\biggr)_{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}\biggr)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=\biggl(\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\ker A_{n}\biggr)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}\,,

where
the second identity can be found in e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 2.6.6]
.
Moreover, we have
A_{n}(y)=0
if and only if
\langle y_{1}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=\dots=\langle y_{n}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=0
. In other words,
the identity
\ker A_{n}=\ker y_{1}^{\prime}\cap\dots\cap\ker y_{n}^{\prime}
holds. Combining this with our previous considerations yields

\displaystyle\overline{\spann\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}}^{w^{*}}=\biggl(\bigcap_{i\geq 1}\ker y_{i}^{\prime}\biggr)^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}\,.

Finally note that

\{y_{i}^{\prime}:i\geq 1\}\subset F^{\prime}
is separating if and only if we have

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i\geq 1}\ker y_{i}^{\prime}=\{0\}\,.

Now the equivalence follows from the well-known fact that
\{0\}\subset F
is the only non-empty subset
B\subset F

with
B^{\perp\hskip-3.31529pt\perp}=F^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.4
.

Let us identify
H
with
H^{\prime}
via the canonical and isometric Fréchet-Riesz
isomorphism
h\mapsto\hat{h}:=\langle h,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{H}
. Then
(\hat{e}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an ONB of
H^{\prime}
, and hence the assertion follows from
the discussion around (
21
) and
Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

We begin by verifying that
(\delta_{t_{i}})\subset F^{\prime}
is separating. To this end, let us fix an
f\in F
with
f\neq 0
.
Then there exists a
t\in T
with
f(t)\neq 0
and then a sequence
(t_{i_{k}})\subset T_{\infty}
with
\metric(t_{i_{k}},t)\to 0
.
This gives
f(t_{i_{k}})\to f(t)
and hence there exists an
i_{k}
with
\langle\delta_{t_{i_{k}}},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=f(t_{i_{k}})\neq 0
.
In other words,
(\delta_{t_{i}})\subset F^{\prime}
is separating.
Applying Proposition

4.3

and then Proposition
4.1
thus gives the assertions.
∎

Proof of Corollary
4.7
.

The separability can be found in e.g.
[
51
, Lemma 4.33]
.

Our next goal is to show that each
h\in H
is continuous. To this end, let

\Phi:T\to H
be the “canonical feature map” given by
\Phi(t):=k(t,\,\cdot\,)
. Since
k
is continuous,
\Phi
is also continuous, see
e.g.
[
51
, Lemma 4.29]
. For
t\in T
and
(t_{n})\subset T
with
t_{n}\to t
, we thus find

h(t_{n})=\langle\Phi(t_{n}),h\rangle_{H}\to\langle\Phi(t),h\rangle_{H}=h(t)
by the reproducing property of
k
.

Now, Theorem
4.5
in combination with the reproducing property gives the assertion.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.8
.

We first show the existence of a countable
\metric_{k}
-dense
T_{\infty}\subset T
. To this end, recall that subsets of separable metric spaces are separable, and hence the set

\displaystyle K_{T}:=\bigl\{k(t,\,\cdot\,):t\in T\bigr\}

is
\|\cdot\|_{H}
-separable. Consequently, there exists a countable
T_{\infty}\subset T
such that

\displaystyle K_{0}:=\bigl\{k(t,\,\cdot\,):t\in T_{\infty}\bigr\}

is
\|\cdot\|_{H}
-dense in
K_{T}
. For a fixed
t\in T
we thus find a sequence

(t_{n})\subset T_{\infty}
with
k(t_{n},\,\cdot\,)\to k(t,\,\cdot\,)
in
H
. The definition of
\metric_{k}
then gives
\metric_{k}(t,t_{n})\to 0
,
i.e.
T_{\infty}
is
\metric_{k}
-dense in
T
.

For the proof of the second assertion, we first note that for
h\in H
and
t,t^{\prime}\in T
the reproducing property of
k
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yield

\displaystyle|h(t)-h(t^{\prime})|=\bigl|\langle h,k(t,\,\cdot\,)-k(t^{\prime},\,\cdot\,)\rangle_{H}\bigr|\leq\|h\|_{H}\cdot\|k(t,\,\cdot\,)-k(t^{\prime},\,\cdot\,)\|_{H}=\|h\|_{H}\cdot\metric_{k}(t,t^{\prime})\,.

Consequently, every
h\in H
is
\metric_{k}
-continuous.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.9
.

Since
F
is separable, Theorem
A.1
shows that
there exists a metric
\metric_{w^{*}}
on
B_{F^{\prime}}
that metrizes the relative
w^{*}
-topology of
B_{F^{\prime}}
. In the following, we fix such a metric

\metric_{w^{*}}
. Moreover, we consider the function
m:T\to[0,\infty)
given by

\displaystyle m(t):=\begin{cases}\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}^{-1}\,,&\mbox{ if }\delta_{t}\neq 0\,,\\
0\,,&\mbox{ else. }\end{cases}

Our first goal is to show that for all
f\in F
and
t\in T
we have

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{t},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}\cdot m(t)\cdot\langle\delta_{t},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,.

(74)

Obviously, if
\delta_{t}\neq 0
this identity directly follows from the definition of
m
. Moreover, in the case

\delta_{t}=0
we have
\langle\delta_{t},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=0
for all
f\in F
, and therefore both sides of (
74
) equal
0
.

Now, from (
74
) we conclude that
\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}=\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}\cdot\|m(t)\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}
,
which in turn implies
\|m(t)\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}\leq 1
for all
t\in T
.
This makes it possible to consider the map

\displaystyle\Phi:T

\displaystyle\to B_{F^{\prime}}\times[0,\infty)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigl(m(t)\delta_{t},\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}\bigr)\,.

In the following, we equip
B_{F^{\prime}}\times[0,\infty)
with the metric

\displaystyle\metric_{w^{*}}^{+}\bigl(y_{1}^{\prime},r_{1}),(y_{2}^{\prime},r_{2})\bigr):=\metric_{w^{*}}(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime})+|r_{1}-r_{2}|\,,\qquad\qquad y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime}\in B_{F^{\prime}},r_{1},r_{2}\in[0,\infty).

Since
(B_{F^{\prime}},\metric_{w^{*}})
is a separable metric space, we quickly verify that
(B_{F^{\prime}}\times[0,\infty),\metric_{w^{*}}^{+})

is also a separable metric space. Since subsets of separable metric spaces are separable metric spaces, we then see that
(\Phi(T),\metric_{w^{*}}^{+})

is also a separable metric space. Consequently, if we define
\metric:T\times T\to[0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle\metric(s,t):=\metric_{w^{*}}^{+}(\Phi(s),\Phi(t))\,,\qquad\qquad s,t\in T,

then
(T,\metric)
becomes a separable pseudo-metric space.

It remains to show that all
f\in F
are continuous with respect to
\metric
. To this end, we fix some
f\in F
and
t\in T
, as well as a sequence
(t_{n})\subset T
with

\metric(t_{n},t)\to 0
. By the definition of
\metric
,
\metric_{w^{*}}^{+}
, and
\Phi
the latter ensures

\displaystyle\metric_{w^{*}}\bigl(m(t_{n})\delta_{t_{n}},m(t)\delta_{t}\bigr)+\bigl|\|\delta_{t_{n}}\|_{F^{\prime}}-\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}\bigr|=\metric_{w^{*}}^{+}(\Phi(t_{n}),\Phi(t))\to 0\,.

This in turn ensures
\|\delta_{t_{n}}\|_{F^{\prime}}\to\|\delta_{t}\|_{F^{\prime}}
and

\displaystyle\bigl\langle m(t_{n})\delta_{t_{n}},f\bigr\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\to\bigl\langle m(t)\delta_{t},f\bigr\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,.

Combining these two limits with (
74
) we find
f(t_{n})=\langle\delta_{t_{n}},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\to\langle\delta_{t},f\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=f(t)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma of
4.10
.

Let us fix an
f\in C^{1}([0,1])
with
f\neq 0
. Then, if
f
is constant, we find
y_{0}^{\prime}(f)=f(0)\neq 0
, and if
f
is not constant,
there is an
t\in(0,1)
with
f^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
. Since
f^{\prime}
is continuous, we then find a
t_{i}
with
y_{i}^{\prime}(f)=f^{\prime}(t_{i})\neq 0

as in the proof of Theorem
4.5
.
∎

8
Proofs for Section
5

In this section we present the proofs related to the results of Section
5
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

The equivalence directly follows from Lemma
E.2
, and the continuity of the mean and covariance functions can be found in
Lemma
E.3
.
∎

For the proof of Theorem
5.2
and Theorem
5.3
we need the following
intermediate lemma that computes the update formulas for finite
S\subset T
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space, and
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a
Gaussian processes on
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
with continuous paths, mean function
m
, and covariance function
k
.
Moreover, let
X:\Omega\to C(T)
be the Gaussian random variable associated to the process via (
26
)
and
S:=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}\subset T
. Finally, let

\displaystyle Y:=I_{S}\circ X

and
m(S):=(m(s_{1}),\dots,m(s_{n}))
,
where
I_{S}:C(T)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by (
30
).
Then for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in T
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=m(t)+K_{t,S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}(y-m(S))

as well as
