\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},(\cov(X)\!-\!\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X))\delta_{t_{2}}\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=k(t_{1},t_{2})\!-\!K_{t_{1},S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}K_{S,t_{2}}.

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Note that our notations give
\mathbb{E}Y=(\mathbb{E}X_{s_{1}},\dots,\mathbb{E}X_{s_{n}})=m(S)
.
Using (
B.3
)
the first identity then follows from

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}(y-\mathbb{E}Y)\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}X_{t}+\bigl(\delta_{t}\circ\cov(X,Y)\circ(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\bigr)(y-\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=m(t)+\bigl(\cov(\delta_{t}\circ X,Y)\circ(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\bigr)(y-m(S))

\displaystyle=m(t)+\cov(\delta_{t}\circ X,Y)\bigl(K_{S,S}^{\dagger}(y-m(S))\bigr)

\displaystyle=m(t)+K_{t,S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}(y-m(S))\,.

For the proof of the second identity, we first observe that

\displaystyle\Bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\bigl(\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X)\bigr)(\delta_{t_{2}})\Bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\cov(X)(\delta_{t_{2}})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}-\bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X)(\delta_{t_{2}})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\,.

Moreover, by (
B.1
) and
\langle\delta_{t_{i}},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=X_{t_{i}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\cov(X)(\delta_{t_{2}})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\cov\bigl(\langle\delta_{t_{1}},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)},\langle\delta_{t_{2}},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\bigr)=k(t_{1},t_{2})\,.

In addition, the definition of cross covariance operators gives

\displaystyle\cov(Y,X)(\delta_{t_{2}})=\int_{\Omega}(X_{t_{2}}-\mathbb{E}X_{t_{2}})(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\cov(Y,X_{t_{2}})(1)=K_{S,t_{2}}\cdot 1=K_{S,t_{2}}\,,

and hence we find

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\cov(X,Y)(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\cov(Y,X)(\delta_{t_{2}})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\cov(\delta_{t_{1}}\circ X,Y)\circ(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\bigr)\bigl(\cov(Y,X)(\delta_{t_{2}})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(\cov(\delta_{t_{1}}\circ X,Y)\circ(\cov Y)^{\dagger}\bigr)(K_{S,t_{2}})

\displaystyle=\cov(\delta_{t_{1}}\circ X,Y)\bigl(K_{S,S}^{\dagger}K_{S,t_{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=K_{t_{1},S}K_{S,S}^{\dagger}K_{S,t_{2}}\,.

Combining these considerations yields the second identity.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

In view of Theorem
3.1
it suffices to translate the formulas for
\mu_{X|Y}
and
C_{X|Y}
to the claimed formulas
for the mean and covariance function via (
27
), respectively (
28
).
This, however, is straightforward by using Lemma
8.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

By Corollary
4.6
the operators
I_{S_{n}}:C(S)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

give a filtering sequence for
F:=C(S)
.
In addition, the definition of
Y
ensures that
(X,Y)
are jointly Gaussian.
Applying
Theorem
3.3
with
E:=C(T)
and
Y_{n}:=I_{S_{n}}\circ Y
gives us a
\mathrm{P}_{Y}
-zero set
N\in\mathfrak{B}(C(S))
such
that the assertions of parts
i)
to
viii)
hold true.
In particular, we know that

\mathrm{P}_{X|Y}(\,\cdot\,|g)
is a Gaussian measure on
C(T)
for all
g\in C(S)\setminus N
.

To compute its mean and covariance function we pick a

g\in C(S)\setminus N
and write
I_{n}:=I_{S_{n}}
. Then, part
i)
of Theorem
3.3
together with Lemma
8.1

shows

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(I_{n}(g)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=m(t)+K_{t,S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}(g(S_{n})-m(S_{n}))

and

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\bigr(\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)\bigr)(\delta_{t_{2}})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=k(t_{1},t_{2})-K_{t_{1},S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}K_{S_{n},t_{2}}

for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in T
.
Combining part
iii)
of Theorem
3.3
with (
27
) we thus obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in T}\Bigl|m(t)+K_{t,S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}\bigl(g(S_{n})-m(S_{n})\bigr)-m_{X|Y=g}(t)\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in T}\Bigl|\bigl\langle\delta_{t},\mathbb{E}X\!+\!\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(I_{n}(g)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}-\langle\delta_{t},\mu_{X|Y}(g)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in T}\Bigl|\bigl\langle\delta_{t},\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(I_{n}(g)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})-\mu_{X|Y}(g)\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\,\mathbb{E}X+\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}(I_{n}(g)-\mathbb{E}Y_{n})-\mu_{X|Y}(g)\,\bigr\|_{C(T)}

\displaystyle\to 0\,,

where in the second to last step we used that the norm of
C(T)
is the supremums norm.

The formula for the covariance function can be derived similarly: Indeed, combining
part
iv)
of Theorem
3.3
with (
28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in T}\Bigl|k(t_{1},t_{2})-K_{t_{1},S_{n}}K_{S_{n},S_{n}}^{\dagger}K_{S_{n},t_{2}}-k_{X|Y}(t_{1},t_{2})\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in T}\Bigl|\Bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\bigl(\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)\bigr)(\delta_{t_{2}})\Bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\langle\delta_{t_{1}},C_{X|Y}\delta_{t_{2}}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t_{1},t_{2}\in T}\Bigl|\Bigl\langle\delta_{t_{1}},\bigl(\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)-C_{X|Y}\bigr)(\delta_{t_{2}})\Bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\sup_{t_{2}\in T}\bigl\|\,\bigl(\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)-C_{X|Y}\bigr)(\delta_{t_{2}})\,\bigr\|_{C(T)}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\,\cov(X)-\cov(X,Y_{n})(\cov Y_{n})^{\dagger}\cov(Y_{n},X)-C_{X|Y}\,\bigr\|

\displaystyle\to 0\,,

where we used that

\delta_{t_{2}}\in B_{C(T)^{\prime}}
for all
t_{2}\in T
.

To prove the last assertion, we note that
\langle\delta_{s},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=X_{s}=\langle\delta_{s},Y\rangle_{C(S)^{\prime},C(S)}
.
Consequently,
the random variable
\langle\delta_{s},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}
is
\sigma(Y)
-measurable.
If we now pick an
\omega\in Y^{-1}(\{g\})
, then we have
\omega\not\in Y^{-1}(N)
and
g=Y(\omega)=X_{|S}(\omega)
, and therefore
part
viii)
of Theorem
3.3

gives

\displaystyle m_{X|Y=g}(s)=\bigl\langle\delta_{s},\mu_{X|Y}(Y(\omega))\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\bigl\langle\delta_{s},X(\omega)\bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=g(s)\,.

Analogously,
part
viii)
of Theorem
3.3
shows

\displaystyle k_{X|Y}(s,t)=k_{X|Y}(t,s)=\langle\delta_{t},C_{X|Y}\delta_{s}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=0

for all
t\in T
.
∎

A
Measurability in Banach spaces and Banach Space Valued Integration

In this supplement we recall some basic measurability results in Banach spaces
E
as well as the Bochner integral.

We begin, however, with a result on the

w^{*}
-topology on
E^{\prime}
for separable Banach spaces
E
. Its first two assertions can be found
in
[
39
, Theorem 2.6.18 and Theorem 2.6.23]

or
[
15
, Theorem V.3.1 and Theorem V.5.1]
, and the last assertion follows
from the fact that compact metric spaces are separable.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space. Then
B_{E^{\prime}}
is
w^{*}
-compact,
the relative
w^{*}
-topology of
B_{E^{\prime}}
is metrizable, and
there exists a countable
\mathfrak{D}\subset B_{E^{\prime}}

that is

w^{*}
-dense in
B_{E^{\prime}}
.

Note that for the proofs of our main results we need the existence of such sets
\mathfrak{D}
, and
another key result, namely Theorem
4.2
that ensures the existence of our approximation scheme,
also needs such sets in its formulation.
In addition,
the fact that the
w^{*}
-topology of
B_{E^{\prime}}
is metrizable is often used in the the proofs as it allows to work with sequences
rather than nets and this in turn opens the door for limit theorems of integrals. Finally, working with
\mathfrak{D}
often makes it possible to
better control unions of
sets with measure zero.

The following theorem, which can be found in e.g.
[
29
, Proposition 1.1.1]
and which also follows from a version of
Pettis’ measurability theorem
[
14
, Theorem E.9]
, shows that for separable Banach spaces the
\sigma
-algebras

\sigma(E^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{B}(E)
coincide.

Theorem A.2
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space. Then we have
\sigma(E^{\prime})=\mathfrak{B}(E)
.

The next
lemma considers the behavior of
\sigma
-algebras on
F
that are generated by some
B\subset F^{\prime}
. A somewhat similar yet less general result can be found in
[
29
, Proposition 1.1.1]
.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
F
be a Banach space and
B\subset F^{\prime}
be non-empty. Then we have

\displaystyle\sigma(B)=\sigma(\spann B)=\sigma(\overline{B}^{w^{*}})=\sigma(\overline{\spann B}^{w^{*}})\,.

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

We first prove the equation
\sigma(B)=\sigma(\spann B)
. Here, the inclusion “
\subset
” is obvious. To establish the converse, we fix an

y^{\prime}\in\spann B
. Then there exist an
n\geq 1
as well as
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and
y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{n}^{\prime}\in B
with

y^{\prime}=\alpha_{1}y_{1}^{\prime}+\dots+\alpha_{n}y_{n}^{\prime}
. Clearly, each
y_{i}^{\prime}:F\to\mathbb{R}
is
(\sigma(B),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable, and hence
y^{\prime}
is also

(\sigma(B),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable. This gives
\sigma(y^{\prime})\subset\sigma(B)
, and since
y^{\prime}\in\spann B
was chosen arbitrarily, we find
\sigma(\spann B)\subset\sigma(B)
.

For the proof of
\sigma(\spann B)=\sigma(\overline{\spann B}^{w^{*}})
we write
V:=\spann B
and

\displaystyle W:=\bigl\{y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}:y^{\prime}:F\to\mathbb{R}\mbox{ is $\sigma(V)$-measurable}\bigr\}\,.

Then
W
is a subspace of
F^{\prime}
with
V\subset W
. Moreover, if we have a sequence
(y_{i}^{\prime})\subset W
that converges to an
y^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
in
the
w^{*}
-topology, then we have

\displaystyle\langle y^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\langle y_{i}^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,,\qquad\qquad y\in F.

Since each
y_{i}^{\prime}:F\to\mathbb{R}
is
(\sigma(V),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable, we then conclude that their pointwise limit
y^{\prime}

is also
(\sigma(v),\mathfrak{B})
-measurable, that is
y^{\prime}\in W
. In other words,
W
is
w^{*}
-sequentially closed.
Now a consequence of Krein-S̆mulian’s theorem, see e.g.
[
39
, Corollary 2.7.13]
, shows that
W
is even
w^{*}
-closed,
and hence we find

\displaystyle\overline{V}^{w^{*}}\subset\overline{W}^{w^{*}}=W\,.

By the definition of
W
we conclude that every
y^{\prime}\in\overline{V}^{w^{*}}
is
\sigma(V)
-measurable, and this in turn shows

\sigma(\overline{\spann B}^{w^{*}})\subset\sigma(\spann B)
. The converse inclusion is trivial.

The proof of
\sigma(B)=\sigma(\overline{B}^{w^{*}})
follows from the already established identities and trivial inclusions, namely

\sigma(B)\subset\sigma(\overline{B}^{w^{*}})\subset\sigma(\overline{\spann B}^{w^{*}})=\sigma(\spann B)=\sigma(B)
.
∎

Let us now turn to the Bochner integral. To this end,
let
(\Omega,{\cal A})
be a measurable space,
E
be a Banach space, and
X:\Omega\to E
.
It is well known, that
X
is strongly measurable if and only if it is weakly measurable and

X(\Omega)
is separable, see e.g.
[
14
, Theorem E.9]
or
[
29
, Theorem 1.1.6]
.
In addition,
if
X
is strongly measurable, then
there exists a sequence
(X_{n})
of (strongly) measurable step functions
X_{n}:\Omega\to E
with

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}(\omega)

\displaystyle=X(\omega)\,,

(A.1)

\displaystyle\|X_{n}(\omega)\|_{E}

\displaystyle\leq\|X(\omega)\|_{E}

(A.2)

for all all
\omega\in\Omega
and
n\geq 1
, see e.g.
[
14
, Proposition E.2]
.
Here we note that step functions are also called simple functions. In addition,
for step functions all three notions of measurability coincide, and for this reason, we usually
speak of measurable step functions.
Finally, every pointwise limit
X
of a sequence
(X_{n})
of strongly measurable functions is strongly measurable,
see e.g.
[
14
, Proposition E.1]
. Consequently, we can characterize strong measurability by pointwise approximations
with measurable step functions in the sense of (
A.1
). In particular, the finite sum of strongly measurable functions is strongly
measurable and if
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable and
X:\Omega\to E
is strongly measurable, then
f\cdot X:\Omega\to E
is
strongly measurable.

If we have a probability measure
\mathrm{P}
on
(\Omega,{\cal A})
and a
measurable step function
X:\Omega\to E
with some fixed representation
X=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}x_{i}
with
A_{i}\in{\cal A}
and
x_{i}\in E
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{P}(A_{i})x_{i}

(A.3)

is actually independent of the chosen representation, where the proof is analogous to the
\mathbb{R}
-valued case. To extend this integral
we say that a strongly measurable
X:\Omega\to E
is Bochner integrable with respect to
\mathrm{P}
, if

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)<\infty\,.

In this case,
using approximating sequences described in (
A.1
) and (
A.2
), one can define the Bochner integral by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}X:=\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}X_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,

(A.4)

where the integral in the middle is independent of the chosen approximation
(X_{n})
, see e.g.
[
14
, page 399]
.
Moreover, the limit is, of course, with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{E}
.
The set
{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
of all Bochner integrable
X:\Omega\to E
is a vector space, see e.g.
[
14
, Proposition E.4]
,
which obviously contains all measurable step functions.
Moreover, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\|X\|_{{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}}:=\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)

(A.5)

defines a semi-norm on
{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
.
In addition, the Bochner integral defines
a linear map
{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}\to E
that is also bounded since we have

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\,\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,\Bigr\|_{E}\leq\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)\,,\qquad\qquad X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}\,,

(A.6)

see e.g.
[
14
, Proposition E.5]
.
As usual, we write
{L_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
for the space of
\mathrm{P}
-equivalence classes, which becomes a Banach space
if we modify the norm above in the usual way, see e.g.
[
14
, page 401]
.

The Bochner integral also enjoys a form of the dominated convergence theorem. Namely, if we have a sequence
(X_{n})
of strongly
measurable
X_{n}:\Omega\to E
for which there exist both an
X:\Omega\to E
and a
\mathrm{P}
-integrable
g:\Omega\to[0,\infty]
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}(\omega)

\displaystyle=X(\omega)\,,

(A.7)

\displaystyle\|X_{n}(\omega)\|_{E}

\displaystyle\leq\|g(\omega)\|_{E}

(A.8)

for all all
\omega\in\Omega
and
n\geq 1
, then
X
and all
X_{n}
are Bochner
\mathrm{P}
-integrable, and we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}X_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,.

(A.9)

For a proof, we refer to e.g.
[
14
, Proposition E.6]
, where we note that the strong measurability of
X
follows from
e.g.
[
14
, Proposition E.1]
.

Moreover, if
T:E\to F
is a bounded linear operator mapping into another Banach space
F
and
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
, then the map
T\circ X:\Omega\to F
satisfies both

T\circ X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},F)}
and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}T\circ X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=T\biggl(\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)\,,

(A.10)

see e.g.
[
17
, Theorem 6 in Chapter II.2]
or
[
29
, Theorem 1.2.4]

for a stronger result in this direction that goes back to E. Hille.
In particular, for all
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
, we have both
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P})}
and the “Pettis property”

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\biggl\langle x^{\prime},\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}\,.

(A.11)

This shows that for
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
the Bochner integral coincides with the so-called Pettis integral, which is
discussed in e.g.
[
29
, Chapter 1.2.c]
.

Similarly to
{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
one can define, for
p\in(1,\infty)
, the vector space

{{\cal L}_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}
as the set of all strongly measurable

X:\Omega\to E
with

\displaystyle\|X\|_{{{\cal L}_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}}^{p}:=\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)<\infty.

Again this defines a semi-norm on
{{\cal L}_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
{L_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}
denotes the Banach space of corresponding
\mathrm{P}
-equivalence classes.

B
Covariance Operators

In this supplement properties of covariance operators of Banach space valued random variables are collected from the literature. In addition, some
basic and probably well-known results we could not find the literature are presented and proved.

To begin with,
let us fix a probability space
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
and Banach spaces
E
and
F
.
Moreover, let
us fix some
X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},F)}
, as well as a
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
.
Since
X
and
Y
are strongly measurable, the map

\displaystyle\langle y^{\prime},Y-\mathbb{E}Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot(X-\mathbb{E}X):\Omega\to E

is also strongly measurable, see the discussion following (
A.2
).
In addition,
the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality shows that it is also Bochner integrable.
Moreover, the resulting linear operator
\cov(X,Y):F^{\prime}\to E
defined by

\displaystyle\cov(X,Y)y^{\prime}=\int_{\Omega}\langle y^{\prime},Y-\mathbb{E}Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,(X-\mathbb{E}X)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,\qquad\qquad y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}

is bounded by virtue of the norm-inequality for Bochner integrals (
A.6
) and another application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Applying the “Pettis property” (
A.11
) then gives

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X,Y)y^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle y^{\prime},Y-\mathbb{E}Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,\langle x^{\prime},X-\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

(B.1)

\displaystyle=\cov\bigl(\langle y^{\prime},Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F},\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigr)\,.

In other words, the cross covariance operator provides the covariances between all pairs of one-dimensional projections of
X
and
Y
.
In particular, if
X
and
Y
are independent, then all pairs
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
and
\langle y^{\prime},Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}
are independent, and hence we find

\cov(X,Y)=0
.
Another routine check shows that the dual
\cov(X,Y)^{\prime}:E^{\prime}\to F^{\prime\prime}
of
\cov(X,Y)
is given by

\displaystyle\cov(X,Y)^{\prime}=\iota_{F}\circ\cov(Y,X)\,,

(B.2)

where
\iota_{F}:F\to F^{\prime\prime}
denotes the canonical embedding of
F
into its bidual
F^{\prime\prime}
.
In addition, if
S:E\to\tilde{E}
and
T:F\to\tilde{F}
are bounded linear
operators and we have
x\in E
and
y\in F
, then the following identities hold true:

\displaystyle\cov(S\circ X,T\circ Y)

\displaystyle=S\circ\cov(X,Y)\circ T^{\prime}\,,

(B.3)

\displaystyle\cov(x+X,y+Y)

\displaystyle=\cov(X,Y)\,.

(B.4)

Indeed, (
B.3
) follows by applying (
A.10
) with the operator
S
and using the definition of
T^{\prime}
. Moreover,
(
B.4
) is a direct consequence of the definition of
\cov(X,Y)
.

In the case
X=Y
, the covariance operator
\cov(X):=\cov(X,X)
is an operator
\cov(X):E^{\prime}\to E
and with (
B.1
) we find

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime}_{1},\cov(X)x^{\prime}_{2}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle x^{\prime}_{2},\cov(X)x^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

(B.5)

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},\cov(X)x^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\var\bigl(\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\bigr)\geq 0

(B.6)

for all
x^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, where
\var(Z)
denotes the variance of an
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable
Z\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
.
This motivates the following definition.

Definition B.1
.

Let
E
be a Banach space. A bounded linear operator
T:E^{\prime}\to E
is called an abstract covariance operator if it is
symmetric and non-negative, that is,
if
for all
x^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Tx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\langle x^{\prime},Tx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\geq 0\,.

Analogously, a bounded linear operator
S:E\to E^{\prime}
is symmetric and non-negative, if for all
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in E
we have

\displaystyle\langle Sx_{1},x_{2}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle Sx_{2},x_{1}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\langle Sx,x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\geq 0\,.

As discussed above, every covariance operator
\cov(X)
is an abstract covariance operator.
In addition, an elementary calculation shows that
the sum
T_{1}+T_{2}:E^{\prime}\to E
of two abstract covariance operators
T_{1},T_{2}:E^{\prime}\to E
is an abstract covariance operator.
Moreover, every abstract covariance operator
T:E^{\prime}\to E
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime},T(x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+2\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

which in turn implies

\displaystyle\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\frac{\langle x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime},T(x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}{2}

(B.7)

for all
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
The following lemma uses this observation to show
that abstract covariance operators are determined by the behavior of
x^{\prime}\mapsto\langle x^{\prime},Tx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
, that is, on the diagonal.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
E
be a Banach space and
S,T:E^{\prime}\to E
be two abstract covariance operators with

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},Tx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x^{\prime},Sx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}.

Then we have
S=T
.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

Let us fix some
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. By (
B.7
) and the assumed identity on the diagonal we then know

\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Sx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
.
This implies
Sx_{2}^{\prime}=Tx_{2}^{\prime}
for all
x^{\prime}_{2}\in E^{\prime}
by Hahn-Banach’s Theorem.
Consequently we have
S=T
.
∎

In the finite dimensional case
E=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, one can quickly check that a linear operator
T:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an abstract covariance operator, if and only if
its representing
n\times n
-matrix
A
with respect to the canonical bases of
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is symmetric and positive semi-definite.
In this case, the spectral theorem for matrices shows
A=S\Lambda S^{\mathsf{T}}
, where

\Lambda
is a diagonal
n\times n
-matrix given by the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
of
A

and
S
is an orthogonal

n\times n
-matrix
S
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle A^{\dagger}=(S\Lambda S^{\mathsf{T}})^{\dagger}=S\Lambda^{\dagger}S^{\mathsf{T}}\,,

(B.8)

where the second identity can be found in e.g.
[
61
, Theorem 1.1.2]
. Moreover, a straightforward calculation shows that

\Lambda^{\dagger}
is the diagonal
n\times n
-matrix having the entries
\lambda_{1}^{-1},\dots,\lambda_{n}^{-1}
with
0^{-1}:=0
.
Consequently,
A^{\dagger}
is also symmetric and positive semi-definite. Moreover, this matrix describes
the operator
T^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}
, and therefore
T^{\dagger}
is symmetric and non-negative.

It can be quickly verified that an abstract covariance operator
T:E^{\prime}\to E
defines a (reproducing) kernel
k_{T}:E^{\prime}\times E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle k_{T}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}):=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,\qquad\qquad x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}\,.

Lemma
B.2
shows that this kernel is determined by its behaviour on the diagonal. Moreover, the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for kernels, see e.g.
[
51
, (4.14)]
yields

\displaystyle|\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}|^{2}=|k_{T}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq k_{T}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})\cdot k_{T}(x_{2}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Tx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,.

(B.9)

Consequently, for every
x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
with
\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Tx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=0
we find

Tx_{2}^{\prime}=0
by a simple application of Hahn-Banach’s theorem.

The next lemma collects some additional information
about this kernel
k_{T}
in the case of covariance operators
T=\cov(X)
.

Theorem B.3
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
be a separable Banach space,

X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
, and
k_{X}:=k_{\cov(X)}:E^{\prime}\times E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
be the resulting kernel. Then the
restriction
(k_{X})_{|B_{E^{\prime}}\times B_{E^{\prime}}}
of
k_{X}
onto
B_{E^{\prime}}\times B_{E^{\prime}}
is
w^{*}
-continuous.

Proof of Theorem
B.3
.

Since
(B_{E^{\prime}},w^{*})
is metrizable, see Theorem
A.1
,
it suffices to show that the restricted kernel is
w^{*}
-sequentially continuous.
Moreover, by (
B.7
) it suffices to show that the kernel restricted to
2B_{E^{\prime}}
is

w^{*}
-sequentially continuous on the diagonal.
Finally, by a simple scaling argument, it actually suffices to verify that

x^{\prime}\mapsto k_{X}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})
is
w^{*}
-sequentially continuous on
B_{E^{\prime}}
.

To prove the latter, we fix some
x^{\prime}_{0}\in B_{E^{\prime}}
as well as a sequence
(x_{n}^{\prime})\subset B_{E^{\prime}}
such that

x_{n}^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}_{0}
with respect to
w^{*}
.
Moreover, we write
Z:=X-\mathbb{E}X
. By (
B.4
) and (
B.1
)
we then find

\displaystyle\langle x_{n}^{\prime},\cov(X)x_{n}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{n}^{\prime},\cov(Z)x_{n}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{n}^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle x_{n}^{\prime},Z\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

(B.10)

for all
n\geq 0
.
For
n\geq 0
we define
g_{n}:\Omega\to[0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle g_{n}(\omega):=\langle x_{n}^{\prime},Z(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}^{2}\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega.

By the assumed
w^{*}
-convergence
x_{n}^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}_{0}
we then see that
g_{n}(\omega)\to g_{0}(\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Moreover, a simple estimate yields

\displaystyle|g_{n}(\omega)|\leq\|x_{n}^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot\|Z(\omega)\|_{E}^{2}\leq\|Z(\omega)\|^{2}_{E}\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega.

Using
Z\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
,
Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, and (
B.10
), we then find

\displaystyle\langle x_{n}^{\prime},\cov(X)x_{n}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x_{0}^{\prime},\cov(X)x_{0}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,

that is, we have shown the assertion.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Let
E
and
F
be separable Banach spaces,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space, and

X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},F)}
be centered.
Moreover, let

S:F\to F^{\prime}
be a bounded, linear, symmetric, and non-negative operator.
Then the operator
K:E^{\prime}\to E
defined by

\displaystyle K:=\cov(X,Y)S\cov(Y,X)\,.

is an abstract covariance operator, and for all
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Kx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\!\int\limits_{\Omega\times\Omega}\!\!\!\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X(\omega_{1})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle x_{2}^{\prime},X(\omega_{2})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle SY(\omega_{1}),Y(\omega_{2})\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{2}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}).

Moreover, the map
B_{E^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
x^{\prime}\mapsto\langle x^{\prime},Kx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is
w^{*}
-continuous.

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

Obviously,
K
is bounded and linear. To verify that
K
is an abstract covariance operator, we fix some

x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and write
y_{i}:=\cov(Y,X)x_{i}^{\prime}\in F
. With the help of (
B.2
) we then obtain

\displaystyle\langle x_{1}^{\prime},Kx_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\langle x_{1}^{\prime},\cov(X,Y)Sy_{2}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle\cov(X,Y)^{\prime}x_{1}^{\prime},Sy_{2}\rangle_{F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\langle\iota_{F}\cov(Y,X)x_{1}^{\prime},Sy_{2}\rangle_{F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\langle Sy_{2},y_{1}\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,.

Since we assumed that
S
is symmetric and non-negative, we can now quickly verify that this is also true for
K
.

To establish the integral formula, we again
fix some
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
Then the definition of
\cov(Y,X)
gives

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{1}:=S\cov(Y,X)x^{\prime}_{1}=S\biggl(\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)=\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot(S\circ Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,

where in the last step we used (
A.10
).
Another routine calculation for
y\in F
thus yields

\displaystyle\langle y^{\prime}_{1},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=\langle\iota_{F}y,y^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\biggl\langle\iota_{F}y,\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot(S\circ Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr\rangle_{F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle\iota_{F}y,S\circ Y\rangle_{F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X(\omega_{1})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle S\circ Y(\omega_{1}),y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{1})\,,

where we used the “Pettis property” (
A.11
) in the third step.
Combining the last identity with the definition of
\cov(X,Y)
and (
A.11
), we then find

\displaystyle\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Kx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle x_{2}^{\prime},\cov(X,Y)y_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\biggl\langle x_{2}^{\prime},\int_{\Omega}\langle y^{\prime}_{1},Y(\omega_{2})\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot X(\omega_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{2})\biggr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle y^{\prime}_{1},Y(\omega_{2})\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot\langle x_{2}^{\prime},X(\omega_{2})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X(\omega_{1})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle S\circ Y(\omega_{1}),Y(\omega_{2})\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot\langle x_{2}^{\prime},X(\omega_{2})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{2})\,.

(B.11)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle g(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})

\displaystyle:=\Bigl|\langle x_{1}^{\prime},X(\omega_{1})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle S\circ Y(\omega_{1}),Y(\omega_{2})\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot\langle x_{2}^{\prime},X(\omega_{2})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{1}^{\prime}\|\cdot\|x_{2}^{\prime}\|\cdot\|S\|\cdot\|X(\omega_{1})\|_{E}\cdot\|Y(\omega_{1})\|_{F}\cdot\|Y(\omega_{2})\|_{F}\cdot\|X(\omega_{2})\|_{E}

(B.12)

for all
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
. Now, by assumption and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
we know
\|X\|_{E}\cdot\|Y\|_{F}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P})}
. This gives

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}|g(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})|\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{1}^{\prime}\|\cdot\|x_{2}^{\prime}\|\cdot\|S\|\cdot\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega_{1})\|_{E}\cdot\|Y(\omega_{1})\|_{F}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega_{2})\|_{E}\cdot\|Y(\omega_{2})\|_{F}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega_{2})

\displaystyle<\infty\,,

(B.13)

and therefore
Fubini’s theorem applied to (
B.11
) yields the formula for
\langle x_{2}^{\prime},Kx_{1}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
.

Let us now recall from Theorem
A.1
that
(B_{E^{\prime}},w^{*})
is a compact metrizable space since we assumed that

E
is separable.
Consequently, it suffices to show that
x^{\prime}\mapsto\langle x^{\prime},Kx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is
w^{*}
-sequentially continuous. To this end,
we fix an
x^{\prime}_{0}\in B_{E^{\prime}}
and a
sequence
(x_{n}^{\prime})\subset B_{E^{\prime}}
that converges to
x^{\prime}_{0}
with respect to
w^{*}
, that is,

\langle x_{n}^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x_{0}^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
for all
x\in E
.
We write

\displaystyle f_{n}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}):=\langle x_{n}^{\prime},X(\omega_{1})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot\langle S\circ Y(\omega_{1}),Y(\omega_{2})\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot\langle x_{n}^{\prime},X(\omega_{2})\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

for all
n\geq 0
and
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
. Then we have
f_{n}\to f_{0}
pointwise and (
B.12
)
shows

\displaystyle|f_{n}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})|\leq\|S\|\cdot\|X(\omega_{1})\|\cdot\|Y(\omega_{1})\|\cdot\|Y(\omega_{2})\|\cdot\|X(\omega_{2})\|

for all
n\geq 0
and
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
.
Since this upper bound of
f_{n}
is
\mathrm{P}\otimes\mathrm{P}
-integrable by (
B.13
) and Fubini’s theorem,
we then find

\displaystyle\langle x_{n}^{\prime},Kx_{n}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}f_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{2}\to\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}f_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{2}=\langle x_{0}^{\prime},Kx_{0}^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

by the already established formula for
\langle x_{n}^{\prime},Kx_{n}^{\prime}\rangle
and Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem.
∎

So far, we have defined the cross covariance operator
\cov(Y,X):E^{\prime}\to F
with the help of an

F
-valued Bochner integral that is defined pointwise, i.e. for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
Our next goal is to show that the cross covariance operator can also be expressed by an operator valued Bochner integral.
Here we note that in the Hilbert space case such a representation can sometimes be found in the literature, while in the general Banach space
case we could not find such representation. For this reason, we explain its construction in some detail.

Let us begin by quickly recalling some basic facts about tensor products:
Let us fix
some Banach spaces
E
and
F
. For
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y\in F
, we define
the bounded linear operator
x^{\prime}\otimes y:E\to F
by

\displaystyle(x^{\prime}\otimes y)(x):=\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y\,,\qquad\qquad x\in E\,.

(B.14)

A simple calculation then shows that

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}\otimes y\|=\sup_{x\in B_{E}}\|\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y\|_{F}=\sup_{x\in B_{E}}|\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}|\cdot\|y\|_{F}=\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y\|_{F}\,.

(B.15)

As usual,
x^{\prime}\otimes y
is called an elementary tensor. Elementary tensors can be added and a
quick calculation shows bilinearity, or more precisely

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{1}\otimes y+x^{\prime}_{2}\otimes y

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{1}+x^{\prime}_{2})\otimes y\,,

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot(x^{\prime}\otimes y)

\displaystyle=(\alpha x^{\prime})\otimes y\,,

\displaystyle x^{\prime}\otimes y_{1}+x^{\prime}\otimes y_{2}

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}\otimes(y_{1}+y_{2})\,,

\displaystyle\beta\cdot(x^{\prime}\otimes y)

\displaystyle=x^{\prime}\otimes(\beta y)

for all
x^{\prime},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}\in E^{\prime}
,
y,y_{1},y_{2}\in F
, and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, where, as usual, we assumed that
\otimes
has a higher priority than
+
.
We define the algebraic tensor product space by

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\otimes F:=\spann\{x^{\prime}\otimes y:x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime},y\in F\}\,.

It is easy to see that
this space equals the space of all bounded linear operators
A:E\to F
with finite rank.
Moreover, for
x^{\prime}\otimes y:E\to F
, its adjoint
(x^{\prime}\otimes y)^{\prime}:F^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle(x^{\prime}\otimes y)^{\prime}=(\iota_{F}y)\otimes x^{\prime}\,.

(B.16)

Indeed, for
x\in E
and
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
we have

((\iota_{F}y)\otimes x^{\prime})(y^{\prime})=\langle\iota_{F}y,y^{\prime}\rangle_{F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime}}\cdot x^{\prime}=\langle y^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot x^{\prime}
, and hence we find

\displaystyle\bigl\langle((\iota_{F}y)\otimes x^{\prime})(y^{\prime}),x\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}=\bigl\langle\langle y^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot x^{\prime},x\bigr\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\langle y^{\prime},y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\cdot\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle y^{\prime},\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y\bigr\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}

\displaystyle=\langle y^{\prime},(x^{\prime}\otimes y)(x)\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\,.

A bounded linear operator
A:E\to F
is called nuclear, if there exist sequences
(x_{n}^{\prime})\subset E^{\prime}
and
(y_{n})\subset F
with

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|x_{n}^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y_{n}\|_{F}<\infty\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad Tx=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\langle x_{n}^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y_{n}

(B.17)

for all
x\in E
, where the convergence in the second series is in
F
.
Note that by (
B.15
) the first condition is equivalent to
(\|x^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y_{n}\|)\in\ell_{1}
, and therefore we have

T=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_{n}^{\prime}\otimes y_{n}
, where the series is absolutely convergent with respect to the operator norm.
Finally, the nuclear norm of
T
is defined by

\displaystyle\|T\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}:=\inf\biggl\{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\|x_{n}^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y_{n}\|_{F}:(x_{n}^{\prime})\subset E^{\prime}\mbox{ and }(y_{n})\subset F\mbox{ satisfy }\eqref{def:nuc-op}\biggr\}\,.

(B.18)

It is well-known that the set
\mathfrak{N}(E,F)
of nuclear operators
T:E\to F
equipped with
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}
is a Banach space
and
we have
\|T\|\leq\|T\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}
for all
T\in\mathfrak{N}(E,F)
, see e.g.
[
63
, Satz VI.5.3]
or
[
16
, Theorem 5.25]
.
Moreover,
every finite rank operator is nuclear, and in particular all elementary tensors are nuclear.
In addition, for
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y\in F
we have

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y\|_{F}=\|x^{\prime}\otimes y\|\leq\|x^{\prime}\otimes y\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y\|_{F}

where in the last step we used that
x^{\prime}\otimes y
is a possible representation (
B.17
) of
T:=x^{\prime}\otimes y
. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}\otimes y\|=\|x^{\prime}\otimes y\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y\|_{F}

(B.19)

for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y\in F
. In particular, for
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in F
with
\|x_{1}^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\leq\|x_{2}^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}

and
\|y_{1}\|_{F}\leq\|y_{2}\|_{F}
we have

\displaystyle\|x_{1}^{\prime}\otimes y_{1}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq\|x_{2}^{\prime}\otimes y_{2}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\,.

(B.20)

The next lemma establishes a simple yet useful convergence result for elementary tensors.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
E
and
F
be Banach spaces,
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
,
y\in F
, and
(x_{n}^{\prime})\subset E^{\prime}
and
(y_{n})\subset F
be sequences
with
x_{n}^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}
and
y_{n}\to y
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y_{n}-x^{\prime}\otimes y\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\to 0\,.

Proof of Lemma
B.5
.

For the nuclear operator
T_{n}:=x^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y_{n}-x^{\prime}\otimes y
we have the alternative representation

T_{n}=(x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime})\otimes y_{n}-x^{\prime}\otimes(y-y_{n})
in the sense of (
B.17
). By the definition of the nuclear norm we hence obtain

\displaystyle\|x^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y_{n}-x^{\prime}\otimes y\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq\|x^{\prime}_{n}-x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y_{n}\|_{F}+\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|y-y_{n}\|_{F}\to 0\,,

since the convergence of
(y_{n})
ensures that the sequence
(\|y_{n}\|_{F})
is bounded.
∎

Given two functions
X^{\prime}:\Omega\to E^{\prime}
and
Y:\Omega\to F
we define the
E^{\prime}\otimes F
-valued function
X^{\prime}\otimes Y:\Omega\to E^{\prime}\otimes F
pointwise by elementary tensors, that is

\displaystyle(X^{\prime}\otimes Y)(\omega):=(X^{\prime}(\omega))\otimes(Y(\omega))\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega.

Under suitable assumptions, it turns out that the Bochner integral of tensor space valued functions can be used to compute cross covariances.
To establish this result we need the following elementary lemma, which investigates such functions in the case of
X
or
Y
being constant.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
and
F
be Banach spaces, and
X^{\prime}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E^{\prime})}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},F)}
.
Then for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y\in F
we have
X^{\prime}\otimes y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E,F))}
and
x^{\prime}\otimes Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E,F))}

with

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}\otimes y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\biggr(\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\otimes y\qquad\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\qquad\int_{\Omega}x^{\prime}\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=x^{\prime}\otimes\biggr(\int_{\Omega}Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\,.

Here, the integrals of
X^{\prime}\otimes y
and
x^{\prime}\otimes Y
are
\mathfrak{N}(E,F)
-valued Bochner integrals and the integrals of
X^{\prime}
and
Y
are
E^{\prime}
-valued, respectively
F
-valued Bochner integrals.

Proof of Lemma
B.6
.

We only prove the assertion for
X^{\prime}\otimes y
, since the proof for
x^{\prime}\otimes Y
is analogous.

Let us first assume that
X^{\prime}
is a measurable step function. We fix a representation

X=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}x_{i}^{\prime}
, where
A_{i}\in{\cal A}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
For
\omega\in\Omega
the bilinearity of elementary tensors then gives

\displaystyle(X^{\prime}\otimes y)(\omega)=(X^{\prime}(\omega))\otimes y=\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}(\omega)x_{i}^{\prime}\biggr)\otimes y=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}(\omega)(x_{i}^{\prime}\otimes y)\,.

Consequently,
X^{\prime}\otimes y:\Omega\to\mathfrak{N}(E,F)

is a measurable step function, and therefore we have
X^{\prime}\otimes y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E,F))}
, see also the discussion in front of (
A.5
).
Moreover, the construction of the Bochner integral for step functions (
A.3
) shows

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}\otimes y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{P}({A_{i}})\cdot(x_{i}^{\prime}\otimes y)=\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathrm{P}({A_{i}})\cdot x_{i}^{\prime}\biggr)\otimes y=\biggr(\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\otimes y\,.

Let us now consider the general case
X^{\prime}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E^{\prime})}
. Since
X^{\prime}
is strongly measurable,
there exists a sequence
(X^{\prime}_{n})
of measurable step functions approximating

X^{\prime}
in the sense of (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
For each fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, Lemma
B.5
in combination with (
A.1
) then
shows

\displaystyle\|(X_{n}^{\prime}\otimes y)(\omega)-(X^{\prime}\otimes y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\to 0\,.

(B.21)

Furthermore, (
A.2
) and (
B.20
) give
\|(X^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq\|(X^{\prime}\otimes y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}
, and
we already know that each
X^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y:\Omega\to\mathfrak{N}(E,F)
is a measurable step function.
In summary,
(X_{n}^{\prime}\otimes y)
is a sequence approximating
X^{\prime}\otimes y
in
\mathfrak{N}(E,F)

in the sense of (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
Consequently,
X^{\prime}\otimes y:\Omega\to\mathfrak{N}(E,F)
is strongly measurable as discussed around (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
Moreover, with the help of (
B.19
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\|(X^{\prime}\otimes y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}\|X^{\prime}(\omega)\|_{E}\cdot\|y\|_{F}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)<\infty\,.

In other words we have found
X^{\prime}\otimes y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E,F))}
.
Finally, with convergence in
\mathfrak{N}(E,F)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}\otimes y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}_{n}\otimes y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\biggr(\biggr(\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)\otimes y\biggl)

\displaystyle=\biggr(\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)\otimes y

\displaystyle=\biggr(\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\otimes y\,,

where the first and last step used the construction of Bochner integrals (
A.4
),
the second step used the already verified assertion in the case of step functions, and the third step used
Lemma
B.5
.
∎

Let us now turn to the announced integral representation of cross covariance operators.
To this end, we fix two Banach spaces
E
and
F
, as well as
x\in E
and
y\in F
. For
x^{\prime\prime}:=\iota_{E}x\in E^{\prime\prime}
, where

\iota_{E}:E\to E^{\prime\prime}
denotes the canonical, isometric embedding of
E
into its bidual
E^{\prime\prime}
,
we then write

\displaystyle x\otimes y:=x^{\prime\prime}\otimes y=(\iota_{E}x)\otimes y\,.

Obviously,

x\otimes y\in E^{\prime\prime}\otimes F
is the bounded linear map
E^{\prime}\to F
given by

\displaystyle(x\otimes y)(x^{\prime})=\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}.

By construction the bilinearity is preserved, and since
\|x\|_{E}=\|\iota_{E}x\|_{E^{\prime\prime}}=\|x^{\prime\prime}\|_{E^{\prime\prime}}
holds true,
we quickly verify that (
B.19
) and (
B.20
) read as

\displaystyle\|x\otimes y\|

\displaystyle=\|x\otimes y\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\|x\|_{E}\cdot\|y\|_{F}\,,

(B.22)

\displaystyle\|x_{1}\otimes y_{1}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{2}\otimes y_{2}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

(B.23)

for all
x,x_{1},x_{2}\in E
and
y,y_{1},y_{2}\in F
with
\|x_{1}\|_{E}\leq\|x_{2}\|_{E}

and
\|y_{1}\|_{F}\leq\|y_{2}\|_{F}
. Moreover, Lemma
B.5
also holds true, since
x_{n}\to x
in
E
implies
\iota_{E}x_{n}\to\iota_{E}x
in
E^{\prime\prime}
.

Finally, Lemma
B.6
remains true:
Indeed, if we have for example an
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
, then
X^{\prime\prime}:=\iota_{E}\circ X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E^{\prime\prime})}
as discussed around (
A.10
),
and therefore Lemma

B.6
gives
X\otimes y=X^{\prime\prime}\otimes y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
with

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}X\otimes y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime\prime}\otimes y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\biggr(\int_{\Omega}X^{\prime\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\otimes y

\displaystyle=\biggr(\int_{\Omega}\iota_{E}\circ X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\otimes y

\displaystyle=\biggl(\iota_{E}\biggr(\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggl)\biggr)\otimes y

\displaystyle=\biggl(\int_{\Omega}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)\otimes y

(B.24)

by an application of (
A.10
) and our nomenclature for tensors of the form
x\otimes y
. In particular, the Bochner integral of
X

in (
B.24
)
is an
E
-valued
Bochner integral and not just an
E^{\prime\prime}
-valued integral as a naïve application of Lemma
B.6
would suggest.

With these preparation we can now present an alternative way to compute the cross covariance operator.

Lemma B.7
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
and
F
be Banach spaces, and
X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},F)}
.
Then the map
X\otimes Y:\Omega\to\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F)

is strongly measurable with
X\otimes Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
. Moreover,
\cov(Y,X)
can be computed by the

\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F)
-valued Bochner integral

\displaystyle\cov(Y,X)=\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}-(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\,.

(B.25)

Proof of Lemma
B.7
.

Our first goal is to show that it suffices to consider centered
X
and
Y
.
To this end, we pick some
X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},F)}
and define

X_{0}:=X-\mathbb{E}X
and
Y_{0}:=Y-\mathbb{E}Y
. Furthermore, we assume that the assertion is true for
X_{0}
and
Y_{0}
.
Our goal is therefore to show that the assertion is also true for
X
and
Y
.
To this end, we first observe that the
bilinearity of tensors gives

\displaystyle X_{0}\otimes Y_{0}=(X-\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(Y-\mathbb{E}Y)=X\otimes Y-X\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)-(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes Y+(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\,.

Now, our assumption guarantees
X_{0}\otimes Y_{0}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
, and Lemma
B.6

ensures both

X\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
and
(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
.
Finally,
(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\in\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F)
does not depend on
\omega
. In summary, we thus have

X\otimes Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
.
Moreover, our calculation above yields

\displaystyle\cov(Y,X)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}X_{0}\otimes Y_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y-X\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)-(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes Y+(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}-\int_{\Omega}X\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}-\int_{\Omega}(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}+(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}-(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)-(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)+(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}-(\mathbb{E}X)\otimes(\mathbb{E}Y)\,,

where in the second to last step we used Lemma
B.6
in the form of (
B.24
) twice.

Let us now turn to the actual proof.
Here, we begin by considering the
case, in which
X
and
Y
are measurable step functions. As discussed above, we may assume without loss of generality that

\mathbb{E}X=0
and
\mathbb{E}Y=0
.
We now fix some representations

X=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}x_{i}
and
Y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{B_{j}}y_{j}
, where
A_{i},B_{j}\in{\cal A}
,
x_{i}\in E
, and
y_{j}\in F
.
For
\omega\in\Omega
,
the bilinearity of elementary tensors then gives

\displaystyle(X\otimes Y)(\omega)=\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}(\omega)x_{i}\biggr)\otimes\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{B_{j}}(\omega)y_{j}\biggr)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}\cap B_{j}}(\omega)(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})\,.

Consequently,
X\otimes Y:\Omega\to\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F)

is a measurable step function, and therefore we have
X\otimes Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
, see also the discussion in front of (
A.5
).
Finally, for
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
the construction of the Bochner integral for step functions (
A.3
) shows

\displaystyle\biggl(\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{P}({A_{i}\cap B_{j}})\cdot(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})\biggr)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{P}({A_{i}\cap B_{j}})\cdot(x_{i}\otimes y_{j})(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{P}({A_{i}\cap B_{j}})\cdot\langle x^{\prime},x_{i}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y_{j}\,,

as well as

\displaystyle\cov(Y,X)(x^{\prime})=\int_{\Omega}\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}}\langle x^{\prime},x_{i}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\biggr)\,\biggr(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}_{B_{j}}y_{j}\biggl)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{i}\cap B_{j}}\langle x^{\prime},x_{i}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{P}({A_{i}\cap B_{j}})\cdot\langle x^{\prime},x_{i}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot y_{j}\,.

Combining both calculations yields (
B.25
).

Let us finally consider the case of centered
X\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},E)}
and
Y\in{{\cal L}_{2}(\mathrm{P},F)}
.
Since
X
and
Y
are strongly measurable, there then exist sequences
(X_{n})
and
(Y_{n})
of measurable step functions approximating

X
, respectively
Y
in the sense of (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
For each fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, Lemma
B.5
in combination with (
A.1
) for
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
then
shows

\displaystyle\|(X_{n}\otimes Y_{n})(\omega)-(X\otimes Y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\to 0\,.

Furthermore, (
A.2
) and (
B.23
) gives
\|(X_{n}\otimes Y_{n})(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq\|(X\otimes Y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

and we already know that each
X_{n}\otimes Y_{n}:\Omega\to\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F)
is a measurable step function.
In summary,
(X_{n}^{\prime}\otimes Y_{n})
is therefore a sequence approximating
X^{\prime}\otimes Y
in
\mathfrak{N}(E,F)

in the sense of (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
Consequently,
X\otimes Y
is strongly measurable as discussed around (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
Moreover, with the help of (
B.22
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\|(X\otimes Y)(\omega)\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}\|X(\omega)\|_{E}\cdot\|Y(\omega)\|_{F}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(\omega)<\infty\,,

where in the last step we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. In other words we have found
X\otimes Y\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},\mathfrak{N}(E^{\prime},F))}
.

To establish (
B.25
), we first note that (
A.1
) and (
A.2
)
for
X
and
(X_{n})
give
\mathbb{E}X_{n}\to\mathbb{E}X=0
by the definition of Bochner integrals and our assumption that
X
is centered.
Analogously, we find
\mathbb{E}Y_{n}\to 0
. Combining both gives
\|\mathbb{E}X_{n}\otimes\mathbb{E}Y_{n}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\to 0
by Lemma
B.5
.
For
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
we thus find

\displaystyle\biggl(\int_{\Omega}X\otimes Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\biggl(\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}X_{n}\otimes Y_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Biggl(\biggl(\int_{\Omega}X_{n}\otimes Y_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)(x^{\prime})\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Biggl(\biggl(\int_{\Omega}X_{n}\otimes Y_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)(x^{\prime})-(\mathbb{E}X_{n}\otimes\mathbb{E}Y_{n})(x^{\prime})\Biggr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigl(\cov(Y_{n},X_{n})(x^{\prime})\Bigr)\,,

(B.26)

where in the first step we used the definition of Bochner integrals, the second step used that the limit in the second expression
is with respect to the nuclear norm and thus also with respect to the operator norm, and in the last step
we used the already established identity (
B.25
) for step functions.
Moreover, we also have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\Bigl(\cov(Y_{n},X_{n})(x^{\prime})\Bigr)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\langle x^{\prime},X_{n}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot Y_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,Y\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\cov(Y,X)(x^{\prime})\,,

(B.27)

where the second step is based upon the dominated convergence property
discussed around (
A.7
) to (
A.9
) with the
\mathrm{P}
-integrable

g:\Omega\to[0,\infty]
defined by

g(\omega):=\|x^{\prime}\|_{E^{\prime}}\cdot\|X(\omega)\|_{E}\cdot\|Y(\omega)\|_{F}
and the convergence

\displaystyle\langle x^{\prime},X_{n}(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot Y_{n}(\omega)\to\langle x^{\prime},X(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\cdot Y(\omega)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Combining (
B.26
) and (
B.27
) yields (
B.25
).
∎

C
Weak Convergence, Characteristic Functions, and Gaussian Random Variables

In this supplement we mostly collect and unify known results on the objects listed in the title.
In addition, a few results we could not find in the literature are presented and proved.

Let us begin by recalling
characteristic functions in the Banach space case. To this end,
let
E
be a separable Banach space and
\mathrm{P}
be a
probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}(E)=\sigma(E^{\prime})
, see Theorem
A.2
. Then the characteristic function
\varphi_{\mathrm{P}}:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}
of
\mathrm{P}
is defined by

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}}(x^{\prime}):=\int_{E}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}(x)\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime},

see e.g.
[
59
, Chapter IV.2.1]
. Like in the finite dimensional case, the characteristic function can be used to identify
probability measures. Namely, if
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{Q}

are probability measures with
\varphi_{\mathrm{P}}=\varphi_{\mathrm{Q}}
, then we have
\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{Q}
, see e.g.
[
59
, Theorem 2.2 in Chapter IV.2.1]
.
Finally, if
X
is an
E
-valued random variable with distribution
\mathrm{P}_{X}
, we write
\varphi_{X}:=\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{X}}
and call
\varphi_{X}
the characteristic function of
X
.

Recall that characteristic functions of affine linear transformations of random variables can
be easily computed. Here we only mention the finite dimensional case.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
X:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be measurable,

A
be an
m\times d
-matrix,
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
Then, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the following two identities hold true

\displaystyle\varphi_{\langle a,X\rangle}(s)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{X}(sa)\,,

\displaystyle\varphi_{AX+b}(t)

\displaystyle=\exp(\mathrm{i}\langle t,b\rangle)\cdot\varphi_{X}(A^{\mathsf{T}}t)\,.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

The second identity can be found in e.g.
[
30
, Theorem 13.3]
,
and the first identity follows from the second one since
\langle a,X\rangle=a^{\mathsf{T}}X
and

as=sa
, where
as
denotes the matrix product and
a
are
s
are viewed as
d\times 1
and
1\times 1
-matrices, respectively.
∎

Let us now recall the notion of weak convergence of probability measures. To this end, let

E
be a Banach space,
(\mathrm{P}_{n})
be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
, and
\mathrm{P}

be another probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
. Then
(\mathrm{P}_{n})
converges weakly to
\mathrm{P}
, if for all
bounded and continuous
f:E\to\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{E}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{n}\to\int_{E}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,.

(C.1)

Like in the finite dimensional case it turns out that the limit
\mathrm{P}
is unique, see e.g.
[
59
, Chapter I.3.7]
.
Moreover, we say that
a sequence
(X_{n})
of
E
-valued random variables converges to an
E
-valued random
variable
X
in distribution, if the distributions of
(X_{n})
converge weakly to the distribution of
X
.

The following result, whose implication
iii)

\Rightarrow

i)
is
known as Lévy’s continuity theorem relates convergence in distribution to
pointwise convergence of the characteristic functions.

Theorem C.2
.

Let
(X_{n})
be a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variables and
X
be another
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable. Then the following statements are equivalent:

i)

We have
X_{n}\to X
in distribution.

ii)

For all
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|a\|_{2}=1
we have
\langle a,X_{n}\rangle\to\langle a,X\rangle
in distribution.

iii)

We have
\varphi_{X_{n}}(t)\to\varphi_{X}(t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof of Theorem
C.2
.

i)

\Rightarrow

iii)
. Follows immediately from the definition of convergence in distribution and from the form of characteristic functions.

iii)

\Rightarrow

i)
. See, for example,
[
18
, Theorem 9.8.2]
or
[
34
, Theorem 15.23]
.

ii)

\Leftrightarrow

iii)
. We first note that
\langle a,X_{n}\rangle\to\langle a,X\rangle
in distribution is equivalent to
\varphi_{\langle a,X_{n}\rangle}(s)\to\varphi_{\langle a,X\rangle}(s)

for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
by the already established equivalence of
i)
and
iii)
. Now the assertion
quickly follows by applying the first formula of Lemma
C.1
.
∎

The next result applies Theorem
C.2
to
\mathbb{R}
-valued Gaussian random variables.

Theorem C.3
.

Let
(X_{n})
be a sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-valued Gaussian random variables and
X
be an
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable with

X_{n}\to X
in distribution. Then
X
is a Gaussian random variable and we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}X_{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{E}X\,,

\displaystyle\var X_{n}

\displaystyle\to\var X\,.

Proof of Theorem
C.3
.

Let us write
\mu_{n}:=\mathbb{E}X_{n}
and
\sigma_{n}:=\sqrt{\var X_{n}}
. By Theorem
C.2

we then know that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\mu_{n}}\cdot\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}t^{2}}{2}\Bigr)=\varphi_{X_{n}}(t)\to\varphi_{X}(t)\,,\qquad\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

(C.2)

Taking the absolute value in
\mathbb{C}
yields

\displaystyle\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\sigma_{n}^{2}t^{2}}{2}\Bigr)=|\varphi_{X_{n}}(t)|\to|\varphi_{X}(t)|=:a_{t}\,,\qquad\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

(C.3)

Obviously, we have
a_{0}=|\varphi_{X}(t)|=1
.
Since
\varphi_{X}
is uniformly continuous, see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 15.21]
, there thus exists a
t_{0}>0
such that
a_{t}>0
for all
t\in[-t_{0},t_{0}]
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{n}\to\sigma^{2}:=-\frac{2}{t_{0}^{2}}\,\ln(a_{t_{0}})\,,

(C.4)

and in particular, we find
b:=1+\sup_{n}\sigma_{n}<\infty
.

Our next step is to show that
(\mu_{n})
is a bounded sequence. To this end,
let us first assume that we had a subsequence
\mu_{n_{k}}\to\infty
.
Since
\mathrm{P}_{X}
is a probability
measure there exists an
a>0
with
\mathrm{P}_{X}([-a,a])\geq 1/2
.
Clearly, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mu_{n_{k}}>a+1
for all
k\geq 1
.
Let us fix a continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
with
f(t)=1
for all
t\in[-a,a]
and
f(t)=0
for all

t\not\in[-a-1,a+1]
. By the assumed convergence
X_{n}\to X
in distribution, we then obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X_{n_{k}}}\to\int_{\mathbb{R}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}\geq\int_{[-a,a]}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X}\geq 1/2\,.

(C.5)

To find a contradiction, let us first assume that there is an
n_{k}
with

\sigma_{n_{k}}=0
. This gives
\mathrm{P}_{X_{n_{k}}}=\delta_{\{\mu_{n_{k}}\}}
, and
since
\mu_{n_{k}}>a+1

our construction of
f
would then yield

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X_{n_{k}}}=f(\mu_{n_{k}})=0\,.

In view of (
C.5
) we thus conclude that there exists a
k_{0}\geq 1
with

\sigma_{n_{k}}>0
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
For such
k
, however, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X_{n_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{n_{k}}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(t-\mu_{n_{k}})^{2}}{2\sigma_{n_{k}}^{2}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{n_{k}}^{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t+\mu_{n_{k}})\exp\Bigl(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma_{n_{k}}^{2}}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\sigma_{n_{k}}t+\mu_{n_{k}})\exp\Bigl(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\sigma_{n_{k}}^{-1}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})}f(\sigma_{n_{k}}t+\mu_{n_{k}})\exp\Bigl(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq{\cal N}(0,1)\bigl((-\infty,\sigma_{n_{k}}^{-1}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})]\bigr)\,,

where in the second to last step we used that
\sigma_{n_{k}}t+\mu_{n_{k}}\leq a+1
is equivalent to
t\leq\sigma^{-1}_{n_{k}}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})
.
Now observe that the definition of
b
implies
\sigma_{n_{k}}\leq b
and thus

\sigma_{n_{k}}^{-1}\geq b^{-1}>0
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Since we also have

a+1-\mu_{n_{k}}<0
for all
k\geq 1
, we thus conclude

\displaystyle\sigma_{n_{k}}^{-1}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})\leq b^{-1}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})

for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Consequently, the estimation above can be continued to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{X_{n_{k}}}\leq{\cal N}(0,1)\bigl((-\infty,\sigma_{n_{k}}^{-1}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})]\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq{\cal N}(0,1)\bigl((-\infty,b^{-1}(a+1-\mu_{n_{k}})]\bigr)

\displaystyle\to 0

for
k\to\infty
, where in the last step we used
\mu_{n_{k}}\to\infty
.
Clearly, this contradicts (
C.5
) and hence there is no
subsequence
\mu_{n_{k}}\to\infty
. Analogously, we find that there is no subsequence

\mu_{n_{k}}\to-\infty
. In other words,
(\mu_{n})
is a bounded sequence.

Now, since
(\mu_{n})
is a bounded sequence, there exist a
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and a subsequence
(\mu_{n_{k}})

with
\mu_{n_{k}}\to\mu
, and this in turn yields

\displaystyle\varphi_{X_{n_{k}}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\mu_{n_{k}}}\cdot\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\sigma_{n_{k}}^{2}t^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\to\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t\mu}\cdot\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\sigma^{2}t^{2}}{2}\Bigr)

(C.6)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since the limit function is the characteristic function of
{\cal N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})

we see by (
C.2
) and the uniqueness of the characteristic function
that
\mathrm{P}_{X}={\cal N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
, that is,
X
is indeed a Gaussian random variable.

In addition,
\var X_{n}\to\var X

immediately follows from (
C.4
).
Finally, we already know that
\mathbb{E}X_{n_{k}}=\mu_{n_{k}}\to\mu=\mathbb{E}X
.
If, however, the full sequence
(\mathbb{E}X_{n})
did not converge to
\mathbb{E}X
, then there would be another subsequence
(\mu_{m_{k}})

and an
\varepsilon>0
with
|\mu_{m_{k}}-\mu|\geq\varepsilon
for all
k\geq 1
. Since
(\mu_{n})
is bounded, so is

(\mu_{m_{k}})
, and hence there would exist a subsequence of
(\mu_{m_{k}})
converging to some
\tilde{\mu}\in\mathbb{R}
. Our construction
ensures
\mu\neq\tilde{\mu}
and repeating the arguments around (
C.6
) would give us

\mathrm{P}_{X}={\cal N}(\tilde{\mu},\sigma^{2})
. This, however, is
impossible by the uniqueness of the weak limit of probability measures, see discussion below (
C.1
).
∎

Let us now turn to general
E
-valued Gaussian random variables.
The first result in this direction shows that the Gaussianity of an
E
-valued random variable can be checked with the help
of
w^{*}
-dense subsets
\mathfrak{D}
of
B_{E}^{\prime}
, where we recall from Theorem
A.1
that there exist such sets that are even countable.

Theorem C.4
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space and
\mathfrak{D}\subset B_{E^{\prime}}
be
w^{*}
-dense in
B_{E^{\prime}}
.
Then a probability measure
\mathrm{P}
on
\mathfrak{B}(E)=\sigma(E^{\prime})

is a Gaussian measure, if and only if the image measure
\mathrm{P}_{x^{\prime}}

is a Gaussian measure on
\mathfrak{B}
for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
.

Moreover, an
E
-valued random variable
X
is a Gaussian random variable, if and only if

\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued Gaussian random variable for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
.

Proof of Theorem
C.4
.

Let us first consider the case of an
E
-valued random variable
X:\Omega\to E
.
We begin by assuming that
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
is a Gaussian random variable for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}
.
Let us now fix an
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. Since the relative
w^{*}
-topology of
B_{E^{\prime}}
is metrizable, see Theorem
A.1
,
there then exists a sequence
(x^{\prime}_{n})\subset\mathfrak{D}
with
\langle x_{n}^{\prime},x\rangle\to\langle x^{\prime},x\rangle
for all
x\in E
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\langle x_{n}^{\prime},X(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x^{\prime},X(\omega)\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega,

and this in turn shows that
\langle x_{n}^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
in distribution.
Now, all
\langle x^{\prime}_{n},X\rangle
are
\mathbb{R}
-valued Gaussian random variables by assumption, and consequently Theorem
C.3

ensures that
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle
is also a Gaussian random variable. Consequently,
X
is a Gaussian random variable. The converse implication is trivial.

Finally, the assertion for Gaussian measures follows from considering the random variable
X:=\id_{E}:E\to E
on
the probability space
(E,\sigma(E^{\prime}),\mathrm{P})
, since in this case we have
\mathrm{P}_{X}=\mathrm{P}
and
\mathrm{P}_{\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}=\mathrm{P}_{x^{\prime}}
.
∎

By definition, every one-dimensional projection
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}
of a centered
Gaussian random variable is a centered Gaussian random variable, and it is obvious that
the set of these collections form a linear space. This motivates the following definition taken from
[
31
]
.

Definition C.5
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space and
H\subset{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
. Then
H
is called a Gaussian Hilbert space, if

H
is a closed subspace of
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
and every
h\in H
is a centered Gaussian random variable.

The next result connects Gaussian Hilbert spaces to Gaussian random variables.

Lemma C.6
.

Let
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
E
be a separable Banach space, and
X:\Omega\to E
be a Gaussian random variable.
Then

\displaystyle{\cal G}(X):=\overline{\bigl\{\langle x^{\prime},X-\mathbb{E}X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}:x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}\bigr\}}^{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}

is a Gaussian Hilbert space and each
\mathrm{P}
-equivalence class in
{\cal G}(X)
has a
\sigma(X)
-measurable representative.
In addition, if
F
is another separable Banach space, then the following statements hold true:

i)

If
A:E\to F
is a bounded and linear operator, then
{\cal G}(A\circ X)\subset{\cal G}(X)
.

ii)

If
Y:\Omega\to F
is a random variable such that
X
and
Y
are jointly Gaussian random variables, then

\displaystyle{\cal G}(X,Y):={\cal G}((X,Y))=\overline{{\cal G}(X)+{\cal G}(Y)}^{{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}}\,.

Proof of Lemma
C.6
.

Without loss of generality we assume that
X
and
Y
are centered.

Now,
[
31
, Example 1.8]
shows that
{\cal G}(X)
is a Gaussian
Hilbert space. However, this also directly follows from the observation that
{\cal G}(X)
is a closed subspace of
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
by construction
and from the fact that
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
-limits of centered Gaussian random variables are centered Gaussian random variables, see also Theorem
C.3
.

Let us now find a
\sigma(X)
-measurable representative. Clearly,
each
\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{\cal G}(X)
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable, and therefore there is nothing to prove for the
\mathrm{P}
-equivalence classes
of these functions. Let us now fix an arbitrary
f\in{\cal G}(X)
, so that our goal is to find a
\sigma(X)
-measurable
g:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathrm{P}(\{f\neq g\})=0
. To this end, we first note that by the definition of
{\cal G}(X)
, there exists a sequence
(x^{\prime}_{n})\subset E^{\prime}
with

f_{n}:=\langle x^{\prime}_{n},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\to f
in
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
. Since there exists a
\mathrm{P}
-almost surely converging subsequence, we may assume without loss
of generality that we actually have the
\mathrm{P}
-almost sure convergence
f_{n}\to f
.
Moreover, each
f_{n}
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable, and therefore we have

\displaystyle A:=\bigl\{\omega\in\Omega:\exists\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(\omega)\bigr\}\in\sigma(X)\,,

see e.g.
[
9
, Lemma 2.1.7]
. In addition, the
\mathrm{P}
-almost sure convergence
f_{n}\to f
shows
\mathrm{P}(A)=1
.
We define
g_{n}:=\boldsymbol{1}_{A}f_{n}
and
g:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
by
g(\omega):=\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(\omega)
for
\omega\in A
and
g(\omega):=0
for
\omega\in\Omega\setminus A
.
Since each
g_{n}
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable and
g_{n}(\omega)\to g(\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
we then see that

g
is
\sigma(X)
-measurable. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}(\{f\neq g\})=\mathrm{P}(A\cap\{f\neq g\})+\mathrm{P}\bigl((\Omega\setminus A)\cap\{f\neq g\})\bigr)=\mathrm{P}(A\cap\{f\neq\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{n}\})=0\,,

and hence we have found the desired
\sigma(X)
-measurable representative
g
of the
\mathrm{P}
-equivalence class of
f
.

i).
For
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
we have
x^{\prime}:=y^{\prime}\circ A\in E^{\prime}
, and this yields

\displaystyle\langle y^{\prime},A\circ X\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}=y^{\prime}\circ A\circ X=\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}\in{\cal G}(X)\,.

In other words we have
\{\langle y^{\prime},A\circ X\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}:y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}\}\subset{\cal G}(X)
and
taking the
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
-closure then yields the assertion.

ii).
For the inclusion “
\supset
” we consider the coordinate
projection
\pi_{E}:E\times F\to E
. Then we have
\pi_{E}(X,Y)=X
and therefore

i)
gives
{\cal G}(X)={\cal G}(\pi_{E}(X,Y))\subset{\cal G}(X,Y)
. Since we analogously find

{\cal G}(Y)\subset{\cal G}(X,Y)
, we conclude that
{\cal G}(X)+{\cal G}(Y)\subset{\cal G}(X,Y)
. Taking the
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
-closure
then yields the desired inclusion since
{\cal G}(X,Y)
is a closed linear subspace of
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
.

To prove the converse inclusion,
we first recall that
E^{\prime}\times F^{\prime}\to(E\times F)^{\prime}
defined by

(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})(x,y):=x^{\prime}(x)+y^{\prime}(y)
for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
,
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
,
x\in E
, and
y\in F

is an isomorphism.
If we now
fix an
z^{\prime}\in(E\times F)^{\prime}
,
there then exist
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
such that

z^{\prime}(x,y)=x^{\prime}(x)+y^{\prime}(y)

for all
x\in E
and
y\in F
, and this in turn implies

\displaystyle\langle z^{\prime},(X,Y)\rangle_{(E\times F)^{\prime},E\times F}=\langle x^{\prime},X\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}+\langle y^{\prime},Y\rangle_{F^{\prime},F}\in{\cal G}(X)+{\cal G}(Y)\,.

Taking the
{L_{2}(\mathrm{P})}
-closure thus gives the inclusion “
\subset
”.
∎

Similar to the finite dimensional case, a Gaussian measure
\mathrm{P}
on a separable Banach space
E
has a
characteristic function that is solely described by its mean and covariance.
The following result, which can be found in e.g.
[
59
, Proposition 2.8 in Chapter IV.2.4]
, provides the details, where
we refer to Definition
B.1
for the notion of
abstract covariance operators.

Proposition C.7
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space and
\mathrm{P}
be a probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
. Then the following statements are equivalent:

i)

\mathrm{P}
is a Gaussian measure.

ii)

There exist a
\mu\in E
and an abstract covariance operator
K:E^{\prime}\to E
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathrm{P}}(x^{\prime})=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle x^{\prime},\mu\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}\cdot\exp\biggl(-\frac{\langle x^{\prime},Kx^{\prime}\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}}{2}\biggr)\,,\qquad\qquad x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}\,.

(C.7)

In this case,
\mu
is the mean of
\mathrm{P}
and
K
is the covariance operator of
\mathrm{P}
.

Our final goal is to generalize Theorem
C.2
to the infinite dimensional case. To this end,
let
E
be a separable Banach space. Then a
set
{\cal M}
of probability measures on
(E,\mathfrak{B}(E))
is called uniformly tight, if for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
a compact
K\subset E
with
\mu(E\setminus K)\leq\varepsilon
for all
\mu\in{\cal M}
.
To quickly discuss the definition recall
that
given a probability measure
\mu
on
(E,\mathfrak{B}(E))
, Ulam’s theorem, see e.g.
[
6
, Lemma 26.2]
or

[
59
, Theorem 3.1 in Chapter I.3.2]
,
shows that
\mu
is a Radon measure in the sense of inner regularity, that is

\displaystyle\mu(B)=\sup\{\mu(K):K\subset B\mbox{ and $K$ is compact}\}

(C.8)

for all
B\in\mathfrak{B}(E)
. In particular,
\{\mu\}
is (uniformly) tight.
Here we emphasize that we use the term “Radon measure” in the sense of
[
59
, p. 28]
,
[
8
, p. 97]
and
[
10
, Definition 7.1.1]
,
as we will need this definition also in the proof of Theorem
C.9
below when applying results from
[
59
]
and
[
8
]
.
By (
C.8
) we conclude that every finite set
{\cal M}
of probability measures on
(E,\mathfrak{B}(E))
is uniformly tight.
Similarly, the finite union of uniformly tight sets is also uniformly tight.

Before we generalize Theorem
C.2
, we also like to recall a characterization of uniform tightness
from
[
10
, Example 8.6.5]
.
In view of our formulation below we note that
[
10
, Example 8.6.5]
additionally lists the
condition

\mu(\{V=\infty\})=0
for all
\mu\in{\cal M}
. However, this condition is implied by the finiteness
of the integrals in
ii)
, and hence we omitted it.

Lemma C.8
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space and
{\cal M}
be a set of probability measures on
(E,\mathfrak{B}(E))
.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

i)

{\cal M}
is uniformly tight.

ii)

There exists a
\mathfrak{B}(E)
-measurable
V:E\to[0,\infty]
such that
\{V\leq c\}
is compact for all
c\in[0,\infty)
and

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu\in{\cal M}}\int_{E}V\,\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty\,.

Finally, we present the announced generalization of Theorem
C.2
to the infinite dimensional case.

Theorem C.9
.

Let
E
be a separable Banach space and
(\mathrm{P}_{n})
be a sequence of probability measures on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
such that the following assumptions are satisfied:

i)

The set
\{\mathrm{P}_{n}:n\geq 1\}
is uniformly tight.

ii)

There exists a function
\varphi:E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\varphi_{\mathrm{P}_{n}}(x^{\prime})\to\varphi(x^{\prime})
for all
x^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.

Then there exists a probability measure
\mathrm{P}
on
E
such
\mathrm{P}_{n}\to\mathrm{P}
weakly and
\varphi_{\mathrm{P}}=\varphi
.

Proof of Theorem
C.9
.

We will apply
[
59
, Theorem 3.1 in Chapter IV.3.1]
for
\Gamma:=E^{\prime}
. Here we first note that Hahn-Banach’s theorem ensures that
E^{\prime}
is
a separating set consisting of continuous functions
E\to\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, every probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}(E)
is a Radon probability measure, see
the references around (
C.8
).
Finally,
[
8
, Theorem 3.8.4]
in combination with
i)
ensures that

\{\mathrm{P}_{n}:n\geq 1\}
is relatively weakly compact since the underlying space
E
is a metric space, and thus a completely regular topological space,
where the latter notion can be found in e.g.
[
8
, Appendix A.1 on page 363]
.
Now the assertion follows from

[
59
, Theorem 3.1 in Chapter IV.3.1]
.
∎

D
Regular Conditional Probabilities

In this supplement we collect some useful properties of regular conditional probabilities and their
relationship to Banach space valued conditional expectations.

We begin with a general statement that ensures the existence and uniqueness of
regular conditional probabilities. For its formulation recall
that a topological space
(T,{\cal T})
is called Polish, if there exists
a metric
\metric:T\times T\to[0,\infty)
generating the topology
{\cal T}
, such that
(T,\metric)
is
complete and separable. Obviously, separable Banach spaces are Polish spaces.
Now, the following result, which can be found in e.g.
[
5
, Theorem 44.3]
,

[
18
, Theorem 10.2.2]
, and

[
34
, Theorem 8.37]
, shows that the
existence and uniqueness of regular conditional probabilities can be guaranteed as long as the space

T
in Definition
2.2

is a Polish space and
{\cal B}
is the corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebra.

Theorem D.1
.

Let
(T,{\cal T})
be a Polish space with Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
(U,{\cal A})
be a measurable space, and

\mathrm{P}
be a probability measure on
(T\times U,\mathfrak{B}\otimes{\cal A})
. Then the following statements hold true:

i)

There exists a
regular conditional probability
\mathrm{P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):\mathfrak{B}\times U\to[0,1]
of
\mathrm{P}
given
U
.

ii)

If
\mathrm{P}_{1}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
and
\mathrm{P}_{2}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
are regular conditional probabilities of
\mathrm{P}
given
U
,
then there exists an
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}_{U}(N)=0
such that for all
B\in\mathfrak{B}
and
u\in U\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{1}(B|u)=\mathrm{P}_{2}(B|u)\,.

Our next result describes how regular conditional probabilities of image measures can be computed
