with the help of regular conditional probabilities of the initial measure.

Theorem D.2
.

For
i=0,1
, let
(T_{i},{\cal T}_{i})
be Polish spaces with Borel
\sigma
-algebras
\mathfrak{B}_{i}
,

(U_{i},{\cal A}_{i})
be measurable spaces,
and
\mathrm{P}^{(i)}
be probability measures on
(T_{i}\times U_{i},\mathfrak{B}_{i}\otimes{\cal A}_{i})
.
Moreover, let
\Phi:T_{0}\to T_{1}
and
\Psi:U_{0}\to U_{1}
be measurable maps, such that we have

\displaystyle\sigma(\Psi)={\cal A}_{0}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\mathrm{P}^{(1)}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{(\Phi,\Psi)}\,.

Then the following statements hold true:

i)

We have
\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{U_{1}}=(\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}})_{\Psi}
.

ii)

For all regular conditional probabilities
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}
given
U_{0}
and
\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of
\mathrm{P}^{(1)}
given
U_{1}

there exists an
N\in{\cal A}_{0}
with
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}(N)=0
such that for all
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{(0)}\bigl((\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})|u_{0}\bigr)=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}\bigl(B_{1}|\Psi(u_{0})\bigr)\,,\qquad\qquad u_{0}\in U_{0}\setminus N.

(D.1)

Proof of Theorem
D.2
.

We begin by making some initial observations. To this end,
we write
\Upsilon:=(\Phi,\Psi):T_{0}\times U_{0}\to T_{1}\times U_{1}
.
A simple calculation then shows

\displaystyle\pi_{T_{1}}\circ\Upsilon=\Phi\circ\pi_{T_{0}}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\pi_{U_{1}}\circ\Upsilon=\Psi\circ\pi_{U_{0}}\,,

where
\pi_{T_{i}}
and
\pi_{U_{i}}
are the coordinate projections for
i=0,1
.
Since
\Phi\circ\pi_{T_{0}}
and
\Psi\circ\pi_{U_{0}}
are measurable, we then see that

\Upsilon
is measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-algebras, see e.g.
[
34
, Corollary 1.82]
, and in particular our assumption

\mathrm{P}^{(1)}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{(\Phi,\Psi)}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{\Upsilon}
makes sense.
For
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}
and
A_{1}\in{\cal A}_{1}
this in turn implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}\times A_{1})=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\Upsilon^{-1}(B_{1}\times A_{1}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})\times\Psi^{-1}(A_{1})\bigr)\,.

(D.2)

i).

Our initial observations together with the standard
formula
for image measures of composed transformations imply

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{U_{1}}=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{\pi_{U_{1}}}=(\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{\Upsilon})_{\pi_{U_{1}}}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{\pi_{U_{1}}\circ\Upsilon}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{\Psi\circ\pi_{U_{0}}}=(\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{\pi_{U_{0}}})_{\Psi}=(\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}})_{\Psi}\,.

ii).

For
A_{0}\in{\cal A}_{0}
and
A_{1}\in{\cal A}_{1}
with
\Psi^{-1}(A_{1})=A_{0}
and
measurable
g:U_{1}\to[0,\infty)
an application of
the transformation formula
for integrals of image measures, see e.g.
[
14
, Proposition 2.6.8]

yields

\displaystyle\int_{U_{0}}\!\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}\cdot(g\circ\Psi)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}=\int_{U_{0}}\!(\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{1}}\circ\Psi)\cdot(g\circ\Psi)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}})

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{1}}\!\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{1}}\cdot g\,\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}})_{\Psi}

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{1}}\!\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{1}}\cdot g\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{U_{1}}\,,

(D.3)

where in the last step we used the already established
i)
.
Applying (
D.3
) to
g:=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}|\,\cdot\,)
shows

\displaystyle\int_{U_{0}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}(u_{0})\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}|\Psi(u_{0}))\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{1}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{1}}(u_{1})\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}|u_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(1)}_{U_{1}}(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}\times A_{1})\,.

(D.4)

Moreover, for a regular conditional probability

\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,)
of
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}
given
U_{0}
our observation (
D.2
) gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}\times A_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})\times A_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{0}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}(u_{0})\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})|u_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}(u_{0})\,.

(D.5)

To motivate the next steps we note that combining (
D.4
) and (
D.5
) gives
the
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}
-almost sure identity

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})|\,\cdot\,)=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}|\Psi(\,\cdot\,))\,.

However, the occurring
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}
-zero set depends on
B_{1}
, and hence
we need to work harder to establish the assertion.

Let us therefore
define the probability measure
\mathrm{Q}:=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{(\Phi,\id_{U_{0}})}
on
\mathfrak{B}_{1}\otimes{\cal A}_{0}
.
For
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}
,
A_{0}\in{\cal A}_{0}
, and
A_{1}\in{\cal A}_{1}
with
\Psi^{-1}(A_{1})=A_{0}
the calculation (
D.2
) then shows

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}(B_{1}\times A_{0})=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}\bigl(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})\times A_{0}\bigr)=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}\times A_{1})\,.

(D.6)

Moreover, we have
\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}(A_{0})=\mathrm{Q}(T_{1}\times A_{0})=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(T_{0}\times A_{0})=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}(A_{0})
, that is

\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}
.

With these preparations let us first
consider the map

\mathrm{Q}_{1}:\mathfrak{B}_{1}\times U_{0}\to[0,1]
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}_{1}(B_{1},u_{0}):=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}|\Psi(u_{0}))\,.

Obviously,
\mathrm{Q}_{1}(B_{1},\,\cdot\,):U_{0}\to[0,1]
is
{\cal A}_{0}
-measurable for all
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}

and
\mathrm{Q}_{1}(\,\cdot\,,u_{0}):\mathfrak{B}_{1}\to[0,1]
is a probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
for all
u_{0}\in U_{0}
.
Let us fix some
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}
and
A_{0}\in{\cal A}_{0}
.
Since we assumed

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{0}=\sigma(\Psi)=\{\Psi^{-1}(A_{1}):A_{1}\in{\cal A}_{1}\}\,,

where the latter identity is the very definition of
\sigma(\Psi)
, there then exists an
A_{1}\in{\cal A}_{1}

with
\Psi^{-1}(A_{1})=A_{0}
. Combining the observed
\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}
with
(
D.4
) and (
D.6
)
then yields

\displaystyle\int_{U_{0}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}(u_{0})\mathrm{Q}_{1}(B_{1},u_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{0}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}(u_{0})\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}|\Psi(u_{0}))\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}\times A_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Q}(B_{1}\times A_{0})\,.

In other words,
\mathrm{Q}_{1}
is a regular conditional probability of
\mathrm{Q}
given
U_{0}
.

Besides
\mathrm{Q}_{1}
, we also
consider the map
\mathrm{Q}_{0}:\mathfrak{B}_{1}\times U_{0}\to[0,1]
given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}_{0}(B_{1},u_{0}):=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})|u_{0})\,.

Again,
\mathrm{Q}_{0}(B_{1},\,\cdot\,):U_{0}\to[0,1]
is
{\cal A}_{0}
-measurable for all
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}

and
\mathrm{Q}_{0}(\,\cdot\,,u_{0}):\mathfrak{B}_{1}\to[0,1]
is a probability measure on
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
for all
u_{0}\in U_{0}
, as it is
the image measure of
\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\,\cdot\,|u_{0})
under
\Phi
.
Let us fix some
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}_{1}
and
A_{0}\in{\cal A}_{0}
. Again there then exists
an
A_{1}\in{\cal A}_{1}

with
\Psi^{-1}(A_{1})=A_{0}
and by combining
\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}

with
(
D.5
) and (
D.6
)
we find

\displaystyle\int_{U_{0}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}(u_{0})\mathrm{Q}_{0}(B_{1},u_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{0}}\boldsymbol{1}_{A_{0}}(u_{0})\mathrm{P}^{(0)}(\Phi^{-1}(B_{1})|u_{0})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{P}^{(1)}(B_{1}\times A_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Q}(B_{1}\times A_{0})\,.

In other words,
\mathrm{Q}_{0}
is also a regular conditional probability of
\mathrm{Q}
given
U_{0}
.
Now the uniqueness of regular conditional probabilities, see Theorem
D.1
, yields the assertion, since we have
already noted
\mathrm{Q}_{U_{0}}=\mathrm{P}^{(0)}_{U_{0}}
.
∎

Our next goal is to investigate Bochner integrals with respect to regular conditional
probabilities and how they relate to Banach space valued conditional expectations.
Let us begin by introducing the latter.
To this end, let
E
be a Banach space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space, and

{\cal C}\subset{\cal A}
be a sub-
\sigma
-algebra.
We denote the restriction of
\mathrm{P}
to
{\cal C}
by
\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}}
and write accordingly

\displaystyle{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}},E)}:=\bigl\{Z\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}:Z:\Omega\to E\mbox{ is $({\cal C},\mathfrak{B}(E))$-measurable }\bigr\}\,,

With this notation, we can now define conditional expectations.

Definition D.3
.

Let
E
be a Banach space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
{\cal C}\subset{\cal A}
be a
sub-
\sigma
-algebra, and
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
. Then

Z\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}},E)}
is called a conditional expectation of
X
given
{\cal C}
, if

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}Z\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}}=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,\qquad\qquad C\in{\cal C}.

One can show that for each
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
there exists a
conditional expectation of
X
given
{\cal C}
,
see e.g.
[
59
, Proposition 4.1 in Chapter II.4.1]
or
[
29
, Theorem 2.6.20]
.
Moreover, if we have two conditional expectations
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
of
X
given
{\cal C}
, then

\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}}(\{Z_{1}\neq Z_{2}\})=0
,
see e.g.
[
17
, Corollary 5 in Chapter II.2]
or
[
29
, Theorem 2.6.20]
. As in the case
E=\mathbb{R}
, we thus
write
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
for any version of the conditional expectation of
X
given
{\cal C}
.
Finally, in the case
{\cal C}=\sigma(Y)
, we write, as usual,
\mathbb{E}(X|Y):=\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
.

Moreover,
note that
for
X_{1},X_{2}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
with
\mathrm{P}(\{X_{1}\neq X_{2}\})=0
we obviously have

\mathbb{E}(X_{1}|{\cal C})(\omega)=\mathbb{E}(X_{2}|{\cal C})(\omega)
for
\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}}
-almost all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Consequently we can view the conditional expectation as a map
{L_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}\to{L_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}},E)}
.

The next results collects some useful properties of conditional expectations, where we note that most of them
are literal generalizations of well-known properties in the case
E=\mathbb{R}
. For a proof we refer to

[
59
, Chapter II.4.1]
or
[
29
, Chapter 2.6.d]
.

Theorem D.4
.

Let
E
be a Banach space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space, and
{\cal C}\subset{\cal A}
be a
sub-
\sigma
-algebra. Then the following statements hold true:

i)

The map
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|{\cal C}):{L_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}\to{L_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}},E)}
is linear and bounded
with
\|\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|{\cal C})\|=1
and
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})=X
for all
X\in{L_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}},E)}
.

ii)

For all
1\leq p\leq\infty
the map
\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|{\cal C}):{L_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}\to{L_{p}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}},E)}
is well-defined, linear, and
continuous with
\|\mathbb{E}(\,\cdot\,|{\cal C})\|=1
.

iii)

For all sub-
\sigma
-algebras
{\cal C}_{1}\subset{\cal C}_{2}\subset{\cal A}
and all
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}

we have
\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C}_{2})|{\cal C}_{1})=\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C}_{1})
.

iv)

If
T:E\to F
is bounded and linear, then
\mathbb{E}(T\circ X|{\cal C})=T\circ\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
for all
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
.

v)

We have
\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C}))=\mathbb{E}X
for all
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
.

Our next goal is to collect information about
E
-valued martingales. To this end, let

(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space and
E
be a Banach space.
Then a sequence
({\cal C}_{n})
of
sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
{\cal A}
is said to be a filtration, if
{\cal C}_{n}\subset{\cal C}_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 1
.
In this case a sequence
(X_{n})
of maps
X_{n}:\Omega\to E
is called a
({\cal C}_{n})
-martingale, if for all
n\geq 1
we have

X_{n}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{|{\cal C}_{n}},E)}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(X_{n+1}|{\cal C}_{n})=X_{n}\,.

Recall that
\mathbb{R}
-valued martingales enjoy well-known convergence results. The following theorem, whose proof can be
found in e.g.
[
59
, Theorems 4.1 and 4.2 in Chapter II.4.2]

or
[
29
, Theorem 3.3.2]
, collects analogous results for
E
-valued martingales.

Theorem D.5
.

Let
E
be a Banach space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
({\cal C}_{n})
be a filtration,
and
{\cal C}_{\infty}:=\sigma({\cal C}_{n}:n\geq 1)
. Moreover, let
1\leq p<\infty
and
X\in{{\cal L}_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}
, and for
n\geq 1
we
define

\displaystyle X_{n}:=\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C}_{n})\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad X_{\infty}:=\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C}_{\infty})\,.

Then
(X_{n})
is a
({\cal C}_{n})
-martingale and we have both

\displaystyle\|X_{n}-X_{\infty}\|_{{{\cal L}_{p}(\mathrm{P},E)}}\to 0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\qquad\mathrm{P}(\lim_{n\to\infty}X_{n}\neq X_{\infty}\})=0.

Our next result establishes a disintegration formula for Bochner integrals and regular conditional
probabilities. In addition, it shows that regular conditional probabilities can be used to construct
conditional expectations. In the case
E=\mathbb{R}
, these results are well-known, see
e.g.
[
18
, Theorem 10.2.5]
and to some extent also

[
34
, Theorem 8.38]
, where in all cases actually a more general setting
that goes beyond the “product case” is treated.

Theorem D.6
.

Let
(T,{\cal B})
and
(U,{\cal A})
be measurable spaces,
\mathrm{P}
be a probability measure on
(T\times U,{\cal B}\otimes{\cal A})
,
and

\mathrm{P}(\,\cdot\,|\,\cdot\,):{\cal B}\times U\to[0,1]
be a regular conditional probability
of
\mathrm{P}
given
U
. Moreover, let
E
be a Banach space and
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
.
Then there exists an
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}_{U}(N)=0
such that for all
u\in U\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle X(\,\cdot\,,u)\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}(\,\cdot\,|u),E)}\,.

(D.7)

Moreover, for all
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}_{U}(N)=0
and (
D.7
),
the map
Z:U\to E
defined by

\displaystyle Z(u):=\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{U\setminus N}(u)X(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,,\qquad\qquad u\in U.

(D.8)

is strongly measurable
and we have both
Z\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{U},E)}
and

\displaystyle\int_{T\times U}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{U}Z\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}=\int_{U}\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{U\setminus N}(u)X(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)\,.

(D.9)

Finally, the function
Z\circ\pi_{U}:T\times U\to E
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
, where
{\cal C}:=\pi_{U}^{-1}({\cal A})
.

Proof of Theorem
D.6
.

We first note that for
X:T\times U\to[0,\infty)
the assertions (
D.7
) and (
D.9
) are well-known: Indeed,
in this case, the measurability of
Z:U\to[0,\infty]
defined by (
D.8
)
with
N:=\emptyset

can be found in e.g.
[
34
, Lemma 14.20]
. Moreover, (
D.9
)
is shown in e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 14.29]
.
For
\mathrm{P}
-integrable
X:T\times U\to[0,\infty)
, the just verified
(
D.9
) then shows that there exists an
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}_{U}(N)=0
and

Z(u)\in[0,\infty)
for all
u\in U\setminus N
, that is, we have (
D.7
).
Finally, if we fix such an
N
and
apply the previous results to
X_{0}:=\boldsymbol{1}_{U\setminus N}X
,
we again have the measurability of
Z
and (
D.9
).

Let us now assume that
E
is a general Banach space. We first consider the case in which

X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
is a step function. To this end, we pick a representation

\displaystyle X=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}x_{i}

for some
x_{i}\in E
and
D_{i}\in{\cal B}\otimes{\cal A}
.
For
u\in U
we have
D_{i,u}:=\{t\in T:(t,u)\in D_{i}\}\in{\cal B}
, see e.g.
[
34
, Lemma 14.13]
,
and hence we see that

\displaystyle X(\,\cdot\,,u)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}(\,\cdot\,,u)x_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i,u}}x_{i}

(D.10)

is a measurable step functions for all fixed
u\in U
. Consequently, (
D.7
) holds for
N:=\emptyset
.
For this
N
we
define
Z
by (
D.8
).
For
u\in U
the definition of Bochner integrals for step functions, see (
A.3
), yields

\displaystyle Z(u)=\int_{T}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}(t,u)x_{i}\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i,u}}(t)x_{i}\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl(\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i,u}}(t)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\biggr)\cdot x_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl(\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\biggr)\cdot x_{i}\,.

Let us define
f_{i}:U\to[0,\infty)
by

\displaystyle f_{i}(u):=\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,.

From our initial remarks we know that each
f_{i}
is measurable, and hence
u\mapsto f_{i}(u)x_{i}
is a strongly measurable,
E
-valued function.
Since
Z
is the sum of these
m
functions, it is also strongly measurable.
Moreover, the already established (
D.9
) for non-negative, measurable functions gives

\displaystyle\int_{U}Z\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}

\displaystyle=\int_{U}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl(\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\biggr)\cdot x_{i}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl(\int_{U}\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)\biggr)\cdot x_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\biggl(\int_{T\times U}\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{i}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\biggr)\cdot x_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{T\times U}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,.

In other words, (
D.9
) holds for measurable
E
-valued step functions and
N=\emptyset
.

Let us now consider the case of general Banach spaces
E
and
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
.
To prove (
D.7
),
we write
Y:=\|X(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)\|_{E}:T\times U\to[0,\infty)
.
Since
X
is strongly measurable,

Y
is measurable. Applying Equation (
D.9
) in the already established case of
E=\mathbb{R}
with
non-negative functions to
Y
we then obtain

\displaystyle\int_{U}\int_{T}\|X(t,u)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)=\int_{T\times U}\|X(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}<\infty\,.

(D.11)

Consequently, there exists an
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}_{U}(N)=0
such that for all
u\in U\setminus N
we have

\displaystyle\int_{T}\|X(t,u)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)<\infty.

This shows (
D.7
).

For the following steps we fix an
N\in{\cal A}
with
\mathrm{P}_{U}(N)=0
such that (
D.7
) holds.
We write
X_{0}:=\boldsymbol{1}_{U\setminus N}X
and define
g:T\times U\to[0,\infty)
by

g(t,u):=\|X_{0}(t,u)\|_{E}
for all

t\in T
and
u\in U
. By our assumption,
g:T\times U\to[0,\infty)
is
\mathrm{P}
-integrable.

Let us first verify that
Z
is strongly measurable. Since
X
is strongly measurable, so is
X_{0}
, and therefore
there exists a sequence
(X_{n})
of
measurable step functions
X_{n}:T\times U\to E
approximating
X_{0}
in the sense of
(
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
We define
Z_{n}:U\to E
in the sense of (
D.8
), that is

\displaystyle Z_{n}(u):=\int_{T}X_{n}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,,\qquad\qquad u\in U.

(D.12)

Since
X_{n}
is a measurable step function
we already know that this definition is possible and
that
Z_{n}
is strongly measurable. Moreover, for each fixed
u
, the sequence
(X_{n}(\,\cdot\,,u))
consists of
measurable step functions, see (
D.10
), which approximates
X_{0}(\,\cdot\,,u)
in the sense of (
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
By the definition of the Bochner integral (
A.4
) we thus find

\displaystyle Z_{n}(u)=\int_{T}X_{n}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\to\int_{T}X_{0}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)=Z(u)\,,\qquad\qquad u\in U.

(D.13)

In other words,
Z
is the pointwise limit of a sequence of strongly measurable functions, and thus also strongly measurable.

To verify that
Z\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P}_{U},E)}
, we simply note that the norm inequality for Bochner integrals
(
A.6
) together with (
D.11
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{U}\|Z\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}

\displaystyle=\int_{U}\Bigl\|\,\int_{T}\boldsymbol{1}_{U\setminus N}(u)X(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,\Bigr\|_{E}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{U}\int_{T}\|X(t,u)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}(u)

\displaystyle<\infty\,.

To
establish (
D.9
)
for general
X\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}
we again fix a sequence
(X_{n})
of
measurable step functions
X_{n}:T\times U\to E
approximating
X_{0}
in the sense of
(
A.1
) and (
A.2
).
For the strongly measurable functions
Z_{n}:U\to E
defined by (
D.12
) we have already established
(
D.13
).
By (
A.2
) we further find

\displaystyle\|Z_{n}(u)\|_{E}=\Bigl\|\,\int_{T}X_{n}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)\,\Bigr\|_{E}\leq\int_{T}\|X_{n}(t,u)\|_{E}\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{T}g(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)

\displaystyle=:h(u)

for all
u\in U
. Since
g
is
\mathrm{P}
-integrable, the initially considered case of non-negative, measurable functions
shows that
h:U\to[0,\infty]
is
\mathrm{P}_{U}
-integrable. In summary, the sequence
(Z_{n})
together with
Z
and
h
satisfy (
A.7
)
and (
A.8
), and hence an application of the dominated convergence theorem for Bochner integrals
(
A.9
) shows

\displaystyle\int_{U}Z_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}\to\int_{U}Z\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}\,.

(D.14)

Since the sequence
(X_{n})
of
measurable step functions
X_{n}
approximates
X_{0}
in the sense of
(
A.1
) and (
A.2
), the definition of the Bochner integral further shows

\displaystyle\int_{T\times U}X_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\to\int_{T\times U}X_{0}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,.

(D.15)

Moreover, we already know that (
D.9
) holds for all pairs of
X_{n}
and
Z_{n}
,
that is

\displaystyle\int_{U}Z_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}=\int_{T\times U}X_{n}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,,

and therefore
the just established
(
D.14
) and (
D.15
) then yield
(
D.9
) in the general case.

Finally, we need to verify that
Z\circ\pi_{U}
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
.
Here we first note that
Z\circ\pi_{U}
is
{\cal C}
-measurable by construction.
Let us now pick a
C\in{\cal C}
. Then there exists an
A\in{\cal A}
with
C=T\times A
.
Now, the function
X_{A}:=\boldsymbol{1}_{A}X_{0}
satisfies
X_{A}\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\mathrm{P},E)}

and by construction we have (
D.7
) for

N_{A}:=\emptyset
. In addition, we find

\displaystyle Z_{A}(u):=\int_{T}X_{A}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)=\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(u)\int_{T}X_{0}(t,u)\,\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{d}t|u)=\boldsymbol{1}_{A}(u)Z(u)

for all
u\in U
.
From this we can conclude
Z_{A}\circ\pi_{U}=1_{C}\cdot(Z\circ\pi_{U})
, and
applying (
D.9
) to
X_{A}
thus yields

\displaystyle\int_{T\times U}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}X\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{T\times U}X_{A}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}=\int_{U}Z_{A}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{U}=\int_{U}Z_{A}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}_{\pi_{U}}

\displaystyle=\int_{T\times U}Z_{A}\circ\pi_{U}\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle=\int_{T\times U}\boldsymbol{1}_{C}\cdot(Z\circ\pi_{U})\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{P}\,.

Consequently,
Z\circ\pi_{U}
is a version of
\mathbb{E}(X|{\cal C})
.
∎

E
Continuous Gaussian Processes

In this supplement we recall some basics about the relationship between
C(T)
-valued Gaussian random variables and Gaussian processes with continuous paths.
Since we could not find a source that really fits to our purposes, all material presented here is also proven.

We begin with a technical result in the spirit of Krein-Milman’s theorem.

Lemma E.1
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space and
T_{0}\subset T
be dense. Then we have

\displaystyle\overline{\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T_{0}\}}^{w^{*}\mathrm{\!-seq}}=B_{C(T)^{\prime}}\,,

where
\aco A
denotes the absolute convex hull of a set
A
.

Proof of Lemma
E.1
.

We first consider the case
T_{0}=T
. Here, we note that the set of extreme points
\expoints B_{{\cal M}(T)}
of
B_{{\cal M}(T)}
is
given by

\displaystyle\expoints B_{{\cal M}(T)}=\bigl\{\alpha\delta_{\{t\}}:t\in T\mbox{ and }\alpha\in\{-1,1\}\bigr\}\,,

see e.g.
[
15
, Theorem V.8.4]
.
Using the identification (
24
) of
C(T)^{\prime}
with
{\cal M}(T)
and the obvious
\hat{\delta}_{\{t\}}=\delta_{t}

we conclude that the set

\expoints B_{C(T)^{\prime}}
of extreme points of
B_{C(T)^{\prime}}
is
given by

\displaystyle\expoints B_{C(T)^{\prime}}=\bigl\{\alpha\delta_{t}:t\in T\mbox{ and }\alpha\in\{-1,1\}\bigr\}\,.

Krein-Milman’s theorem, see e.g.
[
39
, Corollary 2.10.9]
, thus gives

\displaystyle\overline{\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T\}}^{w^{*}}=\overline{\co\bigl\{\alpha\delta_{t}:t\in T\mbox{ and }\alpha\in\{-1,1\}\bigr\}}^{w^{*}}=B_{C(T)^{\prime}}\,,

where
\co A
denotes the convex hull of a set
A
and the first identity is trivial.
Moreover,
C(T)
is separable, and hence the relative
w^{*}
-topology on
B_{C(T)^{\prime}}
is metrizable. This gives

\displaystyle\overline{\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T\}}^{w^{*}}=\overline{\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T\}}^{w^{*}\mathrm{\!-seq}}\,,

where we note this identity can also be deduced from
[
39
, Corollary 2.7.13]
.

Let us now prove the assertion in the case of a general, dense
T_{0}\subset T
.
To this end, we fix a
\hat{\mu}\in B_{C(T)^{\prime}}
. By the already established case we then find a
sequence

\displaystyle(\hat{\mu}_{n})\subset\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T\}

with
\langle\hat{\mu}_{n},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\to\langle\hat{\mu},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}
for all
f\in C(T)
.
Clearly, each
\hat{\mu}_{n}
is of the form

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\alpha_{i,n}\delta_{t_{i,n}}\,,

(E.1)

where
t_{i,n}\in T
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}|\alpha_{i,n}|\leq 1
. Since
T_{0}\subset T
is dense, we then find
s_{i,n}\in T_{0}

with
\metric(t_{i,n},s_{i,n})\leq 1/n
for all
i=1,\dots,m_{n}
and
n\geq 1
. We define

\displaystyle\hat{\nu}_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\alpha_{i,n}\delta_{s_{i,n}}\,.

Let
us fix an
f\in C(T)
and denote its uniform modulus of continuity by
\omega(f,\,\cdot\,)
, see e.g.
[
1
, page 232]
. Then we have

\displaystyle\bigl|\langle\hat{\mu}_{n}-\hat{\nu}_{n},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\bigr|=\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}\alpha_{i,n}\langle\delta_{t_{i,n}}\!-\!\delta_{s_{i,n}},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}|\alpha_{i,n}||f(t_{i,n})-f(s_{i,n})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m_{n}}|\alpha_{i,n}|\cdot\omega(f,t_{i,n}-s_{i,n})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(f,1/n)\,,

and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\omega(f,1/n)=0
as
f
is uniformly continuous by the compactness of
T
.
This gives

\displaystyle\bigl|\langle\hat{\mu}-\hat{\nu}_{n},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\bigr|\leq\bigl|\langle\hat{\mu}-\hat{\mu}_{n},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\bigr|+\bigl|\langle\hat{\mu}_{n}-\hat{\nu}_{n},f\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\bigr|\to 0\,.

This gives
\hat{\mu}\in\overline{\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T_{0}\}}^{w^{*}\mathrm{\!-seq}}
, and hence we have shown the inclusion
“
\supset
”. The inclusion “
\subset
” follows by monotonicity from the already established case
T=T_{0}
.
∎

To describe the relationship between
C(T)
-valued Gaussian random variables and Gaussian processes with continuous paths, we now fix a
map
X:\Omega\to C(T)
. As in Section
5
we then obtain
a family
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
with
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
by setting

\displaystyle X_{t}(\omega):=\langle\delta_{t},X(\omega)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=(X(\omega))(t)\,,\qquad\qquad\omega\in\Omega.

(E.2)

Obviously, all maps
t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)
are continuous.
Conversely, if we have a family
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
of functions
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that for each
\omega\in\Omega
the path
X_{\bullet}(\omega)
is
continuous, then

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{\bullet}(\omega)

(E.3)

defines a map
X:\Omega\to C(T)
.
Clearly, the operations (
E.2
) and (
E.3
) are inverse to each other.
The following folklore result shows that they map
C(T)
-valued (Gaussian) random variables to (Gaussian) processes with continuous paths and vice versa.

Lemma E.2
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,

X:\Omega\to C(T)
be a map, and

(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be given by (
E.2
). Then we have:

i)

X
is weakly measurable if and only if
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is a stochastic process with continuous paths.

ii)

X
is a Gaussian random variable if and only if
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is a Gaussian process with continuous paths.

Proof of Lemma
E.2
.

i).
If
X
is weakly measurable, then (
E.2
) ensures that
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is a stochastic process with continuous paths.
To show the converse, we
fix a
\mu\in{\cal M}(T)

with Hahn-Jordan decomposition
\mu=\mu^{+}-\mu^{-}
and
we consider the map

\displaystyle\Omega\times T

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

(E.4)

\displaystyle(\omega,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)\,.

Then,
\omega\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)
is measurable for all
t\in T
since we have a stochastic process. In addition,

t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)
is continuous for all
\omega\in\Omega
since the stochastic process has continuous paths.
Combining these observations with the separability of
(T,\metric)
, we conclude by e.g.
[
3
, Lemma 4.51]
that
(
E.4
) is
{\cal A}\otimes\mathfrak{B}(T)
-measurable.
Fubini-Tonelli’s theorem, see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 14.16]
, then shows that
the two functions

\displaystyle\omega\mapsto\langle\mu^{\pm},X(\omega)\rangle_{{\cal M}(T),C(T)}:=\int_{\Omega}X_{t}(\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\pm}(t)

are measurable, and hence
\omega\mapsto\langle\mu,X(\omega)\rangle_{{\cal M}(T),C(T)}
is also measurable. Using the identification of
C(T)^{\prime}
with
{\cal M}(T)
given in
(
24
) we conclude that
X
is weakly measurable.

ii).
Let us first assume that
X
is Gaussian. We fix some
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T
and write
Y:=(X_{t_{1}},\dots,X_{t_{n}})
.
Clearly, our goal is to show that the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued random variable
Y
is Gaussian. To this end, we fix some

a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and write
\hat{\mu}:=a_{1}\delta_{t_{1}}+\dots+a_{n}\delta_{t_{n}}
.
Then a simple calculation using (
E.2
) shows

\displaystyle\langle a,Y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}X_{t_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\langle\delta_{t_{i}},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\langle\hat{\mu},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\,,

(E.5)

and since
X
is a Gaussian random variable, so is
\langle\hat{\mu},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}
, and thus also
\langle a,Y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. Since
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
was chosen arbitrarily,
we conclude that
Y
is Gaussian.

Let us now assume that
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
is a Gaussian process with continuous paths. It suffices to show that
X:\Omega\to C(T)
is Gaussian.
To this end, we consider the set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}:=\aco\{\delta_{t}:t\in T\}\,.

By Lemma
E.1
we know that
\mathfrak{D}
is
w^{*}
-dense in
B_{C(T)^{\prime}}
. By (
24
)
and Theorem
C.4
it therefore
suffices to show that
\langle\hat{\mu},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}
is Gaussian for all
\hat{\mu}\in\mathfrak{D}
.
Let us therefore fix a
\hat{\mu}\in\mathfrak{D}
. Then there exist an
n\geq 1
and

a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|a_{1}|+\dots+|a_{n}|\leq 1
and
\hat{\mu}=a_{1}\delta_{t_{1}}+\dots+a_{n}\delta_{t_{n}}
.
Defining
Y:=(X_{t_{1}},\dots,X_{t_{n}})
, we then find

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mu},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\langle a,Y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

as in (
E.5
). Since
Y
is Gaussian, so is
\langle a,Y\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
,
and thus
\langle\hat{\mu},X\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}
is Gaussian.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a metric space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,
and

(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be a Gaussian process with continuous paths. Then its mean and covariance functions are continuous.

Proof of Lemma
E.3
.

To show the continuity of the mean function, we fix a
t_{\infty}\in T
and a sequence
(t_{n})\subset T
with
t_{n}\to t_{\infty}
. Our assumption then guarantees

X_{t_{n}}(\omega)\to X_{t_{\infty}}(\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
, and therefore also
X_{t_{n}}\to X_{t_{\infty}}
is distribution. Now, Theorem
C.3
gives
\mathbb{E}X_{t_{n}}\to\mathbb{E}X_{t_{\infty}}
.

For the continuity of the covariance function
k
, we again fix a
t_{\infty}\in T
and a sequence
(t_{n})\subset T
with
t_{n}\to t_{\infty}
. As for the mean function,
Theorem
C.3
then shows

\displaystyle k(t_{n},t_{n})=\var X_{t_{n}}\to\var X_{t}=k(t,t)\,.

(E.6)

Let us now fix an
s_{\infty}\in T
and a sequence
(s_{n})\subset T
with
s_{n}\to s_{\infty}
.
We write
Y_{n}:=X_{s_{n}}-\mathbb{E}X_{s_{n}}
and
Z_{n}:=X_{t_{n}}-\mathbb{E}X_{t_{n}}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Then we have

Z_{n}(\omega)-Z_{\infty}(\omega)\to 0
for all
\omega\in\Omega
by our assumption and the already established continuity of the mean function. Moreover, each

Z_{n}-Z_{\infty}
is a centered Gaussian random variable, and therefore another application of Theorem
C.3
shows
\var(Z_{n}-Z_{\infty})\to 0
. The latter implies

\displaystyle|k(s_{\infty},t_{n})-k(s_{\infty},t_{\infty})|=|\mathbb{E}Y_{\infty}Z_{n}-\mathbb{E}Y_{\infty}Z_{\infty}|

\displaystyle\leq(\mathbb{E}Y_{\infty}^{2})^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}(Z_{n}-Z_{\infty})^{2})^{1/2}

\displaystyle\to 0\,.

(E.7)

In combination with (
E.6
) we analogously obtain

\displaystyle|k(s_{n},t_{n})-k(s_{\infty},t_{n})|=|\mathbb{E}Y_{n}Z_{n}-\mathbb{E}Y_{\infty}Z_{n}|\leq(\mathbb{E}(Y_{n}-Y_{\infty})^{2})^{1/2}\cdot(\mathbb{E}Z_{n}^{2})^{1/2}\to 0

(E.8)

Combining (
E.7
) and (
E.8
) with

\displaystyle|k(s_{n},t_{n})-k(s_{\infty},t_{\infty})|\leq|k(s_{n},t_{n})-k(s_{\infty},t_{n})|+|k(s_{\infty},t_{n})-k(s_{\infty},t_{\infty})|

then yields the assertion.
∎

Lemma E.4
.

Let
(T,\metric)
be a compact metric space,
(\Omega,{\cal A},\mathrm{P})
be a probability space,

X:\Omega\to C(T)
be a Gaussian random variable. Moreover, let

(X_{t})_{t\in T}
be the associated Gaussian process given by (
E.2
) and
k
be its covariance function.
Then for all
\mu,\nu\in{\cal M}(T)
we have

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mu},\cov(X)\hat{\nu}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\int_{T}\int_{T}k(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(s)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)\,.

Proof of Lemma
E.4
.

We first note that using Hahn-Jordan decompositions for
\mu,\nu\in{\cal M}(T)
we may assume without loss of generality that
\mu
and
\nu
are finite measures.
Moreover,
from Lemma
E.2
and Lemma
E.3
we know that
k
is continuous, and consequently, we can consider the map
\Phi:T\to C(T)
defined by

\Phi(t):=k(\,\cdot\,,t)
.

Our first goal is to show that
\Phi\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\nu,{C(T)})}
. To this end, we first note that for all
\mu\in{\cal M}(T)

the measurability of

\displaystyle t\mapsto\langle\hat{\mu},\Phi(t)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\int_{T}k(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(s)

follows from measurability of
k
and Fubini-Tonelli’s theorem, see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 14.16]
. Consequently,
\Phi
is weakly measurable, and since
C(T)
is separable,

\Phi
is also strongly measurable. In addition we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in T}\|\Phi(t)\|_{\infty}=\sup_{s,t\in T}|k(s,t)|<\infty

since
k
is continuous and
T\times T
is compact. Combining these considerations yields
\Phi\in{{\cal L}_{1}(\nu,{C(T)})}
.

Now, (
28
) and the symmetry of
k
shows
k(s,\,\cdot\,)=\cov(X)\delta_{s}
for all
s\in T
. Together with (
B.5
)
and the Pettis property (
A.11
) of Bochner integrals
this in turn implies

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{s},\cov(X)\hat{\nu}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\langle\hat{\nu},\cov(X)\delta_{s}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{\nu},k(s,\,\cdot\,)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=\int_{T}k(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\langle\delta_{s},\Phi(t)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)

\displaystyle=\Bigl\langle\delta_{s},\int_{T}\Phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)\Bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

for all
s\in T
. In other words, the functions
\cov(X)\hat{\nu}
and
\int_{T}\Phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)
have the same values for all
s\in T
, and hence we have

\displaystyle\cov(X)\hat{\nu}=\int_{T}\Phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)\,.

This in turn gives

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mu},\cov(X)\hat{\nu}\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}=\Bigl\langle\hat{\mu},\int_{T}\Phi(t)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)\Bigr\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\langle\hat{\mu},k(\,\cdot\,,t)\rangle_{C(T)^{\prime},C(T)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\int_{T}k(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(s)\,\mathrm{d}\nu(t)\,,

where in the second step we again used (
A.11
).
∎
